--- Page 4 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with ritual instructions for worship and the creation of sacred circles (mandalas). This page transitions into a narrative dialogue from the Dharma Purana, featuring Subahu and Dharmaraja (the Lord of Justice), focusing on the individual responsibility of one's actions (karma).</meta>

<margin>Section 5, Chapter 5</margin> ...whatever has been done by a human. **Subahu spoke:** Having heard the words of the <term>Lord of Justice</term> <note>original: "dharmarāja"; another name for Yama, the god of death and justice who judges the dead according to their deeds.</note>, the human was filled with wonder. "I have committed no evil deed for which I should suffer this pain. I have done nothing to harm any living beings, nor have I caused suffering. I have never asked anything of anyone that would lead to such misery. Why then has this suffering come upon me in my place?" **The Lord of Justice spoke:**

"Listen, dear one, and I shall explain the various fruits of <term>actions</term> <note>original: "karma"; the principle of cause and effect where a person's intent and actions influence their future.</note>. These consequences are diverse, O wise one, regarding the human condition. Whatever <term>merit</term> <note>original: "puṇya"; spiritual capital earned through virtuous deeds.</note> or good and bad deeds were previously accumulated, it is from those that humans in the world receive both the auspicious and the inauspicious. 

Tell me, whatever good or bad was done—even if a friend is named, it is only the one who acted who enjoys the fruit of that action. As I proceed with justice, no other can interfere. Know, O human, that your good deeds are exactly as you performed them in the past. This [fate] was obtained by me [as your judge], and by no one else; what was done by me belongs to me, and what was done by you belongs to you. Know, O human, that this is the fruit of being human. 

Just as whatever comes into the hand of a <term>Twice-born</term> <note>original: "dvijāti"; a member of the three higher social classes in ancient India who has undergone a spiritual initiation, or 'second birth.'</note> while a man is asleep continues just the same [upon waking], so too is everything seen exactly as it is. Know, O human, that this is how the fruit of deeds manifests. Whether one is submerged in worldly life or not, they must consume what they have earned. 

The fruits for humans in this world are exactly as they are; I have not obtained this knowledge here alone, but it has been spoken by <term>Chitragupta</term> <note>original: "citragupta"; the celestial scribe and deity tasked with keeping complete records of the good and evil actions of every human being.</note>. Why were you not wise regarding your small sins? I am being asked this in the very presence of the Lord of Justice. This answer was not given by me, but was spoken by Chitragupta." **Thus ends [this section] in the Dharma Purana. 53.**

<summary>This page records a dialogue from the Dharma Purana where the deity Yama explains the inescapable nature of karma to a confused soul named Subahu. It emphasizes that every individual is solely responsible for their own spiritual merits and sins, as recorded by the celestial scribe Chitragupta.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Yama, Dharmaraja, Subahu, Chitragupta, Dharma Purana, Justice, Merit and Sin, Afterlife</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>While the previous page's translation discussed the *Dharma Purana* and the concept of individual karma, this page begins a new text entirely: the *Sukhāvatīvyūha Sūtra*, a fundamental scripture of Pure Land Buddhism. This shift suggests this manuscript is a compendium of various sacred Buddhist and philosophical works bound together.</meta>

->Om! Salutations to the Great Buddha!<-

## Now begins the **Mahāyāna Scripture on the Array of the Land of Bliss**
<note>original: "Sukhāvatīvyūha Mahāyāna Sūtra"; this is a foundational text describing the Western Paradise or "Pure Land" of the Buddha Amitābha.</note>

**Thus have I heard:** At one time, the Blessed One was staying at **Rajgir** <note>original: "Rājagṛha"; the capital of the ancient Magadha kingdom and a frequent location for the Buddha's discourses.</note>, on the **Vulture Peak** mountain, together with a great community of twelve hundred and fifty <term>monks</term> <note>original: "bhikṣu"; ordained male followers who have renounced worldly life to follow the Buddha's path.</note>.

They were all <term>Perfected Saints</term> <note>original: "arhat"; those who have reached the final stage of spiritual evolution and will not be reborn into the cycle of suffering.</note> whose spiritual taints were exhausted, who were free from worldly afflictions, and who had mastered themselves. Their wisdom was fully liberated. They were like "great dragons" <note>original: "mahānāga"; a traditional metaphor for powerful, spiritually advanced beings who are immovable in their realization.</note> who had accomplished what needed to be done, fulfilled their duties, laid down their heavy burdens, and achieved their own goals. 

Having completely destroyed the bonds of rebirth, their minds were entirely freed through perfect knowledge. They had attained the highest perfections in all forms of mastery—with the exception of one single person, namely, the Venerable **Ananda** <note>The Buddha’s cousin and personal attendant; he is often depicted as the "recorder" of the sūtras, having not yet attained full Arhatship during the Buddha's lifetime so that he might remain "human" enough to remember the teachings for others.</note>.

Also present with those great disciples were many **Awakening Beings** <note>original: "bodhisattva"; beings who have generated the "mind of enlightenment" and seek Buddhahood to liberate all sentient beings.</note>, led by **Maitreya** <note>The "Future Buddha" currently residing in the Tuṣita heaven, waiting for the appropriate time to be reborn on Earth.</note>. All of them were destined for enlightenment and possessed unhindered awareness. They were firmly set upon attaining perfect awakening and dwelled in the perfection of all higher knowledge. They had served thousands of Buddhas, their minds were free from attachment to the shifting nature of reality, and their fame was renowned throughout all the worlds. They had served many hundreds of thousands of Buddhas...

<summary>This page marks the grand opening of the Sukhāvatīvyūha Sūtra, setting the scene at Vulture Peak where the Buddha is surrounded by an assembly of over a thousand perfected saints and a host of great Bodhisattvas.</summary>

<keywords>Sukhāvatīvyūha Sūtra, Vulture Peak, Ananda, Maitreya, Arhat, Bodhisattva, Pure Land, Rajgir</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a portion of the *Sukhāvatīvyūha Sūtra*, a Buddhist text. However, this page marks a sharp transition to a Brahmanical or Vaishnava text, likely a Purana or a philosophical treatise on creation. This suggests the manuscript is a composite volume containing works from different traditions. The text on this page shifts to the praise of Vishnu and the Sāṅkhya-influenced cosmology of the "Cosmic Egg."</meta>

->Om! Salutations to the Lord Vasudeva!<-
->Salutations to Krishna!<-

I take refuge in **Vishvaksena** <note>original: "Viṣvaksena"; the commander-in-chief of Lord Vishnu's army, often invoked at the start of rituals to remove obstacles.</note>, whose attendants—led by the **elephant-faced one** <note>original: "dviradavaktra"; referring to Ganesha, the remover of obstacles, who in this Vaishnava context is seen as part of Vishnu's divine retinue.</note> and hundreds of others—destroy all obstacles for those who worship him. (1)

Victory, victory to the Lord of the Universe! You are the auspiciousness of the world’s auspiciousness, the treasury of all sacred scriptures, whose incomparable and great power protects the entire circle of the world. Your two lotus-like feet are truly beautiful.

Salutations to you, O Blessed Lord! This [teaching] is indeed the purifier of the world, the supreme purifier among all things that purify. It is beautiful, the destroyer of all sins, and acts as a swan swimming in the lotus-like minds of the sages.

The Lord is **Purushottama** <note>literally "the Supreme Person," a common title for Vishnu-Narayana.</note> Narayana, who is without beginning and possesses unimpeded, infinite power. That Blessed Lord, through portions of Himself, manifested as the conscious **individual souls** <term>Purushas</term>, **primordial nature** <term>Prakrti</term>, the **great intellect** <term>Mahat</term>, **ego** <term>Ahamkara</term>, and the **five subtle elements** <term>Tanmatra</term> <note>These are the foundational building blocks of reality in Sāṅkhya philosophy, which the text integrates into a divine creation story.</note>.

Through the **gross elements** and the **senses**, He shone forth and produced the **Cosmic Egg** <note>original: "bhuvanāṇḍam" or "Brahmāṇḍa"; the "Egg of Brahma" which contains the entire manifested universe.</note>. This Egg, resembling a mountain of gold, shone in every direction, and within it were situated the **fourteen worlds**. 

The world itself became the form of the Lord. Within that Cosmic Egg, produced from the qualities of the Lord, the one known as **Virinchi** <note>Another name for Brahma, the creator god who functions as an agent of the Supreme Lord Narayana.</note> was born. That Blessed Virinchi is the Great Grandfather of all the worlds.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a Vaishnava cosmological text, transitioning from the previous Buddhist content. It describes the invocation of Vishnu and his attendants, followed by a Sāṅkhya-influenced account of how Narayana manifested the universe and the creator Brahma through the "Cosmic Egg."</summary>
<keywords>Vasudeva, Narayana, Vishvaksena, Sāṅkhya, Prakrti, Brahmanda, Virinchi, creation myth, Vaishnavism</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page marks a significant shift in the manuscript's contents. While the previous page focused on Vaishnava cosmology and the deity Vishnu, this page contains a Jain devotional hymn (stuti). The use of terms like "Vitaraga" (One Beyond Passion) and the mention of "Shri Vimala" (likely Vimalanatha, the 13th Jain Tirthankara) confirms a change in sectarian context, typical of composite manuscripts where multiple texts are bound together.</meta>

### Page 3

Having bowed to the **All-knowing Lord** <note>original: "Sarvajna"; an epithet for an enlightened being who possesses infinite knowledge</note>, who appears as a source of auspiciousness for those who understand their own spiritual welfare; to be praised is the glorious **One Beyond Passion** <note>original: "Vitaraga"; a term for a Tirthankara who has conquered all worldly attachments</note>, who is the refuge for thousands of monks and who stays the cycle of worldly rebirth. To be praised is the **community** <term>Sangha</term> beginning with the teachers [Gurus], which is the seat of all auspiciousness; these are worthy of worship by one who is humble. (64) 

...The beautiful-faced one... that pure one who is constantly worshipped... (65) ...radiant... the terrors of worldly existence... we speak of this illuminator of right knowledge...

...He who has become the end of the terrors of existence, he leads away delusion; he is the sun that liberates one from the enemy of worldly rebirth. May the glorious and virtuous [Sucharita], the pure one, be victorious; he who stays the cycle of existence and is forever praised... the pure one, worthy of praise.

<margin>3</margin>

...the entire circle of the world... in purity... the one called pure, the glorious **Vimala** <note>The text likely refers to Vimalanatha, the 13th Tirthankara, while also punning on the word "vimala" meaning "pure."</note>, who is worthy of praise. (73) This hymn of praise celebrates the glorious and virtuous one, the one of many qualities; may the pure Vimala be victorious; he [the author] performs this hymn of praise. (74)

In him, this great purity exists. Salutations to the pure one of the many worlds. He is a **wish-fulfilling tree** <term>Kalpadruma</term> of virtues. This pure and very clean [teaching] exists for the sake of praise. (75) Salutations to the pure one, always well-praised. Salutations to the one who transcends worldly existence. He performs the praise of Shri Vimala, which is well-conducted and pure. Like a **necklace of pearls** <note>original: "muktāvalī"; a common metaphor for a series of beautiful verses</note>, he is to be praised; he is always worshipped by the hosts of monks. (76)

In whom the pure one is praised... he performs this very pure hymn of praise. (77) Having praised the glorious and pure one, who is always worthy of worship, the mind becomes tranquil and pleased. (78) 

->Composed by the monk **Sucharita Muni**.<- 
->May there be success!<- 
->May this endure as long as the moon and the sun.<-

<summary>This page concludes a devotional hymn (stuti) composed by Sucharita Muni in praise of the Jain Tirthankara Vimalanatha. It emphasizes the "purity" of the enlightened mind and the role of the teacher and community in spiritual liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Jainism, Vimalanatha, Vitaraga, Sucharita Muni, Stuti, Tirthankara, Sangha, Purity</keywords>

It also refers to a lineage of physicians, suggesting this part of the manuscript may contain medical or ritual-healing instructions integrated with Buddhist philosophy.</meta>

**Om!** Salutations to the holy **Ganesha**. **Om!** Salutations to **Sarasvati** <note>The goddess of knowledge, music, and the arts.</note>. **Om!** Salutations to the holy **Gurus**. **Om!** Salutations to the **Blessed Lord Vasudeva** <note>A name for Krishna or Vishnu, signifying the "indwelling Lord."</note>. **Om!** Salutations to **Vishnu**. **Om!** Salutations to the **Great Ganapati**.

#### Now begins the Twilight Worship <term>Sandhyaupasanam</term>
<note>original: "Sandhyaupasanam"; a mandatory daily ritual performed by practitioners at the "junctions" of the day (dawn, noon, and dusk) involving prayers, mantras, and water offerings.</note>

To the one by whose mere remembrance one is released from the bonds of the cycle of birth and worldly existence—salutations to that **Vishnu**, the all-powerful Lord.

Salutations to the vessel of all blessings; I take refuge in that **Hari** <note>A title for Vishnu, meaning "the one who removes [sins/sorrow]."</note>, the eternal, unborn Supreme Person.

**Om!** Salutations to the Blessed Lord **Vasudeva**. **Om Tat Sat** <note>original: "Om Tat Sat"; a sacred phrase from the Bhagavad Gita signifying that "The Absolute Truth is the only reality."</note>.

We meditate upon **Narayana**; we contemplate **Vasudeva**; may that **Vishnu** inspire us. [1]

**Om!** Salutations to the Infallible One <term>Achyuta</term>.
**Om!** Salutations to the Infinite One <term>Ananta</term>.
**Om!** Salutations to the Guardian of the Earth <term>Govinda</term>.
Having prayed with these three mantras... <note>This refers to the "Achamana," the ritual sipping of water to purify the body and mind before prayer.</note>

**Om!** The Waters, the Light, the Essence, the Nectar, the Absolute, the Earth, the Atmosphere, and the Heavens—**Om**. [2]

[The practitioner] bows to the **Gayatri** <note>A highly revered mantra from the Rig Veda, personified here as a goddess.</note>:
**Om!** Salutations to the Goddess of Twilight.
**Om!** Salutations to **Savitri** <note>The solar deity associated with the morning sun.</note>.
**Om!** Salutations to **Gayatri**.
Salutations to the Goddess of the Twilight ritual.

The One with the Beautiful Face, the One-Tusked, the Tawny-Colored, the Elephant-Eared, the Large-Bellied, the Fearsome, the Destroyer of Obstacles, the Leader... [3]

The Smoke-Hued, the Chief of the Attendants, the Moon-Browed, the Elephant-Faced—whoever recites or even hears these twelve names... [4]

...during the beginning of studies, at a wedding, when entering or departing, in battle or in crisis, no obstacle shall arise for them. [5]

<summary>This page initiates the Sandhya (twilight worship) ritual, beginning with invocations to Ganesha, Vishnu, and the Gayatri mantra, followed by the twelve protective names of Ganesha.</summary>
<keywords>Sandhya ritual, Vishnu, Ganesha, Gayatri mantra, Achamana, twilight worship, Hindu liturgy</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page marks a transition from the previous page's focus on the Ten Great Wisdom Goddesses (Dasa Mahavidyas) and their specific mantras. It shifts to a Puranic-style dialogue between the gods Vishnu and Shiva (referred to here as Ishwara). The subject moves from the forms of the Goddess to the divine origin and significance of Shiva’s matted hair (jata), which is a central iconographic and ascetic feature in Shaivism.</meta>

**Om! Salutations to Shiva.** **Vishnu said:**

"How did the Blessed Lord Shambhu's <note>original: "Shambhu"; a name for Shiva meaning 'Source of Happiness'.</note> crown of matted hair <term>jata-makuta</term> <note>original: "jata-makuta-manditam"; refers to the hair of an ascetic piled up to resemble a crown, symbolizing both renunciation and sovereignty.</note> come to be? How was it born from the Great Soul as something divine and encompassing all the gods? I wish to hear this in detail from you, O Lord who has the bull as his banner <note>original: "vrshabhadhvaja"; an epithet of Shiva whose mount and banner is the bull Nandi.</note>."

**The Lord <note>Shiva</note> said:**

"Listen, O you who are worshipped by the immortals, I shall tell you the greatness of the matted locks. I speak of that which is without beginning, middle, or end, O Lord of the Gods. In former times, the most fortunate ones were detached from the world, appearing just like Shivas. In a previous age <term>Purakalpa</term> <note>original: "purakalpe"; refers to a vast period of cosmic time in the distant past.</note>, O beautiful one <note>original: "vararohe"; literally 'one with beautiful hips,' usually a feminine address, here likely used as a poetic honorific or reflecting a source text where the Goddess was the interlocutor.</note>, they stayed in my presence. Having served them, I remained in perfect meditative concentration. The characteristics of the matted locks of those who are...

<column-break/>

...endowed with devotion to me—listen, I shall describe the greatness of those matted locks worshipped by the immortals. It is not possible to fully describe them, my child, even in hundreds of millions of eons. I am the God who wears matted hair as a garment <note>original: "jatambhara-dharam"; suggesting the hair is so long or significant it serves as a covering.</note> and is adorned with a mass of matted hair. Nevertheless, out of a spirit of devotion, I shall tell you, O Keshava <note>original: "keshava"; an epithet of Vishnu, often interpreted as 'the one with beautiful hair,' providing a wordplay against Shiva's matted hair.</note>.

In a former age, those ascetic sages were created by me. Adorned with matted hair, they were steady and unwavering by my grace. Now listen to the characteristics arising from the circle of matted locks. I shall tell you which matted locks are the principal ones, O you of good vows. I shall speak of the Great-Mantra-Matted-Hair <note>original: "mahamantra-jatam"; implying a spiritual connection between the physical hair and the power of sacred chants.</note> for the welfare of mankind."

**Om! Salutations to Shiva.**

<meta>This page continues the manuscript's transition from ritual invocations to a deeply philosophical discussion on the nature of language and reality. # The Explanation of Manifestation and Concealment

<unclear>Now</unclear>, we shall explain the designation of "Manifestation" in its proper sequence. The term "Manifestation" is, in truth, the same as the term "Concealment." Or rather, one may say that "Concealment" is "Manifestation," based on their deeper etymological derivation <term>vyutpatti</term> <note>original: "vyutpattyā"; the linguistic analysis of how a word's meaning is derived from its roots.</note>.

<unclear>Specifically</unclear>, <unclear>indeed</unclear>, the designation of "Manifestation" refers to **contraction**, while the designation of "Concealment" refers to **expansion**. Between these two, he first describes **contraction** [using the term] "Manifestation."

If one were to ask: "But how can 'Concealment' be called 'Manifestation'?" 

Here it is said in response: <unclear>Just as</unclear> the initial "intention to speak" <term>vivaksha</term> <note>original: "vivakṣā"; the desire or impulse of consciousness to express itself or manifest into form.</note> is expressed through <note>string hole</note> concealment. This is the technical convention <term>sanketa</term> <note>original: "saṅketitaṃ"; a specialized or symbolic meaning agreed upon within a specific tradition, often different from common usage.</note> used here. 

If one then asks: "Since the designation 'Manifestation' is defined as contraction, how can **expansion** be the intended meaning here?" 

<unclear>It is said</unclear> in reply: because **expansion** exists even within **contraction**. Just as when someone <note>string hole</note> performs an action, the expansion [of that act] arises only when there is a prior contraction; this is the underlying intention. 

<unclear>And so</unclear> it has been said: "In that which is contracted, there is expansion." In this way, even though "Manifestation" is defined as **contraction**, there is no contradiction because its ultimate purpose is **expansion**.

<unclear>Having</unclear> thus explained the designation of "Manifestation," he begins to explain "Concealment," saying: "And the designation of Concealment..." In that context, the term "Concealment" refers to **expansion**. 

<unclear>Just as</unclear> "Manifestation" is called contraction, so "Concealment" is called expansion. When this technical convention is understood, there is no <unclear>contradiction</unclear> between "Manifestation" and "Concealment."

<summary>This page discusses the paradoxical relationship between manifestation (vivṛti) and concealment (saṃvṛti), defining them through the philosophical concepts of contraction (saṅkoca) and expansion (vikāsa). It argues that these opposing forces are interdependent, as the expansion of consciousness or speech requires an initial contraction.</summary>

<keywords>manifestation, concealment, contraction, expansion, vivaksa, Sanskrit philosophy, linguistics, Kashmir Shaivism, paradox of expression</keywords>

# The Ritual Invocation of the Lord Vishnu

**Om! May there be well-being!**

**Auspicious is the Lord Vishnu; auspicious is the one whose banner is the eagle Garuda <note>The celestial bird who serves as Vishnu's mount.</note>. Auspicious is the lotus-eyed one; Hari is the very abode of auspiciousness.**

Salutations to the illustrious Ganesha <note>The elephant-headed deity of beginnings, traditionally invoked at the start of any rite to remove obstacles.</note>. 

Now, the mantra: **Om, salutations to Narayana.** <note>Narayana is a primary name for Vishnu, often interpreted as "the one who dwells in the waters of creation" or "the refuge of all humanity."</note>

## The Ritual of Placement

One should perform the ritual placement <term>nyasa</term> <note>original: "nyaset"; a practice of touching specific parts of the body while reciting mantras to "seat" or "install" divine energy within the physical frame.</note> as follows:

At the navel center; in the heart; on the head; at the crown of the head; for the armor; for the three eyes; and for the weapon.

Now, the mantra: **Om, salutations to the Lord Vasudeva.**

Salutations to the two thumbs. Salutations to the two index fingers. Salutations to the two middle fingers. Salutations to the two ring fingers. Salutations to the two little fingers. Salutations to the palms and the backs of the hands. 

Having thus performed the ritual placement beginning with the heart and other limbs...

## The Meditation

**The Meditation** <term>dhyāna</term>:
One should always meditate upon the Lord Narayana, who resides in the center of the solar orb. He is seated upon a lotus throne, adorned with armlets, shark-shaped earrings <note>original: "makara-kuṇḍala"; the Makara is a mythical sea-creature representing the life-force of the waters.</note>, and a crown. His body is golden and enchanting, and he holds the sacred conch and the discus.

Having meditated thus, one should perform mental worship.

## The Sacred Prayer

**We recognize Narayana; we meditate upon Vasudeva. May that Vishnu inspire us.** <note>original: "Nārāyaṇāya vidmahe vāsudevāya dhīmahi tanno viṣṇuḥ pracodayāt"; this is the Vishnu-Gayatri, a sacred verse intended to attune the mind to divine light.</note>

One should chant this mantra twenty-eight times.

### The Procedure
One should chant according to one's capacity. Through this chanting, may the illustrious Vishnu be pleased.

**Om, That is the Truth.** <note>original: "Om Tat Sat"; a standard Vedic formula used to seal a ritual, affirming that the act is dedicated to the ultimate reality.</note>

**May this be an offering to the illustrious Krishna.**

<keywords>Vishnu, Narayana, Nyasa, Dhyana, Gayatri mantra, ritual practice, solar meditation, Krishna, spiritual purification</keywords>

# The Excellence of Divine Knowledge

...residing in the center of the orb of the sun. Salutations to the First of the Perfected Ones <note>original: "Siddhadya"; refers to the primordial enlightened beings or the first of the great teachers.</note> and the supports of the earth. 

May this praise of his glory, composed in pure and stainless letters, conquer the earth with its flourishing form. Witnessed by the wise, possessing dear and auspicious marks, may it lead the practitioner to the presence of good deeds and the immortal realm. **[71]**

This Knowledge, of great majesty and praised by the wise, is the primary cause for the destruction of the fear of mundane existence <term>bhava-bhaya</term> <note>The cyclic fear of birth, death, and rebirth in the material world.</note> for all embodied beings. It has been expanded here by one of noble intellect. May the Lord of the World, who is the vessel of all well-being, be pleased. **[72]**

Even without the sun, this well-ordered and praised Knowledge produces light night after night, without ceasing. She is the beloved companion of those with a refined and steady intellect. **[73]**

Through the grace of the Guru of great souls, the Divine Intelligence <note>original: "Mati"; here personified as a goddess or a guiding spiritual force.</note>, praised by the wise, reigns supreme. Victory to her, the remover of the fear of existence and the upholder of the entire world! **[74]**

Following the path of the wise, she never falters. She is the means to achieve merit and great fame. This Great Knowledge is the very ornament of prosperity and grants the blessing of true renown. **[75]**

Hearing the Sacred Law <term>Dharma</term> grants a path to the higher realms; she is that well-ordered beloved who provides the way. **[76]**

Blessed with a multitude of virtues is that wise person in this world whose intellect is well-disposed and properly aligned. To those who engage in sacred dialogue, she brings constant welfare and enduring glory. **[77]**

Composed of the Sacred Law, pure and refined, she grants the immortal state. The wise man, having followed this stainless intellect through the grace of the Great Souls and the gift of Knowledge, finds his way to eternal happiness. **[78]**

With the **dawning of awakening** <note>original: "prabodhodaya"; the moment of spiritual realization or enlightenment.</note>, welfare and auspiciousness are established forever. **[79]**

These commentaries and modes of practice are thus properly arranged.

# Page 1

Om, salutations to the Great God <note>original: "Mahādevāya"; a title for Shiva.</note>. Well-being; salutations to the holy Teachers. Om, salutations to the Blessed Lord Vasudeva <note>original: "Bhagavate Vāsudevāya"; a primary name for Vishnu.</note>. By the mere remembrance of Vishnu, all sins are destroyed. Therefore, with every effort, following the path of the Great God, this has been told by me in detail.

Listen with a focused mind. Regarding the Supreme Self, who is unchanging:

Whom Brahma, Varuna, Indra, Rudra, and the Maruts <note>A group of storm deities in Vedic mythology.</note> praise with divine hymns; whom the singers of the Sama Veda <note>one of the four Vedas, focused on melodies and chants.</note> sing of through the Vedas, along with their auxiliary branches <note>term: "Aṅga"; the six traditional disciplines like grammar and astronomy needed to understand the Vedas.</note>, the specific chanting methods <note>term: "Pada-krama"; technical styles of Vedic recitation that preserve the text's integrity.</note>, and the Upanishads; whom the Yogis see with a mind fixed in meditation; and whose limit is not known by the hosts of gods or demons—to that Deity, salutations. || 1 ||

In this very context, they cite this ancient history, the dialogue between Sudarshana and Narada, O descendant of Bharata. || 2 ||

**Narada said:**
"Oh, how wonderful this is, O King, what has been spoken by the Blessed Lord. Who is the most praiseworthy of all the worlds? Or what learned priest <note>original: "Vipra"; a member of the Brahmin caste.</note> has conquered his senses? || 3 || Whose mind is truly tranquil? Who contemplates liberation <note>term: "Mokṣa"; the release from the cycle of rebirth.</note>? Who has constant devotion to Vishnu? Tell me that, O Great Sage." || 4 ||

**Sudarshana said:**
"Regarding this, I shall tell you; listen to me as I speak. He who is the source of the entire universe is sung of as Vishnu. || 5 || In Him this entire universe is woven and interconnected <note>The Sanskrit "protam" implies the way threads are woven together on a loom; the universe is the fabric and God is the thread.</note>. There is nothing higher than Him, for nothing exists apart from Supreme Consciousness. || 6 || Without His manifest form, O King, nothing indeed functions. He alone is the essence of all beings; no other can be conceived. || 7 || When the world is dissolved into Him, what remains, O best of the wise? Just as the sun, standing in the midst of clouds, surely destroys the thick shadow, so the knowledge of the dispassionate one dissolves into the Self alone. || 8 || Just as a peacock dances by its own nature <note>A metaphor suggesting that the divine or the enlightened soul acts out of its inherent nature without external compulsion.</note>, of this there is no doubt." || 9 ||

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative shift from general praise to a specific Purāṇic dialogue. While the previous page featured Narada and King Sudarshana, this folio introduces a new dialogue within the Varaha Purana between the sage Shweta and the serpent-deity Shankhapala. It establishes the setting at the sacred Badarikashrama in the Himalayas and pivots to the specific virtues of the Purushottama region (modern-day Puri, Odisha).</meta>

<margin>3</margin>

Om, salutations to the White Boar <note>original: "Śvetavarāhāya"; refers to the Varaha or Boar incarnation of Vishnu, for whom this Purana is named.</note>.

Now, there is a mountain named Himavan <note>The Himalayas.</note>, which consists of all manner of jewels and is served by all the gods and sages. On its side lies the beautiful and auspicious Badarikashrama <note>The famous hermitage of Badarinath, a site of deep meditation.</note>. In that place resided a certain best of sages, a great sage named Shweta. At his side lived a Great Naga <note>term: "Nāga"; a semi-divine race of serpent-beings in Hindu mythology, often associated with water and guardianship.</note> named Shankhapala, who possessed great splendor.

Regarding this Shankhapala: he was entirely freed from all sins and was a supreme devotee of the Blessed Lord. That Naga was a virtuous sage at all times and for all eternity. He was wise and high-minded. At that place, a conversation occurred between him and the sage Shweta.

**Then the sage Shweta said to the Naga:**
"O best of Nagas, O highly fortunate one, please tell me what I ask: How does liberation truly occur from the ocean of worldly existence <note>term: "Saṃsāra"; the cycle of birth, death, and rebirth often compared to a turbulent ocean.</note>? How many holy places <note>term: "Tīrtha"; literally a "ford" or crossing place; a pilgrimage site where the boundary between the human and divine is thin.</note> are there in this world that grant the fruits of worldly enjoyment and final liberation? Tell me, O wise one, what specific fruit is assigned to each place?"

**The Naga spoke:**
"Excellent, excellent, O fortunate one, O best of sages and wise one! I shall tell you the secret that grants release from all sins. I have been asked by you, O wise one, about that which consists of all holy places, the Supreme, the Supreme Lord—a secret more hidden than any secret, and full of merit. It grants both worldly enjoyment and liberation; it is divine and bestows all happiness. It is not possible for mere humans to know, and it is very difficult even for the gods to attain.

Listen to that, O fortunate one; I shall tell it to you exactly as it is. I shall speak its name, which destroys all sins. That name is **Purushottama** <note>original: "Purushottama"; literally 'The Supreme Person.' While a name for Vishnu, here it specifically refers to the sacred region of Puri, Odisha, and the deity Jagannath.</note>, who is the eternal Great Vishnu himself. He is the eternal Blessed Lord, the self-born Master of the Universe. He alone is the Cause and the Creator; he is the Supreme Person.

Without Vishnu, there is indeed no liberation; without Vishnu, there is no happiness. Without Vishnu, there is no true holy place; without Vishnu, there is no good company. Therefore, with every effort, one should perform the worship of Vishnu. Through the union with Purushottama, one is released from all sins."

Thus ends [a portion of] the "Greatness of the Sacred Region of Purushottama" in the glorious Varaha Purana.

<meta>This page transitions from the general technical and auspicious invocations found on the previous page to a specific instructional discourse on the month of Vaishakha. While the previous page focused on universal stability and the divine architect Vishvakarma, this page begins a "Mahatmya" or a text glorifying the spiritual merits of a specific time and ritual practice.</meta>

I shall speak a little. This sixth month is called Vaishakha. The opinion of the wise sages regarding this has been stated previously. The learned say that even at the beginning, two lunar mansions <note>original: "rkshau" (nakshatras); stars or constellations used in the lunar calendar to determine auspicious timing.</note> are proclaimed. The first quarters [of these stars] are present when Vaishakha is joined with the Magha constellation. The New Moon <note>original: "Ama" (Amavasya)</note> is declared by the ancient texts <note>original: "Puranatah"; referring to the Puranas, a genre of ancient Indian literature consisting of history, mythology, and philosophy.</note> to be for the sake of ancestral rites. Having taught the rules <note>original: "niyamas"; prescribed religious observances or self-restraints.</note> of Vaishakha, O Great Sages, one who hears all those rules is liberated from all sins. Having bathed here, the gods emerged from sin at the holy pilgrimage sites. 

<margin>50</margin>

### The Greatness of the Month of Vaishakha

->Beginning<-

Just as the merit of bathing and other acts is obtained by the Vaishakha bath, a person obtains that same fruit through penance, celibacy, rules of conduct, and self-restraint. Through all pure gifts, a person attains that result. The sage Vidyadhara spoke to the Great-souled Kashyapa: "Just as He [Vishnu] is the favorite among all gods and holy places, so this month of Vaishakha is always pleasing to Vishnu. There is no pilgrimage, no knowledge, no penance, and no vow greater than this. Three should always be meditated upon: the Vishnu-forms who are affectionate to their devotees. For their pleasure, one should give an offering of water <note>original: "arghya"; a ritual offering of water, often mixed with flowers or grain, poured from a vessel.</note> to the Son of the Sun <note>original: "Sauraye"; a title for Yama, the god of justice and death, who is the son of the Sun-god Surya.</note>. Bathing here, a man goes to the supreme, imperishable state." 

->The first chapter of the Greatness of Vaishakha is complete.<-

We recognize the Son of the Sun; we meditate upon the one who holds the noose in his hand. May that God of Justice <note>original: "Dharma"; here referring to Dharmaraja or Yama.</note> inspire us. Then, he who bathes according to the tradition shall go to the supreme goal. Whoever gives to the Son of the Sun <note>original: "Suryaputra"</note>, having offered to the Son of the Sun and worshiped him according to the rules, even a slayer of a Brahmin <note>Considered one of the most serious sins in ancient Indian law.</note> is liberated if he bathes in Vaishakha according to the tradition. From bathing alone, a man becomes liberated from all sins.

<column-break/>

Therefore, with all effort, one should perform the ritual bath in Vaishakha. Just as the Tulsi <note>Holy Basil, a plant sacred to the god Vishnu.</note> is the greatest among all flowers, and just as Vishnu—the Lord of All and the Remover of Sins <note>original: "Hari"</note>—is the greatest among all gods, so Vaishakha is called the best among months for the leaders of sages. The merit earned in Vaishakha is said by the wise to be inexhaustible <note>original: "Akshaya"; that which does not decay, providing eternal spiritual results.</note>. Whatever deed they perform, whether small or great, all that should be known as inexhaustible and always pleasing to Vishnu. 

He who gives rice mixed with curd <note>original: "dadhiyuktānnaṃ"; a traditional cooling food offering.</note> according to the rules for the pleasure of Vishnu, being liberated from all sins, goes to the supreme state. Whoever, being high-minded, gives gold or silver according to their ability, goes to the world of Vishnu; I speak the truth, I speak the truth. Passing through the month of Chaitra, one should perform the ritual bath in Vaishakha. Whichever man, waking up in the early morning, bathes in Vaishakha, he goes to the supreme place where the Lord Janardana <note>A name for Vishnu meaning 'He who stirs the people' or 'the Protector of humanity'.</note> resides. 

On the fourth day of the bright half <note>original: "Shukla Paksha"; the waxing phase of the moon.</note> of Vaishakha, O best of sons, whoever worships Vishnu with abundant and pure offerings, he becomes most dear to Vishnu; I speak the truth, I speak the truth. Especially on the eleventh day <note>original: "Ekadashi"; a recurring lunar day considered the most spiritually potent for fasting and devotion to Vishnu.</note>, whichever man bathes, being liberated from all sins, he shall attain the world of Vishnu. He who gives milk to a Brahmin <note>A member of the priestly and scholarly class.</note> in the pure waters of Vaishakha, he shall always drink milk in the heavenly world, without a doubt. Whoever desires the supreme place should perform the ritual bath in Vaishakha.

Om, salutations to the Goddess of Prosperity <note>original: "sriye" (Shri); referring to Lakshmi or the divine energy of abundance.</note>.

Having bowed to the Lord of the Attendants <note>original: "Gaṇādhyakṣa"; a title for Ganesha as the leader of Shiva's semi-divine cohorts.</note>, the Supreme Teacher and bestower of boons, I shall here speak of the excellent knowledge <note>original: "suvidyā"; literally 'good knowledge' or 'right learning'.</note> that grants eternal success. I shall describe the brief and meritorious praise that bestows the accomplishment of knowledge <note>term: Vidhyāsiddhi (the successful mastery of science, art, or spiritual knowledge)</note>. Having performed the ritual bath according to the rules and having washed the deity with water, one should then worship with effort using offerings of perfume, flowers, and other traditional items. ->Ha Ha Hum Hum<-

Salutations, salutations to the Lord of the Attendants! <note>original: "Gaṇādhipataye"; another title for Ganesha.</note> So be it! <note>original: "Svāhā"; a ritual exclamation used when making offerings into a fire or concluding a mantra.</note> So be it! Salutations to the Pure One.

Grant me success in knowledge always. Bestow the gift of knowledge upon me. Through this singular knowledge, O Imperishable One, be pleased with me.

I bow to the Leader of the Ganas, the remover of all obstacles, who has a curved trunk, the luster of the sun, and a great belly <note>original: "mahākukṣi"; a reference to Ganesha’s iconic physical form representing the containment of the universe.</note>. If one does not worship the Lord of the Ganas for the sake of success in all undertakings, then that person's work shall never succeed anywhere.

Without praising him, the gift of wisdom is not given, and the work remains in its place <note>unclear: "vidā radā dattā svasthānāya"; likely suggesting the work remains stagnant or the benefits return to the source.</note>. 

I bow to the Lord of the Attendants—the Beautiful-faced, the One-tusked, the Short-statured, and the Elephant-eared—who bestows the accomplishment of all tasks.

A King, addicted to the hunt <note>original: "mṛgayā-vyasanī"; someone for whom hunting is a consuming passion or vice.</note>, went to the beautiful great forest. Entering that terrible wilderness, which was crowded with tigers, lions, and other beasts, that Royal Sage <term>Rājarṣi (a king who has attained the status of a saint or seer through his deeds and power)</term> arrived, sword in hand and bearing his bow. With his arrows, he pierced all the deer and other game here and there.

Lions, tigers, elephants, and their kind—seeing him, the lions, tigers, and elephants were all terrified. All the forest-dwellers fled in every direction, struck with fear. There were many deer there, and also lions and other such wild beasts.

Sword-bearers <note>likely referring to the king's retinue or the king himself</note> stood here and there among those who range the forest. Together with the tigers, the deer stood still; and the lions stood together with the elephants. How could this be? By the power of the Royal Sage, they stood together like brothers! Those forest-dwelling animals did not harm one another.

Even though living side by side, no enmity arose among them due to the power of that King. To the beings staying in the forest, he was indeed a source of awe. That Royal Sage of great splendor, having entered the forest in that manner—

Like lightning accompanied by clouds in every direction, that Royal Sage of great splendor, having entered the forest in that manner, made the twang of his bow <note>original: "dhanuṣ-ṭaṅkāram"; the resonant, metallic sound of a bowstring being released.</note> resound through all the quarters of the world. Making the twang of his bow resound through all the quarters—

Hearing the sound of his voice, the sky-dwellers <note>original: "khecarāḥ"; can refer to birds, but often implies celestial beings or spirits flying in the air.</note> living in the forest also cried out "Ha! Ha!" in distress. Even the mountains and the lords of mountains <note>original: "nagesvarāḥ"; great peaks or mountain spirits.</note> trembled. Making the twang of his bow resound through all the quarters—

To the beings staying in the forest, he was indeed a source of awe. Hearing the sound of his voice, the sky-dwellers living in the forest also cried out "Ha! Ha!" in distress. Even the mountains and the lords of mountains trembled. And that King of great splendor was highly skilled in all weapons and missiles <term>śastra-astra (śastra refers to handheld weapons like swords; astra refers to weapons released or thrown, often with a mantra)</term>.

### Now begins the Greatness of the Holy Bhagavatam

**Suta** <note>the traditional storyteller and narrator of the Purāṇas</note> said:

I bow to that sage <note>Shuka, the son of Vyasa</note>, the heart of all beings. When he left home to wander as a renunciant—even before his initiation and before he had performed any worldly duties—his father **Dvaipayana** <term>Dvaipāyana (another name for the sage Vyāsa, meaning "born on an island")</term>, distressed by the coming separation, called out "O my son!" At that moment, because the sage was so absorbed in the essence of all things, the trees responded on his behalf. (4)

In **Naimisha**, the field of the Unblinking Lord <term>Naimiṣa-kṣetra (a sacred forest where the "blinking" of an eye does not occur, symbolizing the eternal presence of the Divine)</term>, the sages led by **Shaunaka** performed a sacrificial session lasting a thousand years for the sake of attaining the heavenly world. (5)

Once, those sages, in the morning...

<unclear>Sam...</unclear> It was indeed of **Prajapati** <term>Prajāpati (the "Lord of Creatures," the Vedic creator deity who brings forth the universe through speech and sacrifice)</term>. When he spoke, the creatures [turned toward him]. Therefore, even now, creatures remain dependent upon Prajapati. She <note>Speech, personified as the goddess Vāk</note> said, "I am his very instruction; for the sake of that instruction and for the sake of desire—the purpose for which I have been sent forth." He said:

"She belongs both to you and to me; she belongs to both of us." Then, she departed. She stayed away for twenty-one years, even while being worshipped. It is said, "This is she who departed." She returned after a year. She spoke, and the Gods heard her.

**Speech** <note>original: "Vāk"; the divine personification of the word, sound, and communication</note> belongs to Prajapati. Because she was the solicitor to Prajapati, he invited her. She said, "What shall be mine?" He replied, "All your desires shall be within us." She was the solicitor to the Gods; the Gods invited her.

The Gods said, "She desired me, she came to me, she stayed with me." Prajapati said, "She came to me, she stayed with me." They held a debate. Both the Gods and Prajapati

approached a wise sage <note>original: "Vipra"; a learned person or priest</note> with their question. He said, "Just as one might cleanse something with fire according to the Vedas, so indeed she was within me. Just as one might cleanse something with fire according to the Vedas, so indeed she was within me." Whatever the Gods and Prajapati said, that [was not the whole truth].

Speech is the essence of **Brahman** <term>Brahman (here referring to the sacred word of the Veda and the ultimate reality)</term>; for Speech is indeed Brahman. All other beings subsist upon Speech. Now, this Speech provides the essence to Brahman. Just as one might milk this essence and drink it, so too is this...

<summary>This page recounts a Vedic myth regarding the ownership and essence of Speech (Vāk). A dispute arises between the creator Prajapati and the Gods over who "possesses" Speech, eventually concluding that Speech is the very essence of Brahman and the foundation of all life.</summary>

<meta>This page contains the "Magha Mahatmya" (The Greatness of the Month of Magha), which appears to be a separate devotional text. ### **Now the Greatness of Magha is written. The venerable Markandeya said:** 

Understand correctly the greatness of the month of Magha <note>original: "Māgha"; the eleventh month of the Hindu calendar, falling in January–February, considered highly auspicious for water rituals</note>. Listen, O seeker of firm vows, to this very secret teaching as it was spoken. Humans in all stages of life praise the month of Magha.

A person goes to **Vaikuntha** <term>Vaikuṇṭha (the supreme celestial abode of the god Vishnu, characterized by eternal bliss)</term> by bathing in Magha with self-restraint. He is released from all sins, even those accumulated over hundreds of millions of ages. Just as the Universal Soul is supreme among the gods, and the cow is supreme among all animals, so the merit of daily charity is most rare during the month of Magha.

Those excellent men who hear the greatness of the month of Magha also reach that supreme place where the Lord **Janardana** <note>original: "Janārdana"; an epithet of the god Vishnu, meaning "the one who is worshiped by people" or "the protector of humanity"</note> resides. For a person who has performed a ritual immersion at **Prayaga** <term>Prayāga (the holy "Place of Sacrifice," modern-day Allahabad, where the Ganges and Yamuna rivers meet)</term> in the month of Magha, there is no more rebirth, even after hundreds of millions of ages.

There is special merit in the month of Magha, particularly in the **Yamuna** river. One who has bathed in the month of Magha is liberated from all sins. When the sun has entered the sign of **Capricorn** <term>Makara (the zodiac sign of the Crocodile, or Capricorn; the sun's entry into this sign marks the winter solstice and the start of the auspicious half of the year)</term> in the month of Magha, the spiritual fruit is ten times greater than the merit gained at holy places like **Kurukshetra** <term>Kurukṣetra (a famous sacred plain and site of pilgrimage, renowned as a "field of righteousness")</term>.

At Prayaga, during the month of Magha when the sun resides in Capricorn, a man who bathes there goes to the sorrowless world of **Vishnu**. Whatever fruit a mortal obtains by being self-restrained in the month of Magha, he would only otherwise attain by the gift of ten million cows.

On a Sunday in the month of Magha, when the sun is in Capricorn, a person is liberated from all sins merely by bathing in the **Ganges**. **Thus ends the glorious Greatness of Magha. 1.** May there be auspiciousness.

<summary>This page outlines the spiritual merits of the month of Magha, emphasizing that ritual bathing in sacred rivers like the Ganges and Yamuna—especially when the sun enters Capricorn—can liberate a soul from millions of years of sin.</summary>
<keywords>Magha Mahatmya, Markandeya, Vaikuntha, Prayaga, Yamuna, Makara, Kurukshetra, Vishnu, Ganges, ritual bathing</keywords>

**Om! Salutations to the Blessed Lord Vasudeva.** <note>original: "oṃ namo bhagavate vāsudevāya"; a principal mantra of Vaishnavism honoring Vishnu/Krishna</note> **He said:**

Salutations, O auspicious **Tulasi** <term>Tulasī (the Sacred Basil plant, personified as a goddess and considered the most beloved devotee of Vishnu)</term>, salutations, O beloved of Vishnu, the bringer of good fortune.

The **Mandara** <note>original: "mandāraḥ"; the coral tree</note>, the **Parijata** <note>original: "pārijāta"; the celestial night-flowering jasmine</note>, the **Kadamba**, and the **Karaviraka** <note>original: "karavīrakaḥ"; the oleander</note>; the **Bakula**, the **Tilaka**, the **Maruva** <note>original: "maruvā"; sweet marjoram</note>, and the **Damanaka** <note>original: "damanaka"; artemisia</note>. || 3 ||

One should meditate on the divine **Narayana** <note>original: "nārāyaṇa"; an epithet of Vishnu as the supreme being</note>, the auspicious one situated in the center of the Discus. He holds the conch, the discus, and the mace in his hands, and is the Lord who wears yellow garments. || 5 ||

He is adorned with a crown, armlets, bracelets, necklaces, and the **Kaustubha** <term>Kaustubha (a divine jewel that emerged during the churning of the ocean, worn by Vishnu on his chest)</term> gem. He is beautiful with his pair of earrings and bears the **Srivatsa** <term>Śrīvatsa (an auspicious mark or curl of hair on the chest of Vishnu, symbolizing his consort, the goddess Lakshmi)</term> mark. || 7 ||

He shines with the radiance of great light, possessing the brilliance of the sun, the moon, and fire. He is surrounded by all weapons, himself being the supreme, auspicious bearer of arms. || 9 ||

<column-break/>

Salutations, O Goddess who grants liberation! Salutations, O auspicious one who grants prosperity! || 1 ||

With **Ketaki**, **Champaka**, **Jati** <note>original: "jātī"; jasmine</note>, **Punnaga**, or **Yuthika**; with **Mallika**, **Malati**, and **Patala** <note>original: "pāṭalī"; the trumpet flower</note> flowers offered with joined palms— || 2 ||

With these flowers, I shall offer worship to Vishnu, the Sage of the Gods. Salutations to Narayana, **Svaha!** <note>original: "svāhā"; an exclamation used when making an offering</note> Salutations to the Blessed Lord Vasudeva! || 4 ||

His eyes are like the petals of a full-blown lotus, his neck is beautiful like a conch-shell, and he is finely adorned. A beautiful **Tilaka** <note>original: "tilakaḥ"; a sacred mark made on the forehead</note> mark is on the forehead of Vishnu, who wears the Kaustubha ornament. || 6 ||

Adorned with the **Vanamala** <term>Vanamālā (the "forest garland" of wildflowers worn by Vishnu, reaching down to his knees)</term> and the flowers of the Wish-fulfilling Tree; the Lord has four arms, a cheerful face, and wears yellow clothes. One should meditate thus and then, with a focused mind, chant the mantra. || 8 ||

O Great **Sudarshana** <note>original: "sudarśana"; literally "the one of auspicious vision," the name of Vishnu's celestial discus</note> Discus, who is illuminated by all mantras! O quieter of all the wicked, O crusher of all [malevolent] beings! 

**The Holy Vishnu said:**

O Great Sudarshana Discus, with a beak as hard as a diamond, of great strength! Prevail in the **Dharma** <term>Dharma (the eternal law and cosmic order that sustains the universe)</term>! O Great Sudarshana Discus, you are most dear to me. Fulfill the command of Vishnu; having reached the enemies, burn them, burn them, strike them down! || 10 ||

->**Salutations to Lord Ganesha. Now begins the Procedure for the Evening Twilight Ritual.**<- <note>original: "ayaṃ sāyaṃ sandhyāvidhiḥ"; the Sandhya is a mandatory daily ritual performed at sunrise, noon, and sunset.</note>

In that ritual, having bathed in the evening and becoming pure, and having washed the feet and the hands; sitting upon a ritual seat; having performed **Breath Control** <term>Prāṇāyāma (the practice of regulating the breath to steady the mind before prayer)</term> three times:

Salutations to the Absolute. Salutations to the **Sacred Fire**. <note>original: "agnaye"; Agni is the messenger between gods and humans.</note> Salutations to the Earth. Salutations to the medicinal herbs. Salutations to Speech. Salutations to the **Lord of Speech**. <note>original: "vācaspataye"; an epithet for Brihaspati, the preceptor of the gods.</note> Salutations to Vishnu. I perform this ritual for the Great One. 

Whatever sin I have committed this day, during the day or the night, may all of that be released. Salutations to the Absolute. Salutations to the Sacred Fire. Salutations to the Lord of Speech. Salutations to Vishnu. Salutations to the speech within me. Salutations to the Lord of Speech. Salutations to Vishnu. I perform this for the Great One. This is the **Twilight Worship**. <note>original: "sandhyāvandanaṃ"</note>

**Now follows Breath Control.**

For the Sacred Sound **Om**, the Seer is the Supreme Absolute, the Deity is the Supreme Soul, and the Meter is the Divine **Gayatri**. <term>Gāyatrī (a highly sacred Vedic meter and the personified goddess of the mantra)</term> The ritual application is for the recitation of either twenty-eight or one hundred and eight times. 

**Om Earth, Om Atmosphere, Om Heaven, Om the Great World, Om the World of Creation, Om the World of Penance, Om the World of Truth.** <note>original: "bhūḥ bhuvaḥ svaḥ mahaḥ janaḥ tapaḥ satyam"; these are the seven vyahritis or planes of existence.</note> 

**We meditate upon that most excellent radiance of the divine Sun; may he inspire our intellect.** <note>original: "tat savitur vareṇyaṃ bhargo devasya dhīmahi dhiyo yo naḥ pracodayāt"; this is the famous Gayatri Mantra from the Rig Veda.</note> 

Om! The waters, the light, the essence, the nectar, the Absolute—Earth, Atmosphere, and Heaven—Om! Om, salutations to the Twilight Goddess.

**Now follows the Purificatory Sprinkling.** <term>Mārjjanam (the ritual act of sprinkling water on oneself while reciting mantras to remove spiritual impurities)</term>

O Waters, you are the source of bliss. Grant us energy, so that we may behold great joy. Like loving mothers, grant us a share of your most auspicious essence. We come to you quickly for that seat of yours, to which you give life. O Waters, give us birth.

May the Sun, and Anger, and the Lords of Anger, protect me from the sins committed through anger. Whatever sin I have committed during the night—by my mind, by my speech, by my hands, by my feet, by my stomach, or by my generative organ—may the Night wipe it away. Whatever impurity remains in me, I offer this "I" into the source of immortality, into the light of the Sun. **Svaha!** <note>original: "svāhā"; an exclamation used when making an offering into a ritual fire.</note>

**Now follows the Ritual Sipping of Water.** <term>Ācamanam (the sipping of water from the palm of the hand to achieve internal purity)</term>

May the waters purify the Earth; may the Earth, being purified, purify me.

|| <unclear>Saluting Vasudeva, the Lord of the Universe, the Master of the world's maintenance and dissolution; saluting Narayana, the sage rich in penance spoke. The Sages asked:</unclear> || (1)

|| From what? How? From where? What? For whom? Of what nature is the sacrificer? Whom do the practitioners of sacrifice know as the <term>Absolute<note>original: "brahma"; the ultimate, unchanging reality amidst the changing world.</note></term>? Whose form is this entire universe? From whom is the world born? In whom is it said to be established? Under whose control is the universe? By whose grace do the sages of peaceful mind see? || (2)

He who lacks properties such as sound and qualities such as <term>Sattva<note>original: "sattva"; the quality of goodness, harmony, and purity. The text suggests the Divine is beyond even these higher material qualities.</note></term>; in whom the eight-fold <term>opulences<note>original: "vibhūti"; supernatural powers or manifestations of divine glory.</note></term> shine eternally; in whom the eight-fold forms—beginning with Earth—are seen; to You, the <term>Soul of all beings<note>original: "bhūtātmane"</note></term>, salutations with speech, body, and mind. || (3) Having been thus addressed, the great-minded sage replied to his disciples:

Listen, all you sages, for Narayana is Supreme. From Him were born the ether, the air, the fire, and also the water; the earth, all medicinal plants, the rivers, and the oceans. From Narayana himself, the entire universe was born; into Him alone it dissolves, and in Him it remains established again. || (4)

Narayana is the Supreme <term>Absolute</term>; Narayana is the supreme penance. Narayana is the supreme duty; Narayana is the supreme goal. Narayana is the supreme sound, the supreme light, and the imperishable. Narayana is the supreme pilgrimage; Narayana is the supreme state. Narayana is the supreme god; Narayana is the supreme fruit. || (5)

Narayana is the supreme mantra; Narayana is the supreme beloved. Narayana is the supreme meditation; Narayana is the supreme revelation. || (6)

Narayana is the supreme truth; Narayana is the supreme lord. Narayana is the supreme god; those who are devoted to Narayana. Whoever constantly meditates on the flawless God Narayana, all their sins from ten million births are destroyed. || (7) Narayana is the Lord of the Universe; those who are devoted to Narayana.

**Om! Salutations to the Blessed <term>Vajrasattva<note>original: "Vajrasattva"; the "Diamond Being" or "Thunderbolt Being," a central figure in Vajrayana Buddhism representing the inherent purity of the mind and the power of purification.</note></term>.**

Thus have I heard: at one time, the Blessed One was staying in <term>Shravasti<note>An ancient city in India that served as a major center for the Buddha's teachings.</note></term>, in the <term>Jetavana<note>Jeta's Grove.</note></term>, the park provided by <term>Anathapindada<note>A wealthy merchant and patron of the Buddha whose name means "one who gives alms to the unprotected."</note></term>. "There is, O Dharanishvararaja, to the north of this <term>Buddha-field<note>original: "buddhakshetra"; a pure realm or spiritual domain presided over by a specific Buddha.</note></term>, a Buddha-field of the same name. There, a <term>Tathagata<note>An epithet for the Buddha meaning "One who has thus gone" or "One who has thus come" to the truth.</note></term> named Mahamaitri (Great Loving-Kindness), an Arhat, a perfectly awakened Buddha, resides. The radiance of that Blessed One spreads everywhere. At the side of that Blessed One stand two Bodhisattvas: one named Maitridharana and the second named <term>Manjughosha<note>original: "Manjughosha"; a name for Manjushri, the Bodhisattva of Wisdom, meaning "Gentle Voice."</note></term>. They attend upon that Blessed One, the perfectly awakened Buddha."

It is a great secret, of great power; it is a pacifier of all sins, a warder-off of all diseases, and a pacifier of all obstacles and <term>Vinayakas<note>original: "vinayaka"; a class of obstacle-creating spirits or demons.</note></term>.

It is as follows: 

**Om! Blaze, blaze! Great Blaze! Flame, flame! Blaze, blaze! Great Blaze! Slay, slay! Slay all wicked ones! Burn, burn! Burn all sinful karmas! Crush, crush! Crush all obstacles and hinderers! Shatter, shatter! Shatter all wicked ones! Hrih Hrih! Hum Hum! Phat Phat! <term>Svaha<note>A ritual exclamation used to conclude a mantra, sealing its power.</note></term>!**

This, O Dharanishvararaja, is the verse of the great mantra called Dharana, spoken by me. Whosoever of those beings..."

<meta>This page continues from the previous introduction of Buddhist figures like Vajrasattva and Dharanishvararaja, but the tone shifts toward a "Phalashruti"—a traditional concluding section of a text that describes the spiritual and worldly benefits (the "fruits") of reading or hearing the work. The supreme treasure, which is the <term>imperishable state<note>original: "aksharam padam"; refers to a permanent spiritual goal or reality that does not decay or change.</note></term>, is that sought-after goal, which is not of a multifaceted nature, but is supreme. It is peaceful, utterly beyond conflict, unagitated, a light beyond the senses, and supreme.

He who is composed of that, who is established in <term>nature<note>original: "prakriti"; the fundamental matter or creative energy of the universe.</note></term>, is the Sovereign Being in this world whose worldly qualities have been exhausted. Likewise, may he who is immutable, stainless, and purified—being praised and satisfied—grant what is desired. Within this work, reasoned arguments have been spoken by me and set forth in stories indeed. Opponents have been defeated, and these matters have been made known to the <term>Great Souls<note>original: "mahatmanam"; refers to spiritually advanced or noble beings.</note></term>. Whoever reads this <term>scripture<note>original: "shastram"; a technical or sacred treatise.</note></term>, which is endowed with every virtue and yields great fruit—

If he is a <term>chief among Brahmins<note>original: "vipra-mukhyah"; the priestly and scholarly class.</note></term>, he attains the end of worldly dissolution; if a ruler of men, he shall be victorious on the earth. A member of the <term>merchant class<note>original: "vaishya"; the third social class, typically involved in trade and agriculture.</note></term> obtains abundant wealth, and a member of the <term>laboring class<note>original: "shudra"; the fourth social class.</note></term> is joined with the capacity to achieve both religious merit and worldly purpose. It bestows health and sons, grants a desired lifespan, and increases the auspiciousness and joy of all people. That man who, being clean and devoted to purity, hears this entire <term>procedure<note>original: "vidhanam"; a prescribed ritual or systematic arrangement of teaching.</note></term>—he, having attained the foremost of all desires and aims, lives happily and fearlessly in this world. Know this noble <term>Dharma<note>original: "dharmam"; here referring to the Universal Law or the teaching itself.</note></term> which grants freedom from hostility; he who understands it proceeds to the highest good. Thus, directed by the mind, he should attain the <term>Auspicious<note>original: "shivam"; while often a name for the deity Shiva, here it functions as an adjective meaning "the benevolent," "the auspicious," or "the state of peace."</note></term>.

# Ritual Timings and the Merit of Sacred Days

<margin>
Shra.
11
</margin>

<margin>
Shra. 7
</margin>

Glory! Beginning with the month of **Shravana**<note>The fifth month of the Hindu lunar calendar, typically falling in July–August; it is considered highly sacred for fasting and vows.</note>, just as the sun enters the Bull (Taurus) and the **southern solstice**<note>original: "dakshinam ayanam"; the half-year period when the sun moves southwards, often associated with rituals for ancestors and spiritual discipline.</note> begins. When Shravana is near, there are thirty **ghatikas**<note>original: "ghatika"; a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes. Thirty ghatikas indicate a twelve-hour period, likely referring to the length of a specific ritual window.</note>. || 1 ||

On the seventh day of the **bright fortnight**<note>original: "shukla"; the waxing phase of the moon.</note> of Shravana, or if it falls on a Tuesday, one should complete the **Ayamaka**<note>original: "ayamakah"; likely a reference to a specific vow of dietary restraint or a ritual "stop" in the movement of the sun.</note>. One should perform the sacrifice, letting go of the top of the head, on the twentieth seat of the bright fortnight. || 2 ||

The seventh day of the bright fortnight of Shravana—lo!—will arrive in the evening on the path. For those who follow the path of the seventh, the fruit is equal to a **Horse Sacrifice**<note>original: "Ashvamedha"; the most prestigious and complex of ancient royal rituals. In this context, it is used as a standard "unit of merit" to describe the immense spiritual value of a simple fast or prayer.</note> or the birth of a son. || 3 ||

The seventh day of the bright fortnight of Shravana is named as that which is washed by the sun during the day. He who makes offerings on this path reaches the Lord of Justice who brings auspiciousness. While the sun stays in Capricorn or Taurus, one should practice **Dharma**<note>original: "dharmam"; religious duty, virtue, or the cosmic law.</note> or meditation. || 4 ||

The seventh day of the bright fortnight in the great month of **Magha**<note>A winter month (January–February) famous for ritual bathing in sacred rivers.</note> is spent in the water. Those men who perform rituals in Shravana attain eight-fold fruit. || 5 ||

The seventh day is wrapped in virtue and kept apart for as long as it is day. In the month of Shravana, the month of virtue, this day is known as the "Horse Sacrifice." Month by month, it is fulfilled. || 6 ||

When the full moon of **Vaishakha**<note>A spring month (April–May) marking the beginning of the grain harvest and many major festivals.</note> occurs, it is known as the virtuous Maghi moon. If it occurs on that path, then it yields the fruit of a full Horse Sacrifice for that month. On the full moon of virtue, it grants full rewards. It is indeed the essence of virtue. || 7 ||

The virtuous month of Magha is complete. Even excluding the full moon, it grants full rewards. It grants rewards in the evening like a Horse Sacrifice. On the **Full Moon**<note>original: "Purnima"</note> of Shravana, the month of virtue, it gives full rewards. || 8 ||

The month of Vaishakha is entirely complete because it is full of all virtue. It is remembered as the "Complete Virtue," filled with all religious merit. Indeed, it yields the fruit of all completeness. On the full day of the month of virtue, it grants full rewards. || 9 ||

On the full moon of the bright fortnight of Vaishakha, it grants the rewards of virtue. That complete full moon is remembered as being comprised of all virtue.

<summary>This page details the spiritual rewards (phala) for observing specific lunar days, particularly the seventh day and the full moon of the months Shravana, Magha, and Vaishakha, comparing their merit to the ancient Horse Sacrifice.</summary>
<keywords>Shravana, Saptami (Seventh Day), Ashvamedha (Horse Sacrifice), Purnima (Full Moon), Vaishakha, Magha, ritual merit, Hindu calendar</keywords>

<meta>Note on continuity: The previous page described a calendar of ritual merits and astronomical timings related to Hindu/Jain traditions (such as the Horse Sacrifice and the month of Shravana). # The Heart of the Perfection of Wisdom

**Om! Salutations to the Blessed Mother, the Perfection of Wisdom!**

Thus have I heard: at one time, the **Blessed One**<note>original: "Bhagavan"; a title for the Buddha, often translated as "Lord" or "Exalted One."</note> was staying in **Rajagriha**<note>A major city in ancient India, modern-day Rajgir, Bihar.</note> on **Vulture Peak Mountain**<note>original: "Gṛdhrakūṭa"; a famous site where the Buddha delivered many sermons.</note>, together with a great community of monks and a great community of **Bodhisattvas**<note>term: Bodhisattva - a being who seeks enlightenment not just for themselves, but for the sake of all sentient beings.</note>. At that time, the Blessed One entered the **meditative absorption**<note>original: "Samadhi"; a state of deep concentration or intense meditative focus.</note> called "Profound Radiance."

At that same time, the noble **Avalokiteshvara**<note>The Bodhisattva of Compassion.</note>, the Great Being, while practicing the profound course of the **Perfection of Wisdom**<note>original: "Prajñāpāramitā"; the philosophical core of Mahayana Buddhism, emphasizing the nature of reality.</note>, looked down and saw that the **five aggregates**<note>term: Skandhas - the five components (form, feeling, perception, mental formations, and consciousness) that make up a person's physical and mental existence.</note> were, in their own nature, empty.

"Here, O **Shariputra**<note>One of the Buddha's primary disciples, known for his wisdom.</note>, form is emptiness, and emptiness is itself form. Emptiness is not separate from form; form is not separate from emptiness. Whatever is form, that is emptiness; whatever is emptiness, that is form. The same applies to feelings, perceptions, mental formations, and consciousness."

"Here, O Shariputra, all **phenomena**<note>original: "dharmas"; in this context, the fundamental building blocks of reality.</note> are characterized by **emptiness**<note>term: Śūnyatā - the Buddhist concept that all things lack an inherent, independent essence and exist only in relation to other things.</note>. They are not produced, not ceased, not impure, not pure, not deficient, and not complete."

"Therefore, Shariputra, in emptiness there is no form, no feeling, no perception, no mental formations, no consciousness; no eye, ear, nose, tongue, body, or mind; no forms, sounds, smells, tastes, touchable objects, or mental objects; no element of the eye... up to no element of the mind-consciousness. There is no ignorance, no exhaustion of ignorance... up to no aging and death, and no exhaustion of aging and death. There is no suffering, no origin of suffering, no cessation, and no path. There is no wisdom, no attainment, and no non-attainment."

"Therefore, Shariputra, because there is no attainment, a Bodhisattva relies on the Perfection of Wisdom and dwells without **mental obscuration**<note>original: "cittāvaraṇa"; the veils or blockages of the mind that prevent clear seeing.</note>. Because there is no mental obscuration, he is unafraid, having transcended delusions, and reaches ultimate **Nirvana**<note>The final liberation from suffering and the cycle of rebirth.</note>."

"All the Buddhas situated in the **three times**<note>Past, present, and future.</note>, relying on the Perfection of Wisdom, have fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfect enlightenment. Therefore, one should know that the Perfection of Wisdom is the great mantra, the mantra of great knowledge, the unsurpassed mantra, the unequaled mantra, the quencher of all suffering, true because it is not false. The mantra of the Perfection of Wisdom is proclaimed as follows:"

->**Gone, gone, gone beyond, gone altogether beyond, awakening, hail!**<-
<note>original: "gate gate pāragate pārasaṃgate bodhi svāhā"</note>

Thus the Heart of the Perfection of Wisdom is completed. May there be auspiciousness for all the world!

---
