All the groups of gods—the **Rudras**<note>original: "Rūdrāḥ"; deities associated with storms and the fierce aspects of nature.</note>, the **Sadhyas**<note>original: "Sādhyā"; a class of celestial beings involved in the earliest rites of creation.</note>, the **Vasus**<note>original: "Vasavo"; eight elemental deities representing aspects of the cosmos like fire, earth, and wind.</note>, and the **Maruts**<note>original: "Marūtas"; the storm-gods who are the companions of Indra.</note>—are gathered together within this **Prajapati**<note>original: "Prajāpati"; the 'Lord of Procreation' or the primordial creator who encompasses the entire universe.</note>. Therefore, they call this the Truth. Thus is the **Agni**<note>original: "Agni"; the ritual fire and the god of fire.</note> explained; likewise is the **Ashvamedha**<note>original: "Aśvamedha"; the 'Horse Sacrifice,' the most significant royal ritual in ancient India.</note>. All the groups of gods are gathered within this Prajapati; therefore, they call this the Truth. **1**

Prajapati beheld the **Viraj**<note>original: "Virāj"; a term meaning 'sovereignty' or 'resplendence,' personified here as a feminine creative power or a specific meter of poetry.</note>. Once she was created, she turned away from him. She became the food of the gods. Therefore, they call this the Truth. **2**

Prajapati desired: "May I create food and the enjoyment of food in the Viraj." He beheld the **Agnishtoma**<note>original: "Agniṣṭoma"; the 'Praise of Agni,' a foundational seven-day Soma sacrifice.</note>. He took it and performed the sacrifice with it. Then, indeed, he created food and the enjoyment of food in the Viraj. One who knows this and performs the Agnishtoma sacrifice creates food and the enjoyment of food in the Viraj. **3**

That Prajapati desired: "May I be many; may I be procreated." He performed **Tapas**<note>original: "Tapaḥ"; spiritual heat or intense meditation used as a creative force.</note>. Having performed Tapas, he beheld this **Threefold Chant**<note>original: "Trivṛtam"; the simplest arrangement of Vedic hymns.</note>. He took it and performed the sacrifice with it. Then, indeed, he was procreated with offspring. **4**

He desired: "May I possess cattle." He performed Tapas. Having performed Tapas, he beheld this **Fifteenfold Chant**<note>original: "Pañcadaśam"; a ritual arrangement of verses.</note>. He took it and performed the sacrifice with it. Then, indeed, he created cattle. One who knows this and sacrifices with the Fifteenfold Chant becomes a possessor of cattle. **5**

He desired: "May I attain stability." He performed Tapas. Having performed Tapas, he beheld this **Seventeenfold Chant**<note>original: "Saptadaśam"</note>. He took it and performed the sacrifice with it. Then, indeed, he became established. One who knows this and sacrifices with the Seventeenfold Chant becomes truly established. **6**

He desired: "May I attain all-encompassing prosperity." He performed Tapas. Having performed Tapas, he beheld this **Twenty-onefold Chant**<note>original: "Ekaviṃśam"</note>. He took it and performed the sacrifice with it. Then, indeed, he attained all-encompassing prosperity. One who knows this and sacrifices with the Twenty-onefold Chant attains all-encompassing prosperity. **7**

That very thing was the **Garland**<note>original: "Srag"; a metaphor for the sequential order of the sacrifice, envisioned as a woven wreath.</note> created in succession. The one whom Prajapati first accepted became the Viraj; therefore, it is called Viraj. **8**

The gods, having become animals, approached it. **Agni**<note>original: "Agniḥ"; the god of Fire.</note> said: "I shall be the first to accept it." Agni, having become the Fifteenfold Chant, accepted it. Therefore, Agni is associated with the Fifteenfold. **9**

Agni said: "One who knows this and performs the Agnishtoma sacrifice becomes a possessor of cattle." **10**

The **Ashvins**<note>original: "Aśvinā"; the twin divine physicians.</note> said to him: "We two shall be the second to accept this." The Ashvins, having become the Seventeenfold Chant, accepted it. Therefore, the Ashvins are associated with the Seventeenfold. **11**

The **Rudras** said: "We shall be the third to accept it." The Rudras, having become the Twenty-onefold Chant, accepted it. Therefore, the Rudras are associated with the Twenty-onefold. **12**

The **Maruts** said: "We shall be the fourth to accept it." The Maruts, having become the **Twenty-ninefold Chant**<note>original: "Triṇavāḥ"</note>, accepted it. Therefore, the Maruts are associated with the Twenty-ninefold. **13**

**Aditi**<note>original: "Aditi"; the mother of the gods, representing the infinite.</note> said: "I shall be the fifth to accept it." Aditi, having become the **Thirty-threefold Chant**<note>original: "Trayastriṃśī"</note>, accepted it. Therefore, Aditi is associated with the Thirty-threefold. **14**

The **Meters**<note>original: "Chandāṃsi"; the rhythmic structures of the hymns like Gayatri or Trishtubh.</note> said: "We shall be the sixth to accept it." These Meters became the face of the Agnishtoma. Prajapati said: "This garland is mine alone; I shall be the first to accept it here." That garland of Prajapati became the **Fourfold Chant**<note>original: "Chatuṣṭoma"</note>. Therefore, Prajapati is associated with the Fourfold Chant. **15**

They know this indeed as that Garland. Therefore, they say: "Prajapati beheld the Viraj; once created, she turned away; she became the food of the gods." **16**

She became united with Prajapati himself. From Prajapati, from the Sun, that Garland became made of his very essence. One who knows this and performs the Agnishtoma sacrifice, that Garland becomes his. **17**

Agni is a thousand, Prajapati is a thousand, the Sacrificer is a thousand, the Garland is a thousand. All his "thousands" become contented and united for the one who knows this and performs the Agnishtoma sacrifice. **18**

Prajapati, having created living beings, felt as if he were emptied out. He beheld this Fourfold Chant. He took it and performed the sacrifice with it. Then, indeed, he was replenished again. One who knows this and performs the Agnishtoma sacrifice is replenished. **19**

**Indra**<note>original: "Indro"; the King of the Gods.</note>, having slain the demon **Vritra**<note>original: "Vṛtram"; the serpent-demon of drought whose death released the waters.</note>, felt as if he were emptied out. One who knows this and performs the Agnishtoma sacrifice is replenished. **20** **36**

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a Vedic liturgical discussion on ancestors and the god Indra. This page begins a new section or belongs to a different manuscript altogether, transitioning into a Jain philosophical commentary (Vivṛti) on logic and epistemology. The focus shifts from ritual practice to formal debate regarding the nature of the Omniscient Being and logical proof.</meta>

->ॐ Salutations to the **One Free from Passion**<note>original: "Vītarāgāya"; an epithet for a Jina or Tirthankara who has conquered all worldly attachments and desires.</note>॥<-

This is worthy of praise, the praise of the Lord Sage; he should extend it to the lineage of the world. Because it is established from two, it would be **deficient in proof**<note>original: "sādhya-vikalaṃ"; a logical fallacy where the example used lacks the quality that one is trying to prove.</note> according to the **means of valid knowledge**<note>original: "pramāṇāt"; the epistemological framework for establishing truth, such as perception or inference.</note>. The assertion of the existence of such a being is not entirely inappropriate. For thus, being established from this, it would be deficient in the thing to be proved, having emerged entirely from the remainder. In this context, both the **thing to be proven**<note>original: "sādhya"; the conclusion or thesis of a logical argument.</note> and the **means of proof**<note>original: "sādhana"; the reason or evidence used to establish the thesis.</note> truly exist. And this is specifically because of the **Omniscient One**<note>original: "sarvajñāditi"; referring to the Jain concept of a being with infinite knowledge.</note>.

Where there is a state of being proven, there is neither **permanence**<note>original: "nityatvam"; the quality of being eternal or unchanging.</note> nor the oneness of existence and so forth; therefore, something distinct from that is being established.

Now, if **omnipresence**<note>original: "sarvagatatvaṃ"; the quality of being present everywhere.</note> is that which is to be proven, then permanence must also be that which is to be proven. And thus it has been said: "Now, if permanence is to be proven, what will be the means of proof?" If it is so, then to say that omnipresence is the thing to be proven and permanence is the means of proof is a contradiction.

Now, if **impermanence**<note>original: "anityatve"; the state of being transient or subject to change.</note> is that which is to be proven, then omnipresence is the **reason**<note>original: "hetuḥ"; the logical ground or middle term in an inference.</note>. And that reason must be characterized by the property of the thing to be proven. It is not proper for the reason to be something other than that which is characterized by the property of the thing to be proven. Therefore, omnipresence and permanence are distinct as the "thing to be proven" and the "means of proof."

For thus: where there is permanence, there is omnipresence; and where there is omnipresence, there is permanence—this **logical pervasion**<note>original: "vyāptiḥ"; the universal, inseparable connection between the reason and the conclusion, essential for a valid inference.</note> is established. And here...

<summary>This page marks a shift into Jain philosophical logic, debating how the qualities of an Omniscient Being—specifically permanence and omnipresence—relate to one another as "proof" and "conclusion." It utilizes formal Indian syllogistic terms to establish the logical connection (vyāpti) between these divine attributes.</summary>
<keywords>Vītarāga, Jain logic, Sādhya, Sādhana, Omniscience, Vyāpti, Permanence, Omnipresence, Epistemology</keywords>

The focus shifts entirely from epistemology to a devotional and ritualistic text (likely Shaivite or Vedic-Agamic) praising Vayu, the god of wind, as the supreme spirit and the vital breath of the universe.</meta>

<margin>8</margin>

# The Regulation of the Worship of Vayu

->**Om! Salutations to Shiva.**<-

Now, this **Vayu**<note>The personified deity of the wind and the air.</note> is famous as the embodiment of all gods and as the one who moves everywhere. In the form of the wind, he is the **vital life-breath**<note>original: "prāṇa-bhūto"; the subtle energy or breath that sustains life.</note> within the three worlds. Upon his rising, all the activities of living beings proceed. He is the one who fashions all things, the giver of breath to all creatures; he is widely known as the very life-breath itself.

He is the radiance of all the gods and is established within the inner self. Sustaining the worlds, he is the source from which this entire universe, both moving and unmoving, arises. This Lord Vayu is the master of all who possess breath. He is renowned as **Vata**<note>original: "vāta"; another common name for the wind, often used in a more elemental sense.</note> and is famous for his all-pervading nature.

He is indeed the holy body assumed by the god **Dhurjati**<note>original: "dhūrjaṭeḥ"; an epithet of the god Shiva, referring to his "matted locks."</note>, who is the soul of all and possesses all forms. That body is famously called "Vayu." It is that body which supports the worlds and sets the worlds in motion.

That same form belongs to Vishnu, to Brahma the Supreme, and to the Lord **Sharva**<note>An epithet of Shiva meaning "The Archer" or "The All-Destroyer."</note>. Likewise, it is the form of **the one whose vehicle is a peacock**<note>original: "śikhivāhanaḥ"; referring to the war-god Skanda or Kartikeya.</note>, as well as of **Agni**<note>The god of fire.</note>, **Varuna**<note>The god of the celestial waters and cosmic order.</note>, and **Yama**<note>The god of death and justice.</note>. That body is established as the form of all the gods.

Lord Vayu is proclaimed to be the very soul of that universal body. He is the physical form of all things, and likewise the soul of all. This Lord Vayu is the **Sacrifice**<note>original: "yajñaḥ"; the ritual offering which, in this context, is identified with the cosmic cycle of life maintained by the wind.</note> famous throughout the world. He is called Vishnu, and he is known as Brahma. He is Rudra, the Lord of the Gods, Indra, and the **Sun**<note>original: "vibhāvasuḥ"; literally "one whose wealth is light."</note>. He is the Lord of All and the Great God, **Maheshvara**<note>A supreme title for Shiva.</note>.

He is the breath of every being and the god **Prajapati**<note>The "Lord of Progeny" or the creator deity.</note>. He is Time, the source of the elements, the god of death, and the sage **Vishravas**<note>The father of Kubera and Ravana, sometimes associated with the wind or Vedic wisdom.</note>. He is the Guardian of the World and is renowned as a God. Since this Lord Vayu is proclaimed as the soul and the sacrifice, everything that exists in this world is intended for him.

Therefore, in all undertakings, one should worship Vayu specifically. Thus has this scripture been prescribed by me and presented exactly as it should be.

### The Rewards of Worship

By worshipping Vayu, a **Brahmin**<note>The priestly class.</note> is freed from all sins. A **Kshatriya**<note>The warrior or ruling class.</note> attains victory in battle. A **Vaishya**<note>The merchant or farmer class.</note> obtains a bountiful harvest of grain. A **Shudra**<note>The laboring class.</note> is also freed from all sins—of this there is no doubt. It is remembered in tradition that there is no merit greater than the fruit gained from the worship of Vayu.

One should perform the worship of Vayu with this very mantra. The ritual should be carried out correctly, as it fulfills all desires:

**"Om, salutations to Vayu."**

### Conclusion

**Thus ends the chapter on the regulations for the worship of Vayu.**

**Finished.**

**May there be auspiciousness.**

<summary>This page concludes a liturgical chapter on the deity Vayu, identifying him as the supreme life-breath (Prana) and the physical manifestation of all major Hindu gods. It ends with the specific benefits (Phalashruti) for each social class and the concluding mantra for his worship.</summary>

<meta>The previous page focused on the spiritual and ritual perfection of mantras, invoking deities like Sarasvati and Lakshmi to grant "all-attainment" and the "restoration of life." It establishes the philosophical and practical foundations of medicine, categorizing diseases and emphasizing the necessity of the right remedy to preserve the body as a vessel for immortality.</meta>

# **The Excellence of Healing**

->**Excellent Sayings**<-

I shall now explain the ten types of **brilliant potency** <note>original: "suvarcalam"; likely referring to a specific class of potent medicinal herbs or a preparation known for its "shining" or "vitalizing" properties.</note>. I shall always speak of this as the destroyer of all defects. Without this, one cannot succeed, just as one cannot fight in a war without a weapon. These actions are indeed certain; there is no other way to decide the matter. For when the body is well-maintained, it is there that **immortality** <note>original: "amṛta"; the nectar of the gods or the state of deathlessness.</note> finds its place.

६५ ॥ When a **disease** <term>vyādhi|physical or mental ailment that disturbs the body's natural balance</term> arises, it is classified in a specific way. It is said to be either **curable** <term>sādhya|conditions that can be successfully treated with medicine and regimen</term> or **incurable** <term>asādhya|terminal or chronic conditions that do not respond to treatment</term>; I shall now explain the remedy for them. Even if a disease is curable, it will not be overcome without the proper means. A curable disease is mastered through a remedy; otherwise, success is never achieved. Therefore, I speak of that which is curable; that which is incurable cannot be overcome.

६६ ॥ Hereafter, I shall explain the excellent characteristics of **medicine** <term>auṣadha|remedies derived from herbs, minerals, or other natural substances</term>, so that disease does not bring about the destruction of the body. One should apply virtuous knowledge to create medicine that grants success. Even with virtue, a curable disease requires a well-administered medicine. If a disease is not overcome, one must look to the specific qualities of the medicine.

६७ ॥ Why would medicine not destroy a disease that has arisen in the body? The excellence of medicine is defined by whether a disease is curable or incurable. With the right medicine, a curable disease is destroyed instantly. Through its qualities, the medicine strikes at the symptoms and the root. However, if a disease is incurable, even the best medicine becomes useless.

६८ ॥ Thus, I shall speak of what is always beneficial for the body. Without medicine, the diseases of men are not destroyed. A mortal becomes free from illness when they always rely upon the right remedy. Therefore, I shall explain medicine and the signs of the destruction of disease. Where a disease is curable, medicine is the essential tool.

६९ ॥ Then, one should perform **purification** <note>original: "prāyaścitta"; usually meaning "atonement" for sins, but in medical texts like the Garuda Purana, it refers to a therapeutic ritual or treatment intended to restore the body’s balance.</note> for the highest pacification of the ailment. Without medicine, a disease is not killed, and its destruction is difficult to obtain. Therefore, I shall explain the signs of the destruction of curable diseases. With medicine, that which is curable is destroyed in an instant.

७० ॥ Thus ends the [unclear] section in the glorious **Garuda Purana** <note>original: "Garuḍa Purāṇa"; one of the eighteen Great Puranas of Hinduism, which covers a vast range of topics including cosmology, medicine, and the afterlife.</note>.

<summary>This page outlines the fundamental principles of Ayurvedic medicine as found in the Garuda Purana, categorizing diseases as curable or incurable. It emphasizes that while medicine is a vital "weapon" against illness, its success depends on the nature of the disease and the quality of the remedy applied.</summary>
<keywords>medicine, healing, Garuda Purana, curable disease, incurable disease, remedy, immortality, anatomy, Ayurvedic ethics</keywords>

Now, with a body bristling with joy like the blossoms of the thorn-apple <note>original: "madana-kantaki"; literally "the thorn of the god of love," a poetic way to describe the hair standing on end from excitement or devotion.</note>, and as if sprinkling the air with the nectar of his words, he approached the Blessed One. Having bowed his head in reverence, he asked: "O Blessed One, what in this world is our most excellent wealth? What is the knowledge that has neither beginning nor end? What is the fame that never perishes? What is the fortune <note>original: "Lakshmi"; referring here to both material wealth and the goddess of prosperity.</note> that remains unhindered? What is the sole ground of all happiness—is it offspring? And what is the cause of such offspring? How is a virtuous child born, through whom the ancestors in the **World of the Fathers** <note>original: "pitru-loke"; the realm where deceased ancestors reside, dependent on the offerings of their living descendants.</note> might be free from sorrow? Or by what means might a lineage become stainless? Therefore, may the Blessed One instruct us, so that we may attain the highest good."

Then the descendant of Atharvan spoke all of this to Prajāpati <term>Prajāpati|The "Lord of Progeny" or Creator deity, often consulted in texts regarding the origins of life and social order</term>. "We, as knowers of the sacred hymns, indeed know this. Just as we have seen it, so shall it be told. If a man’s **vital seed** <term>śukra|the refined essence of the body, often translated as semen or reproductive energy</term> is nourished and strong, then through his offspring he gains all the worlds. This is exactly how the generation of offspring is ordained. There is a distinction between a man producing an heir and the offspring born of women."

Prajāpati said: "Everything is a matter of maturation. And what is the cause? Therefore, one must first have a direct perception of the cause, and then the refinement <note>original: "saṃskāra"; a transformative rite or preparation.</note> follows. In this matter, one should first examine the **fertile window** <term>ṛtukāla|the specific period following menstruation considered optimal for conception in Ayurvedic tradition</term>. For when union occurs during the fertile window, the **vital potency** <note>original: "vīrya"; the concentrated power within the body responsible for strength and reproduction.</note> becomes unfailing. 

She should be one who has performed the ritual bath after her season. Purity, the presence of fire, the wearing of garlands and ointments, and the sweet fragrance of incense and similar things are most excellent. Indeed, within a year, one obtains a good son. Now, what is the nature of offspring? And of what kind? In this matter, some consider a son or daughters to be the best offspring. But a virtuous child brings joy to both the mother and the father. Truly, everything is the ripening of one's vital potency. Therefore, one should increase that potency. Just as the potency increases, so should one understand its ripening. He who possesses this potency becomes truly powerful. Therefore, the supreme secret of this **vitality** <note>original: "vīrya"</note> must be maintained by all means." 

Thus says **Vātsyāyana** <note>The famous sage and author, best known for the Kamasutra, but often cited in broader contexts of "Kama-shastra" or the science of social and physical life.</note>.

<summary>This page shifts into a dialogue between a seeker and Prajāpati regarding the importance of virtuous offspring and the preservation of vital energy (vīrya). It emphasizes the timing of conception (ṛtukāla) and the physical and ritual purity required for a healthy lineage.</summary>

Or else, the **Lord of the Gods** <note>original: "surādhipa"; referring to Indra, the king of the celestial realms.</note> gave to him a divine garland and anointing unguent. He, being of noble mind and firm in his vows, then accepted all of those gifts. Indeed, **Dharmarāja** <term>Dharmarāja|Literally "The King of Righteousness," a title for Yudhisthira, the eldest Pandava brother, known for his adherence to duty.</term> attained a kingdom that was very difficult to cross, terrifying, and as vast as a shoreless sea <note>This metaphor describes the heavy and often dangerous burden of sovereign responsibility.</note>. Within this kingdom, the entire world was conquered, according to its importance and through all its various riches. 

For the wise say that the root of a kingdom is **Righteousness** <term>dharma|The complex concept of duty, law, ethics, and universal order that sustains society.</term>. 

"Therefore, O you of cruel intellect <note>The speaker is likely addressing a person of worldly or malicious intent, contrasting their mindset with the path of virtue.</note>, I wish to speak of the balance between **Righteousness, Wealth, and Pleasure** <note>original: "dharmārthakāma"; the three worldly goals of human life.</note>. For just as there are those born into unrighteousness, **Vibhīṣaṇa** <note>The brother of the demon-king Ravana, who famously defected to Rama's side to uphold moral law over family loyalty.</note> acted in the opposite way. 

For one who desires to follow his own duty, both Righteousness and Desire lead toward happiness, though they are distinct from one another. Men always seek to increase their **Wealth** <term>artha|Material prosperity and political power.</term>, but the wise say that Righteousness and Wealth must be held as equals.

A man who has heard the teachings of Righteousness is released from every kind of sin. By establishing Righteousness through Righteousness, and attaining Wealth through Righteousness, he may obtain happiness; and from that happiness, he attains Righteousness once more. 

Truly, he who lives by transgressing Righteousness falls into sin. For this reason, they say that Righteousness is the supreme **Goal of Human Existence** <term>puruṣārtha|The fundamental aims of a human being: duty, prosperity, pleasure, and liberation.</term>. Thus, Righteousness is declared to be the highest principle in the world. 

The person who stands firm in Righteousness is liberated from the bonds of **Worldly Existence** <term>saṃsāra|The cycle of birth, suffering, and rebirth.</term> in the sense of ultimate truth. If a kingdom is obtained by crushing Righteousness, it will never bear auspicious fruit. Therefore, a person who has strayed from the path of Righteousness is mindless and shall never find happiness."

<summary>This page discusses the ethical foundations of kingship, arguing that Righteousness (Dharma) is the essential root of a stable kingdom and the primary goal of human life. It contrasts the pursuit of wealth and pleasure with the necessity of moral duty, using the figures of Dharmarāja and Vibhīṣaṇa as examples of virtuous conduct.</summary>

**Righteousness** <term>dharma|The ethical, moral, and cosmic order that sustains society and the universe.</term> is characterized by **detachment** <term>nivṛtti|The path of withdrawal from worldly desires and active engagement, focusing instead on spiritual liberation.</term>. On this matter, the lawgiver **Manu** <note>The legendary progenitor of humanity and author of the *Manusmriti*, a foundational text on social and moral duty.</note> says: "Vedic action is of two kinds: **active engagement** <note>original: "pravṛtta"; refers to actions performed with the desire for a specific result, such as wealth or heaven.</note> and **detachment** <note>original: "nivṛtta"; refers to actions performed without desire, leading to spiritual knowledge.</note>." Herein lies the true essence of Righteousness, as also stated by the sage **Gautama** <note>One of the ancient seers and author of a foundational *Dharmasutra* (legal manual).</note>.

**The Honorable Dhritarashtra** <note>The blind king of the *Mahabharata* epic, whose inquiries often serve as the framework for philosophical teachings.</note> said: "O Blessed One, what then is this **Righteousness**? By hearing which a person may become happy? From what is one liberated from the **cycle of worldly existence** <term>saṃsāra|The continuous cycle of birth, death, and rebirth in which the soul is caught.</term>? Whose great soul's words are these?"

These words were spoken even in the presence of **Vibhishana** <note>A key figure from the *Ramayana*; although a demon by birth, he is celebrated for defecting to Rama’s side to uphold Dharma.</note>. Why was this Vibhishana present? Was it because of a doubt regarding Righteousness and Unrighteousness?

On this point, the wise say: Even if there is a doubt between Righteousness and **Unrighteousness** <note>original: "dharmādharmau"</note>, that which is associated with the name Vibhishana is indeed Righteousness. Those who go to the house of Vibhishana find it so. "What was spoken by Vibhishana and recounted by me, of this I have no doubt." All of this is the speech of Vibhishana.

Having been asked thus by Dhritarashtra, **Sanjaya** <note>Dhritarashtra’s advisor and charioteer, who possesses divine vision to witness the events of the war and provide moral guidance.</note> spoke again: "Listen, O King, to the eternal Righteousness, which is free from all doubt. As it is practiced eternally, so it increases and flourishes. All these actions are either right or wrong."

**Dhritarashtra** said: "Ah! Salutations to **Righteousness**! How did this proceed, Sanjaya?"

Here it is explained by the counselor Vibhishana: "What I have truly spoken, when there was doubt, was the same Righteousness spoken of by Dhritarashtra, which destroys all sins. As Righteousness is practiced eternally, it eternally increases. One is not obstructed by Righteousness." Just as there is constant scrutiny and constant investigation into the nature of right and wrong—Sanjaya, how is this matter of Righteousness and Unrighteousness constantly spoken of? It is explained through eight **sacred verses** <note>original: "mantraiśca"</note> regarding Righteousness, without any doubt.

**Dhritarashtra** said...

# The Essence of Righteousness

Where **Righteousness** <term>Dharma|A foundational concept in Indian philosophy referring to cosmic order, social duty, law, and moral virtue.</term> is slain by **Unrighteousness** <term>Adharma|The opposite of Dharma; chaos, vice, or the failure to perform one's inherent duty.</term>, and where **Truth** is slain by **Untruth** while the members of the assembly look on, then those members are themselves slain by their silence. <note>original: "hatāstatra sabhāsadaḥ"; literally "the assembly-sitters are killed." This verse emphasizes that witnesses to injustice share the guilt if they do not intervene.</note> 

Therefore, **Righteousness** must not be violated, lest violated Righteousness destroy us. For Righteousness, being destroyed, destroys; and Righteousness, being preserved, preserves. <note>original: "dharmo rakṣati rakṣitaḥ"; a famous maxim meaning that the moral order protects those who uphold it.</note>

Therefore, Righteousness is not to be destroyed; it is established everywhere. Just as an elephant made of wood, or a deer made of leather, or a **Brahmin** <term>Vipra|A member of the priestly or scholarly class in traditional Indian society.</term> who has not studied the sacred texts—these three bear the name only. <note>original: "nāmadhārakāḥ"; meaning they possess the outward label but lack the essential substance or function of the thing they claim to be.</note> Just as a donkey carrying a load of sandalwood knows the weight of the load but not the fragrance of the wood, so too is one who has studied the **Law Books** <term>Dharmaśāstra|A genre of Sanskrit texts concerning religious and social duties.</term> but does not understand their meaning; such persons bear the name only.

That is Righteousness which is praised in this world by virtuous men. That is called Unrighteousness by the world where Righteousness is not found. 

Practice Righteousness, not Unrighteousness; speak the truth, not untruth. Look to the highest Righteousness, do not look to the lower. 

Whenever there is a decline in Righteousness, O **Descendant of Bharata** <note>original: "Bhārata"; a title for Arjuna in the Bhagavad Gita, referring to his lineage.</note>, and a rise in Unrighteousness, then I manifest Myself. For the protection of the good, for the destruction of the wicked, and for the establishment of Righteousness, I am born age after age. <note>These two verses are among the most famous in the Bhagavad Gita (4.7-8), where the deity Krishna explains the purpose of his earthly incarnations.</note>

The four "feet" or pillars of Righteousness were fully established in the **Golden Age** <term>Kṛtayuga|The first and most perfect of the four world ages in Hindu cosmology.</term>. In the second age, three feet remained; in the third age, two; and in the **Current Age** <term>Kaliyuga|The fourth and final age, characterized by spiritual decline and discord.</term>, only one foot remains.

Thus, the discourse on Righteousness is concluded. May there be auspiciousness. These **Questions on Righteousness** <term>Dharmapraśnāḥ|A title or category for a series of inquiries or verses regarding moral conduct.</term> are finished. <unclear>May there be well-being.</unclear>

# The Nature of the Wise and the Structure of Sound

Who is the man possessed of **Wisdom** <term>Pragna|A state of intuitive insight or higher consciousness that goes beyond mere intellectual knowledge.</term> who, having attained it, gives forth his voice in debate? In this vast expanse of knowledge, who is the virtuous one of mine? Indeed, who would not be moved by such a name? The illustrious one, who is like a vessel of knowledge, gives but does not take. He possesses a graceful eloquence, a master of the resonance of sound, skilled in his actions. He is one who has truly reached the **Secret Heart of the Scriptures** <term>Shastra-marma|The hidden or essential meaning of sacred texts that lies beneath the literal word.</term>, his intellect firm and steady as a mountain.

Just as the illustrious one is like a forest of wisdom, in him there is no lack of great insight. He understands the beginning and the end, the friend and the master of the three modes of debate, whose words are filled with meaning and purpose. The illustrious one resides in the essence of **Musical Notes** <term>Svara|In Sanskrit, this refers to both a musical note and a vowel, reflecting the belief that all language and music share a divine vibrational origin.</term>, which are the very self of sound.

He understands the meaning of the beautiful sounds and the essence of the **Middle Voice** <term>Madhyama|The intermediate stage of speech, where thought begins to take form as mental sound before it is spoken aloud.</term>. There is no waste in his expression; his mind is established in the accomplishment of meaning and purpose. Like the flow of the **Middle Path**, he balances the sounds of the world.

<note>The following section of the manuscript contains highly repetitive syllabic patterns (artha, sukha, mana, svara). This is a common feature in technical Sanskrit treatises, either serving as a mnemonic device for students or representing a "Citra-kavya"—a form of patterned poetry where syllables are arranged to create visual or rhythmic puzzles.</note>

The most excellent of the created **Notes** <term>Svara|Refers here to the foundational vibrations of the universe.</term> is that which is established in the self, a form of happiness and a means of attainment. For the one who understands the purpose of the mind, the meaning is found in the joy of the soul. Through the repetition of the sacred syllables, the mind becomes purified. The purpose of the meaning is the meaning of the purpose; in the joy of the mind, the wise find their rest. 

Through the repeated essence of sound, the purpose is achieved. The mind, being well-directed toward the meaning of the scriptures, finds the happiness that is true and lasting. In the resonance of the **Notes**, the seeker finds the path to the ultimate goal.

It then transitions into a technical, rhythmic meditation on the relationship between sacred sound (Svara), meaning (Artha), and mental peace (Sukha).</summary>

# The Inquiry into the Ultimate Reality

Now, therefore, the inquiry into **Brahman** <term>Brahman|The absolute, infinite reality or the supreme cosmic spirit in Hindu philosophy.</term> || [Brahman is that] from which proceed the origin, sustenance, and dissolution of this universe || Because Scripture is the source of knowledge regarding it || But that Brahman is the subject of study because of the harmony and systematic connection of all sacred texts || Because the Self is described as "seeing" or thinking, the "Un-scriptural" [the primordial matter of the Samkhya school] is not the cause of the world || If it is argued that the word "Self" is used in a figurative sense, we say no, because of the command of liberation for one who is devoted to it || And because there is no statement that it should be abandoned || Because of the individual's "merging" into the Self during deep sleep ||

Because this is heard in the scriptures || Because of the uniformity of the teachings || Because it is heard directly in the scriptures || The "One Consisting of Bliss" is the Supreme Self, because of the repetition of the word "Bliss" || If it is argued that the word refers to a modification or product, we say no, because the suffix denotes abundance || And because He is designated as the cause of that bliss || Moreover, that which is described in the hymns of the **Vedas** <term>Veda|The oldest and most sacred layer of Sanskrit literature, regarded as divine revelation.</term> is the same as that which is sung here || The "other one" [the individual soul] is not the subject, because the descriptions do not fit ||

And because of the declaration of difference || And because of the expression of desire, there is no dependence on inference [of matter] || And the scripture teaches the union of the soul with this Bliss || The "One Within" the sun and the eye is the Supreme, because His attributes are taught || And because there is another declared who is different from the individual soul || The "Space" mentioned is Brahman, because of the characteristic marks || For the same reason, the "Life-Force" is Brahman || The "Light" is Brahman, because of the mention of its "feet" [parts or quarters] ||

If it is argued that Brahman is not meant because the **Metre** <term>Chhandas|The rhythmic and poetic structures of Vedic hymns, often personified as sacred powers.</term> is mentioned, we say no, because the instruction is intended to direct the mind toward Brahman; for so it is seen elsewhere || And this is possible because of the description of beings as "feet" || If it is argued that there is a difference in the instruction [between the two texts], we say no, because there is no contradiction in either case || The "Life-Force" is Brahman, because of the consistency of the context || If it is said that the instruction is about the speaker's own self [and not Brahman], we say no, because there is an abundance of references to the **Inner Self** <term>Adhyatma|That which pertains to the individual soul or the spiritual self within the body.</term> in this passage || But the instruction is given from the perspective of a Seer who has realized the Truth, like the sage Vamadeva || If it is argued that the characteristics of the individual soul and the primary life-force are found, we say no; for then the meditation would be three-fold; and because the life-force is accepted here [as Brahman] due to the connection of its characteristics || Thus concludes the first section of the first chapter in the Commentary on the Holy **Brahma Sutras** <term>Brahma Sutra|A collection of short, pithy aphorisms attributed to Badarayana that systematize the teachings of the Upanishads.</term> composed by the most excellent wandering teacher, the holy feet of God [Shankara]. || 1 || <margin>Here: 11 Topics, 31 Aphorisms</margin>

<summary>This page lists the first 31 aphorisms of the Brahma Sutras, which establish that the "Inquiry into Brahman" is the highest goal and define Brahman as the intelligent cause of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Brahman, Vedanta, Shankara, Sutras, Inquiry, Bliss, Life-Force, Reality</keywords>

# The Dialogue Between Vishnu and Narada
## From the Padma Purana

Tell me, O Lord of Sages, of that conversation with **Vishnu** <term>Vishnu|One of the principal deities of Hinduism, the Preserver and Protector of the universe.</term>, which achieves both worldly happiness and the highest liberation—whether it be concerning the Divine with attributes or the Divine beyond all qualities. || 1 ||

**Suta** <note>Suta is the traditional narrator of the Puranas, often addressing a gathering of sages in the Naimisha forest.</note> said: 
Listen, all you sages, to what was asked by **Yudhishthira** <term>Yudhishthira|The eldest of the Pandava brothers and a protagonist of the Mahabharata, known for his righteousness.</term> || 2 || I shall tell you a beautiful account from a past age, O best of sages. || 3 || 

Once, the sage **Narada** <term>Narada|A divine sage and traveling musician known as a messenger of the gods and a primary devotee of Vishnu.</term>, accompanied by other sages and filled with great joy || 4 || offered praise to the Supreme Lord, who was pleased and is always affectionate toward his devotees. || 5 || Then the Lord Vishnu, whose eyes are like lotus petals, being satisfied || 6 || spoke sweet words to Narada, the most excellent of sages. || 7 ||

**The Holy Lord said:** 
O Narada, you are greatly fortunate and ever-desirous of the welfare of all worlds. || 8 || Tell me exactly what is dwelling in your mind. || 9 ||

**Narada said:** 
O Lord, God of the gods, All-knowing Supreme Person; tell me of **Knowledge** <term>Jnana|Philosophical or spiritual knowledge.</term> combined with **Realization** <term>Vijnana|Practical or experiential wisdom that goes beyond mere theoretical knowledge.</term>, and of devotion directed toward no other. || 10 || And speak of detachment, O Great-minded Lord of the Universe || 11 || by which realization alone, men truly become **Liberated while living** <term>Jivanmukta|A person who has attained spiritual liberation (Moksha) while still inhabiting a physical body.</term>. || 12 || Tell me this, O Fortunate One, out of your desire for the welfare of your devotees. || 13 ||

**The Holy Lord said:** 
Listen, Narada, I shall tell you the great secret, the most hidden of all secrets, by knowing which all sages attain the supreme **Nirvana** <term>Nirvana|The state of final liberation from the cycle of birth and death; here used synonymously with Moksha.</term>. || 14 || 

It is without beginning, middle, or end; eternal, pure, and ancient. || 15 || It is realization, supreme bliss, the very form of truth, and free from all duality. || 16 || This single form is the Supreme **Brahman** <term>Brahman|The ultimate, unchanging reality or cosmic soul in Hindu philosophy.</term>, all-pervading and stainless. || 17 || This entire universe was created by Me alone, for those whose minds are deluded by **Maya** <term>Maya|The powerful force of illusion that makes the temporary world appear as the ultimate reality.</term>. || 18 ||

Therefore, listen to Me as I explain the means of crossing over this illusion. Worship Me with a concentrated mind and with devotion that seeks no other. || 19 || My devotee, making Me the supreme goal, with the mind fixed on Me, shall come to My state. || 20 || 

Free from "mine-ness," free from ego, and delighting in the welfare of all beings || 21 || whoever worships in this way eternally, he reaches the supreme goal. || 22 || Therefore, O tiger among sages, practice firm devotion to Me. || 23 || 

Having spoken thus, O sage, Lord Vishnu then disappeared. || 24 || Narada, then, out of great joy, bowed to the Lord of the World || 25 || and went quickly to the forest of penance, meditating on the eternal Vishnu. || 26 ||

Thus has been told to you, O **Brahmana** <note>original: "vipra"</note>, the vision of Narada. Whoever reads this or even hears it, he goes to the supreme goal. || 27 || 

->**Thus ends the chapter named "The Yoga of Knowledge" in the conversation between Vishnu and Narada within the Final Section of the glorious Padma Purana.**<-

->**Finished.**<-

<summary>This text details a divine dialogue where the god Vishnu reveals the "secret of secrets" to the sage Narada, explaining that spiritual liberation (Moksha) is achieved through a combination of experiential knowledge and exclusive devotion (Bhakti).</summary>

# The Stages of Renunciation
## Rules for the Four Classes of Ascetics

When a person desires to enter the path of **Renunciation** <term>Sannyasa|The final stage of life in the Hindu social system, characterized by the abandonment of worldly ties to focus on spiritual liberation.</term>, they first become a **Kutichaka** <term>Kutichaka|The first stage of renunciation where the ascetic lives in a hut near their family, receives food from them, and keeps their sacred thread and hair-tuft.</term>. In this stage, one carries a triple staff, maintains the hair-tuft and the sacred thread, and remains near their mother, father, virtuous wife, and sons for their support. One should perform the **Purification Sacrifice** <term>Viraja Homa|A fire ritual performed during the initiation into renunciation to symbolize the burning away of worldly desires and the purification of the inner elements.</term> while chanting the appropriate Vedic mantras. One should then conceptually place the sacred fires within their own soul and formally entrust their son with their worldly duties. After performing the rites to the Lord of Procreation and making a fist as previously prescribed, and after staying in this state for some days, one attains the stage of **Bahudaka** <term>Bahudaka|The second stage of renunciation where the ascetic leaves home to wander, collecting water from many sources and begging for food from virtuous households.</term>. At this stage, one takes up the loincloth, a covering, a water pot, and specifically a bamboo staff according to the traditional mantras.

<column-break/>

When the ascetic desires further progress, the following rules apply to the **Hamsa** <term>Hamsa|The third stage of renunciation, symbolized by the "swan" which can distinguish milk from water; the ascetic gives up the sacred thread and focuses on meditation.</term> stage: one must discard both the hair-tuft and the sacred thread into the water. The sage must wander, staying only one night in a village and five nights in a city. During the four months of the rainy season, the sage must remain in one place. One should call upon their friends and relatives, and after asking their permission and inviting the officiating priests as before, one should perform the **Sacrifice to the Universal Fire** <term>Vaishvanara Ishti|A Vedic ritual dedicated to Agni as the "Universal Man," performed here to internalize the sacrificial fire.</term> using the mantras from one's own Veda. One should recite: "You are Brahman, you are the sacrifice, you are the sacred call" <note>original: "tvam brahma tvam yajnas tvam vashatkara"</note>. Then, one should recite the **Savitri Mantra** <term>Savitri|Commonly known as the Gayatri Mantra, the most sacred verse of the Rigveda dedicated to the solar deity Savitr.</term>. After giving up the hair-tuft and sacred thread to one's sons or heirs, one should make the **Declaration of Renunciation** <note>original: "praisha"; a formal announcement</note> by saying: "I have renounced" <note>original: "mayā sannyastam"</note>. The holder of the triple staff must always perform these duties and daily seek alms at the houses of the learned. After giving everything away according to the mantras, one becomes a **Hamsa**. Finally, when one desires the highest renunciation, the rule for the **Paramahamsa** <term>Paramahamsa|The highest state of an ascetic, "the Supreme Swan," who has realized the Absolute and is free from all social conventions and rituals.</term> is followed. In this state, after giving the hair-tuft and sacred thread to the sons and others, one becomes a holder of a single staff, maintains silence, and specifically lives only on begged food. 卍

<summary>This page outlines the four progressive stages of Hindu renunciation (Kutichaka, Bahudaka, Hamsa, and Paramahamsa), detailing the specific rituals, mantras, and behavioral restrictions required to transition from a householder to a wandering ascetic.</summary>
<keywords>Renunciation, Sannyasa, Kutichaka, Bahudaka, Hamsa, Paramahamsa, Vaishvanara Ishti, Viraja Homa, Asceticism, Vedic Rituals</keywords>

# The Discipline of the Mind
## On the Dangers of Attachment and Passion

Indeed, it leads him by the hand—that person who is joined to the mind's desires—whereas he who is free from passion is truly free. When one is colored by attachment, the torment of **Manmatha** <term>Manmatha|A name for the god of love or lust, literally meaning 'the churner of the mind,' representing the agitating power of desire.</term> dwelling in the heart is destroyed <note>The text likely implies the torment is "conquered" through the discipline previously described, or conversely, that it "destroys" the seeker's peace.</note>. Just as someone might lose their life-breaths on account of poison, so too can one be lost through the various flavors of sensory pleasure.

He whose love is for a **wanton woman** <term>Pumshchali|Literally 'one who runs after men'; used in ascetic literature to symbolize the most destabilizing forms of worldly attraction.</term>, his life-breaths are truly at risk. The one who is ignorant of what is beneficial or harmful simply throws his life away. For the wise man, seeing that sensory objects are the very abode of faults, keeps his mind constant <unclear>or</unclear> focused.

If men in this world know that diseases and **passionate women** <term>Pramada|A term meaning a young or alluring woman, but derived from a root meaning 'intoxication' or 'carelessness.' In this context, it represents the intoxicating power of lust.</term> churn or agitate them, then they know the truth. The wise are indeed deluded by these <unclear>words</unclear> of passion. He who is attached to them and becomes colored by that attachment attains only suffering. Just as hands <unclear>grasp</unclear>, and one cannot hold what is not grasped, one should not be attached to passionate women.

Thus ends the section on **Passionate Women**. Now begins the [instruction on]...

<column-break/>

...happens as if with a wanton woman. He is abandoned by all his life-breaths, yet he does not feel the pain of it until it is too late. Whatever fault is committed through the various flavors of the senses, the fault attaches to him; one should not become colored by such association. 

One should recognize misfortune even when it looks like a skill, and see the pitiless nature of things as before. The dull-witted and the careless are attached as if to a wanton woman. No one is able to lead away a man who stands on the wrong path, just as one cannot easily be moved from a physical pit. 

Thus, joined with passionate women and tormented by **Manmatha** (Lust), one should not associate with these if the self is to be happy. Joined with passionate women and tormented by Lust, that man attains only suffering; only the wise man understands this delusion. He who knows this does not eternally suffer from Lust, which is known by the very name of "Sorrow." 

**Passionate women** (and the lust they represent) are always destined for the sake of **Samsara** <term>Samsara|The eternal cycle of birth, death, and rebirth, characterized by suffering and driven by the illusions of the material world.</term>. They are created for the sake of eternal suffering; thus is the soul cut asunder. Humans joined with such passions encounter sorrow. Just as in the hand, whoever possesses sensory "taste" or desire... <note>The text repeats the hand metaphor, likely referring to the way desire 'grasps' the soul.</note>

We shall explain: Those humans who are mindless and attached to passions are seized by misfortunes and thrown down as if by a firm hand-grip. Passionate women and the desires they stir are always destined to keep the soul in the cycle of existence.

<summary>This page serves as an ascetic warning against the dangers of sensory attachment and sexual desire, using the figures of the "Pramada" (passionate woman) and "Pumshchali" (wanton woman) as metaphors for the traps of Samsara. It emphasizes that the wise must recognize these attractions as the source of suffering and mental agitation (Manmatha).</summary>

This is for the sake of happiness for those seeking the essence. Now, the dream is to be spoken of with nectar; that dream is described with nectar. **The Piercing of the Intoxicated** means the dream is to be known through nectar. **The Piercing of the Intoxicated** is to be spoken of with nectar via the **white lotus stalk** <term>Shveta-mrinala|The cool, white fibrous stalk of a lotus, symbolizing the cool clarity of a mind freed from the 'heat' of passion.</term>, as the **Goad of the Mind**. Now, what white lotus stalk is to be spoken of with nectar, as that which is easily known for the sake of happiness for those who were once intoxicated? Indeed, the white Durva grass is the delight. Now, the **Goad of the Mind** is the delight of the white Durva grass. Now, the white Durva grass is the delight. A dream of nectar, a dream of nectar, a dream of nectar. Indeed, the white Durva grass is the delight. Indeed, the white Durva grass is the delight of the **Goad of the Mind**. A dream of nectar, a dream of nectar, a dream of nectar. Now, the Piercing of the Intoxicated is through nectar.

<meta>Continuity Note: There is a significant shift in subject matter from the previous page. ### Now begins the account of the origin of the sixteenfold types of palaces.

Among the expansive <term>Prithunga (a category of wide or elevated structures)</term> class, one hundred and fifty-five types of <term>Vimanas (temples or celestial palaces)</term> are described. In these, the main body of the structure is three hundred units long, with a square area at its center. It is equipped with a door-opening measuring eight <term>hastas (a traditional unit of measurement roughly equal to 18 inches or the length of a forearm)</term>.

The height of the main structure is said to be ten *hastas*, with the <term>shikhara (the mountain-like spire or tower of the temple)</term> situated above it. Above that, the height is four *hastas* and the width is eight *hastas*. This is proclaimed as the first of the *Vimana* types, known as the *Prithunga*.

#### Now, the second type is described:

It is considered to be three hundred units in both length and width, with a square center. Likewise, it is equipped with a door-opening of eight *hastas*. The height should be ten *hastas*, with a *shikhara* of five *hastas*. The width is prescribed as eight *hastas*. This is the arrangement for the second type.

#### And likewise for the third type:

It is three hundred and fifty units long, with a central square measuring eight and a half units. The door is said to be three *hastas* high and five *hastas* wide. The *shikhara* should be five *hastas*, the length ten *hastas*, and the width should be eight *hastas*. This is proclaimed as the third type.

#### Now the fourth:

It is four hundred units long, and the central square is ten units. The door is ten *hastas* high and five *hastas* wide. The *shikhara* is six *hastas* high and the width is ten *hastas*. The overall height measurement is ten *hastas*. This is known as the fourth type.

#### Now the fifth:

It is four hundred and fifty units long, and the central square is ten and a half units. The door is said to be five *hastas* high and three *hastas* wide. The *shikhara* is six *hastas* high and five *hastas* wide. The height measurement is ten *hastas*. This is known as the fifth type.

#### Now the sixth:

->Rama.<- <note>original: "Rāmaḥ"; an invocation to the deity Rama.</note> By the passing of many, twenty years are remembered. By sixty, fifty are remembered; by sixty, it is known in three ways. By two, there is one fortnight, and twelve months are well-known. <note>This introductory passage appears to be a mnemonic or a record regarding the calculation of time or a specific era, possibly related to the age of the manuscript or a cycle of years.</note> Auspiciousness.

Just as the one sun illumines this entire world, so does the <term>Lord of the Field (the soul or consciousness)</term> illumine the entire <term>Field (the body and material world)</term>, O descendant of Bharata. Those who, through the eye of knowledge, perceive the distinction between the Field and the Knower of the Field, and the liberation of beings from <term>Prakriti (material nature)</term>, they reach the Supreme. <note>These verses conclude Chapter 13 of the Bhagavad Gita.</note>

That Supreme Person, O son of Pritha, is attainable through unswerving devotion—He within whom all beings reside and by whom all this world is pervaded. I shall now declare to you, O bull among the Bharatas, the time at which departing <term>yogis (practitioners of spiritual discipline)</term> attain the state of non-return, and also the time of return.

Fire, light, daytime, the bright fortnight of the moon, and the six months of the sun’s northern path—departing in these, the people who know <term>Brahman (the Absolute Reality)</term> go to the Absolute. Smoke, night, the dark fortnight, and the six months of the sun’s southern path—reaching the lunar light by these, the *yogi* returns to the cycle of birth.

These two paths, the light and the dark, are considered the world's eternal paths. By the one, one goes to the state of non-return; by the other, one returns again. Knowing these two paths, O son of Pritha, no *yogi* is ever deluded. Therefore, O Arjuna, be established in <term>Yoga (spiritual union)</term> at all times.

Whatever meritorious fruit is promised in the **Vedas**, in **sacrifices**, in **penances**, and in **gifts**—the *yogi*, having understood all this, transcends it all and reaches the Supreme Primeval Abode.

Thus, in the Upanishads of the glorious **Bhagavad Gita**, in the science of the Absolute, in the scripture of Yoga, in the dialogue between Sri Krishna and Arjuna... <note>This is the standard closing formula (colophon) for a chapter of the Gita, though the text cuts off before naming the specific chapter.</note>

**Homage to the Buddha.**

Thus have I heard: At one time, the Blessed One <note>original: "Bhagavan"</note> was dwelling at **Rajagriha** <note>a major city in ancient India, modern Rajgir</note> on the **Vulture Peak mountain**, together with a great assembly of five hundred monks and a great assembly of <term>Bodhisattvas (beings following the path to Buddhahood)</term>.

There, the Blessed One addressed the Venerable **Sharadvatiputra** <note>original: "Sharadvatiputra"; another name for Sariputra, one of the Buddha's chief disciples</note>: "Sharadvatiputra, there exists the <term>Dharmata (the intrinsic nature of reality)</term>. Whether <term>Tathagatas</term> <note>original: "Tathagata"; a title for the Buddha meaning 'One who has thus gone'</note> arise or do not arise, this nature of things remains fixed: this stability of things, this regularity of things, this <term>Dharmadhatu (the sphere of ultimate reality)</term>.

"And Sharadvatiputra, this nature of things—whether <term>Tathagatas</term> arise or do not arise—this nature of things, this stability of things, this regularity of things, this sphere of reality remains fixed. The Buddha has realized this for himself and has fully awakened to it.

"Having fully awakened to it, he teaches it, makes it known, reveals it, illuminates it, and explains it clearly; namely: **Ignorance** <note>original: "avidya"</note> is the condition for **Mental Formations**; mental formations are the condition for **Consciousness**; consciousness is the condition for **Name-and-Form** <note>original: "namarupa"; the mental and physical constituents of existence</note>; name-and-form is the condition for the **Six Sense Spheres**; the six sense spheres are the condition for **Contact**; contact is the condition for **Feeling**; feeling is the condition for **Craving**; craving is the condition for—

<summary>This page contains the opening of a Buddhist Sutra where the Buddha explains the fixed nature of reality (Dharmata) to his disciple Sariputra, specifically introducing the Twelve Nidānas or the chain of Dependent Origination.</summary>
<keywords>Buddha, Sariputra, Rajagriha, Vulture Peak, Dharmata, Dharmadhatu, Dependent Origination, Twelve Nidanas, Buddhism</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the "miscellany" nature of the manuscript. While the previous page (33) contained a Buddhist sutra on dependent origination, this page (82) contains a commentary on the Chandogya Upanishad, a primary text of Hindu philosophy. The shift suggests this manuscript was a compendium of diverse philosophical traditions. The text focuses on the "Dialogue of the Vital Breaths," a famous allegory used to explain the supremacy of the life force (Prana) over sensory functions.</meta>

...endowed with various excellent qualities. 

**Now, concerning the dialogue of Speech and the other faculties:** Regarding the state in which Speech and the others experience their own objects, the mantra states how "we have become gathered together <note>original: "samvrttah"</note> in our own functions." They took up that mantra themselves. To show that it is not proper for any one faculty—such as Speech—to claim the pride of understanding the mantra's meaning alone, the text says:

**"Now indeed, he introduces this mantra."** In the dialogue of Speech and the others, just as in the practice of chanting, he strives to show how the meaning of the mantra benefits all the faculties.

"The <term>Prana (Vital Breath)</term> is indeed the <term>Udgitha (the High Chant)</term>," and so forth. While this Vital Breath was striving, Speech and the others gathered around it. In the dialogue between Speech and the Udgitha, although the Udgitha is the cause of all beings—for indeed, all these beings enter into the Breath and arise from the Breath—it is for this reason that while the Breath was striving, Speech and the others gathered. 

Here, "gathering" <note>original: "samvrtih"</note> means "accumulation" or "benefit" <note>original: "upacayah"</note>. Just as when servants strive for the sake of a King, their own interests are increased; in the same way, when Speech and the others strive for the sake of the Breath, their own benefit increases by assisting the Breath.

Thus, since the Udgitha alone is the driver of all beings, the accumulation of the faculties is directed toward it. Knowing this intention, by chanting the Udgitha as the Vital Breath, and having attained the Breath, one attains everything. Just as one who reaches the Emperor attains the whole kingdom, so it is in this case.

<summary>This page provides a philosophical commentary on the Chandogya Upanishad, explaining how the sensory faculties (Speech, etc.) are subordinate to and supported by the primary life force (Prana) during the Udgitha chant. It uses the metaphor of a King and his servants to illustrate how supporting the central life principle leads to the prosperity of all individual functions.</summary>
<keywords>Prana, Vital Breath, Udgitha, High Chant, Chandogya Upanishad, Speech, sensory faculties, Vedic commentary, allegory of the senses</keywords>

<margin>Ra. Pra. <note>Likely an abbreviation for the Rajaprashniya (Rāyapaseṇaiya), a major Jaina scripture describing the dialogue between King Paesi and the monk Keshikumara.</note></margin>

# Om! Salutations to the Illustrious Ganesha.

Through virtue and the **good monk** <note>original: "susaha"</note>, every action is indeed fully perfected. Like a palace of ancient friendship, the scripture and the conduct are heart-pleasing. The Dharma is a dwelling of various kinds, pure and excellent in every part. || 1 ||

I forever bow to the <term>Tirthankaras (Ford-Makers)</term> and the <term>Siddhas (Perfected Beings)</term>—those whose lotus-like feet are worshipped by gods, demons, and the lords of men; those who are adorned with the jewels of stainless virtue and are honored by the celestial and the worldly alike. || 2–3 ||

To that Dharma which is the most **auspicious of all auspicious things** <note>original: "mangala-mangalam"</note>, the cause of all welfare, and the sanctuary for all living beings—to that Dharma, I bow. || 4 ||

I worship at all times the <term>Shramanas (ascetics)</term>, the "Beloved of the Gods," who possess manifested virtues and whose perfected success is born of a stainless and bright intellect. || 5 ||

May those who have conquered the dust of sensory objects, who are firm in their vows and complete in their discipline and humility—the well-learned, the well-behaved, and the possessors of virtuous knowledge—be victorious and happy for all time. || 6 ||

And now, whatever pertains to the **power of religious stories** <note>original: "dhamma-kaha-anubhava"</note> shall be told by me for the sake of the Dharma. Whatever has been seen, or heard, or previously composed in various ways—all that I honor through the power of religious storytelling, always desiring happiness. || 7 ||

In this world, through the power of religious storytelling, whatever meaning or purpose exists—both the scripture <note>original: "suttam"; the thread of the text</note> and its inner meaning—all of it is obtained through the **grace of the Guru** <note>original: "guru-pasadena"</note>. || 8 ||

Herein lies the method of religious storytelling: it is the source of all sciences and the attainment of all goals. Through the power of religious storytelling, this "Composition of Religious Tales" has been created. || 9 ||

The scripture and its explained meaning have all been composed through the power of religious storytelling. Whoever performs their duty here, by that very act, the entire Dharma is practiced. || 10 ||

May the power of religious storytelling always grant all success to the Siddhas. May all the people of the world, having earned merit, perform the Dharma at all times. || 11 ||

Perform the Dharma according to your own nature; for humans, the Dharma is always the source of happiness. One who practices the Dharma will surely attain only happiness. || 12 ||

May all tasks be successful! I bow to the Dharma always. I bow to the Dharma! May all tasks be successful!

Om. For this Primordial <note>original: "Adya"</note> Great Illusion <note>original: "Mahamaya"</note>, the Supreme Goddess, the six-limbed placements <term>Nyasa: a ritual where the practitioner touches parts of their body while reciting mantras to instill divine energy</term> beginning with the heart should be performed. Om Aim Hrim Shrim. To the Primordial Great Illusion, who embodies the forms of Great Kali, Great Lakshmi, and Great Saraswati: the placement of the heart and other limbs begins. 

Om Hram, salutations to the heart.

Om Hum, to the crown of the head, vashat!

<note>These are seed-syllables (bijas) paired with specific body parts and ritual exclamations to "protect" the practitioner during the rite.</note>

Thus the six-limbed placements starting with the heart are to be performed. Then, breath control. Om, salutations to the Great Kali. Having performed breath control in this way, one should display the ritual hand-gestures <term>Mudra</term> beginning with the invocation. 

There, first, the <margin>Primordial</margin> invocation <note>original: "avahana"</note> should be shown. Then the installation, then the invitation to be present, and then the containment. Having displayed the hand-gestures in this manner, one should worship with incense and other offerings using the root mantra: "Om, salutations to the Primordial Goddess." 

Then, one should perform the worship of the **First Circle** <term>Avarana: a "veil" or layer of deities surrounding the central deity in a sacred diagram or mandala</term>. 

Within that, one should worship the Primordial Goddess in the center: "Om, salutations to the Primordial Goddess." Then, one should worship in the corners and other directions: 
Om, salutations to the Kali of the South.

Om, salutations to the Secret Kali.

Om, salutations to the Kali of Wealth. 
Om, salutations to the Kali of Success. 
Om, salutations to the Fierce Kali. 

This concludes the First Circle. Then, the **Second Circle**. One should worship in the corners and other directions: 
Om, salutations to the Skull-Bearing Kali. 
Om, salutations to the North-Eastern Kali. 
Om, salutations to the Terrifying Kali. 
Om, salutations to the Frightful Kali. 
Om, salutations to the Kali of the Night of Time. 
Om, salutations to the Fierce Goddess Kali. 

This concludes the Second Circle. Then, the **Third Circle**. One should worship in the corners and other directions: 
Om, salutations to Brahmi. 
Om, salutations to Maheshvari. 
Om, salutations to Kaumari. 
Om, salutations to Vaishnavi. 
Om, salutations to Varahi.

Salutations to the Younger Brother of Indra!
Salutations to the Remover of Sins!
Salutations to the Holy Krishna!

### Breath Control <term>Pranayama</term>

**Om Earth, Om Atmosphere, Om Heaven, Om the Vast, Om the Generative, Om the Penance, Om the Truth.** <note>These are the seven "Vyahritis" or planes of existence</note>

**We meditate upon that adorable light of the divine Sun; may he inspire our intellect.** <note>This is the famous Gayatri Mantra from Rigveda 3.62.10</note>

**Om! The Water is Light, the Essence is Immortality, the Absolute is Earth, Atmosphere, and Heaven! Om!**

This concludes the **Breath Control**.

### The Ritual Sprinkling <term>Marjana: a ritual for inner and outer purification using water</term>

O Waters! Since you are the source of bliss, grant us energy so that we may behold great joy. Like loving mothers, grant us a share of your most auspicious essence here on earth. We come to you quickly for that seat of yours, for the satisfaction of which you nourish us. O Waters, grant us the power of procreation. <note>This is a translation of Rigveda 10.9.1-3</note>

This concludes the **Ritual Sprinkling**.

### Adoration of the Sun <term>Suryopasthana: standing in prayer facing the sun</term>

The rays of light bear up that God who knows all beings, the Sun, so that everyone may see him. The wonderful face of the gods has risen—the eye of Mitra, Varuna, and Agni. He has filled the heaven, the earth, and the atmosphere. The Sun is the soul of all that moves and all that is still. <note>Rigveda 1.50.1 and 1.115.1</note>

May we see that bright Eye, established by the gods, rising in the East, for a hundred autumns. May we live for a hundred autumns. May we hear for a hundred autumns. May we speak for a hundred autumns. May we be free from poverty for a hundred autumns. And may we live even more than a hundred autumns. <note>A prayer for longevity and health from the White Yajurveda 36.24</note>

This concludes the **Adoration of the Sun**.

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page follows a section of Tantric salutations. The focus shifts here to a philosophical discourse on the nature of the Self (Atman) using classical metaphors of gold and fire. The text appears to be a commentary or a philosophical treatise on "self-luminosity" (svayaṃprakāśa), a key concept in Indian philosophy where consciousness is seen as being aware of itself without needing an external light.</meta>

<margin>Pr. Ma. <note>original: "प्र.म." (pra.ma.), likely an abbreviation for a chapter or section title such as Prathama Maṇḍala or Prakaraṇa-mālā.</note></margin>

The Self is self-evident and self-luminous, the Creator <note>original: "dhātā"</note>. The Self is self-evident and self-luminous; it is the giver and the earth; the Self is self-evident as the Creator. Thus it was said by the sage Atri <note>original: "atṛkenāha," referring to one of the Seven Sages of Vedic tradition</note>.

Just as the attainment of the Self is like the attainment of gold, and gold, and gold, so too is the self-evident Self. Wherever there is self-luminosity, what need is there for a giver or for earth? || 21 || The elemental Self <term>dhātvātmā<gloss>The core essence or constitutional self that forms the basis of existence</gloss></term> is self-evident.

Just as fire manifests itself in connection with wood and the consumer of offerings <note>original: "hutāśana," a ritual epithet for fire</note>, so the Self is self-luminous by its own self-luminous nature. For that which manifests itself, being self-existent in its self-luminous nature, self-luminous knowledge <term>jñāna<gloss>spiritual or conscious awareness</gloss></term> perceives nothing else [as separate]. Just as one says "this is gold, this is gold," so it is for the self-luminous Self. Just as "this is gold" manifests of its own accord, so it is.

For just as the self of seeing <note>the faculty of perception</note> is not [usually perceived as] self-luminous, nevertheless, it is self-luminous. For as every specific object is self-luminous, so knowledge would be self-luminous. The mind is called "gold" because of its self-luminous nature. Just as each specific object is self-luminous, so knowledge should be of a self-luminous nature, just as the Self itself would be self-luminous.

How does it manifest through the perfected Self? In that same way, the Self would be self-luminous. Self-luminous through the Self, self-luminous in the self-born...

<vocab>self-luminous, self, gold, knowledge, fire, self-evident</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the concept of 'svayaṃprakāśa' (self-luminosity), arguing that the Self and consciousness are self-evident and require no external proof, much like fire is revealed through wood or gold is recognized by its own luster. It uses the sage Atri as an authority to establish that knowledge and the mind share this inherently luminous quality.</summary>
<keywords>Atman, self-luminosity, Svayamprakasha, Jnana, Advaita philosophy, metaphor of gold, nature of consciousness</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---

It begins with a standard Puranic invocation to Vishnu and the author Vyasa, then transitions into a series of mantras honoring a wide array of Buddhist and potentially Hindu-Tantric deities. This suggests the manuscript may be a compendium of rituals or a multi-text codex common in Himalayan traditions.</meta>

<meta>This page, numbered 8 in the manuscript, marks a significant shift from the devotional and ritualistic focus of the previous section (page 4). # Page 8 (८)

As my words are heard and the meanings of things are fully processed <note>original: "praviracitāḥ," referring to the mental construction of the world</note>, they are abandoned; and as they are let go, my heart feels as if it is greatly rejoicing. ||1||

As the mind comes to know the true nature of reality <term>Tattva<gloss>The "that-ness" or fundamental principle of existence</gloss></term>, the mental stains <term>Kashayas<gloss>Vices or emotional attachments that "color" or cloud the mind</gloss></term> no longer flow out, and the mind attains a state of deep peace. ||2||

Oh, wonderful! Salutations to myself, for whom there is nothing! Or rather, salutations to myself, to whom belongs everything that falls within the range of the mind. ||3||

Knowledge, the object of knowledge, and the knower—this triad does not exist in reality. I am that stainless one <note>original: "nirañjanaḥ," meaning without blemish or "collyrium"</note> in whom this whole world appears due to ignorance. ||4||

Duality is the root of all suffering. There is no other medicine for it than this: all that is seen is false, and I am the one, pure, stainless essence of consciousness. ||5||

I am pure awareness alone. The limitation <term>Upadhi<gloss>A "superimposition" or limiting adjunct, like a colored glass that makes clear light appear colored</gloss></term> of ignorance was merely imagined by me. By reflecting in this way eternally, my state is now one that is beyond all mental constructs <note>original: "nirvikalpe," a state of "changeless" or "undifferentiated" absorption</note>. ||6||

I have no bondage, nor do I have liberation. My delusion has been pacified and is now without support. Oh! The universe resides in me, yet in truth, it does not reside in me. ||7||

This entire universe in all its detail is clearly nothing at all. The Self is pure consciousness alone; so what is there to imagine, and by whom? ||8||

The body, heaven and hell, bondage and liberation, and fear—all of these are merely imagination. What use have I for these, I who am the conscious Self? ||9||

Oh! Even in a crowd of people, I see no duality. It has become like a forest to me <note>suggesting the crowd no longer distracts him from his solitary nature</note>. Where should I find delight? ||10||

I am not the body, nor is the body mine. I am not the individual soul <term>Jiva<gloss>The "living being" or individual ego-self</gloss></term>; I am indeed pure Consciousness. This alone was my bondage: that I had a thirst for living. ||11||

Oh! In me, the infinite great ocean, when the wind of the mind rises, the various wonderful waves of the world suddenly emerge. ||12||

In me, the infinite great ocean, when the wind of the mind grows calm, the merchant-like individual soul finds that the perishable ship of the world is lost—what a misfortune! <note>The "misfortune" is ironic, as the loss of the world-ship is actually liberation</note> ||13||

Oh! In me, the infinite great ocean, how wonderful it is that the waves of individual souls rise, collide, play, and then naturally dissolve back into me. ||14||

Thus ends the fifteenth section <note>Historically, these verses usually appear in Chapter 2. This manuscript identifies it as the "Mumukshu Prakarana," or the Section for the Seeker of Liberation</note> in the glorious *Ashtavakra Gita*. ||15||

### Ashtavakra said:

Through this teaching of things as they truly are, you have fulfilled your purpose. Contemplating your true nature, remain composed; do not engage in further asceticism. ||1||

And he spoke further: Do whatever you wish, say whatever you wish, or know whatever you will. You are the Lord, pure Consciousness, within whom this entire universe resides. ||2||

<summary>This page captures a moment of enlightenment where the student (King Janaka) describes the dissolution of his ego and his realization that he is the infinite ocean of consciousness. It concludes with the sage Ashtavakra confirming that Janaka has reached the goal of spiritual practice.</summary>
