--- Page 87 ---
<meta>Folio 43 continues the philosophical inquiry into the nature of reality. While the previous page (if following a standard Vedāntic commentary) likely concluded the technical methods of textual interpretation, this page applies those methods to prove the central tenet of Advaita: that the perceived world of many things is illusory, and only the One (non-duality) is truly real.</meta>

<margin>Ve. Da. <note>original: "वे. द." — an abbreviation for *Vedānta-darśana*, the system of philosophy being discussed</note></margin>

When the determination of the meaning of the Upanishadic sentences is achieved through these **six-fold criteria** <term>ṣaḍvidha-liṅga<gloss>Traditional analytical tools used to find the core message of a text: the beginning, the conclusion, repetition, originality, the result, and supporting arguments</gloss></term>, then: because duality is subject to **sublation** <term>bādhya<gloss>The process of being invalidated or proven false by a higher level of reality</gloss></term>, it is characterized as **illusory** <term>mithyātva<gloss>The state of being "neither real nor unreal," describing the material world which appears real but disappears upon spiritual enlightenment</gloss></term>. Because non-duality cannot be sublated, it is characterized as Truth. Thus, the proof for the illusoriness of the material world is also established. **It has been said:**

An objection is raised: Since the world of duality is perceived as real, how can non-duality be established? **To this, he says:** The establishment of non-duality must be stated because that world is sublated. **"Truth"** <note>original: "satyam" — used here as a formal concession: "It is true that you see duality, but..."</note> is taken up in order to support the argument for illusoriness.

Is duality sublated by logic or inference? No, **Scripture alone indeed** <term>Śruti<gloss>The "heard" revelation, specifically the Vedas and Upanishads, considered the highest authority in this tradition</gloss></term> is the sublater. Because of the sublation of the world of duality, **the establishment of non-duality occurs.**

Fearing the objection—"How can sublation occur without a valid means of knowledge acting as the sublater?"—**he says "by Scripture alone."** In the second half of the verse beginning "Non-duality must be stated," the reason is given: **"By Scripture."** The sublation of the world of duality occurs **by Scripture alone**, and not through logic alone—**this is the intent.**

An objection is raised: How can duality, which is directly experienced through **perception** <term>pratyakṣa<gloss>Knowledge gained through the five senses</gloss></term>, be sublated by Scripture? **Fearing this, he says: Sublation by Scripture should be understood here**, because Scripture is **stronger** than perception and other means. In a conflict between Scripture and perception, Scripture is **stronger**. And that [authority of Scripture] depends on the sublation of perception—**this is the intent.** Scripture is stronger than perception...

<meta>catchword at bottom center: 43 Thus</meta>

<summary>This page argues for the superiority of scriptural revelation (Śruti) over physical perception (Pratyakṣa) in determining ultimate reality. It concludes that because the world of duality is "sublatable" by higher knowledge, it is ultimately illusory, while the non-dual Truth remains constant.</summary>
<keywords>Advaita, non-duality, sublation, illusoriness, Scripture vs. Perception, Vedānta logic</keywords>

|| Just as one might cover something with barley-meal, or with a vessel of clarified butter, or with a cow-pen, or with the hand, in the very same way, this [breath] envelops the internal consciousness. <note>The text uses everyday objects to describe how the life-force contains or "wraps" the consciousness during the process of withdrawal.</note> Om. ### Now begins the section on the remembrance of the object of meditation

In that regard, it has been said that the object of meditation is the Supreme Light; how should it be remembered? The text says: Om. This Self is indeed immortal; this "person" <term>Purusha<gloss>The inner conscious being or cosmic man</gloss></term> desiring to sleep reaches this very life-breath. This state of profound serenity <term>Samprasada<gloss>The state of deep, dreamless sleep where the soul is said to be in its purest form</gloss></term> is this life-breath; this "person" is golden. || 4 ||

This is the breath of breath itself; it is every being, high and low. He reaches this life-breath, breath by breath. Om. Just as a bird <term>Shakuni<gloss>A bird, often used as a metaphor for the restless mind or soul</gloss></term> tied by a string flies in every direction and, finding no other resting place, takes refuge in the life-breath alone. <note>original: "prāṇamevopāśrayate"</note> Life-breath is the resting place here; as it is said: "Just as a bird is tied by a string."

### Now, these are the scriptures regarding dreams

Therefore, it is from this [breath] that one is asleep in a dream, or even in death. Now, these channels <term>Nadis<gloss>Energy channels in the subtle body through which life-force flows</gloss></term> of his, with the life-breath enclosed, become as if they are not sleeping. For he is asleep during the time of dreaming. || 40 || Now these channels are as if sleeping, or [it is like] death.

The space of the heart is the first life-breath to be gathered; the channels of that life-breath become covered. Now, these channels of his are covered, appearing as if asleep, or it is like death. Just as one might cover his channels with barley-meal, or a vessel of clarified butter, or a cow-pen, or with the hand.

**Salutation to the Great God (Mahadeva)** || || Now the mind becomes asleep; it is like a bird tied by a string. This is the abode of the vital breaths; it is like a bird tied by a string. Now, this is indeed the life-breath; it is called the life-breath. At this time, all the vital winds <term>Maruts<gloss>Here referring to the various sub-functions of the vital breath or Prana</gloss></term> reach this state.

"Kindler" <note>original: "Heindho" — a rare technical term likely referring to the illuminating aspect of the vital force</note> is the name of this life-breath, or it is simply called "this life-breath." At this time, all the vital winds are withdrawn. "Kindler" is the name of that life-breath. It is this life-breath; it is sleep or death. For the essence is sleep or death. The dreaming state is death, or like death. Now, the channels are in a state of difficulty, just like a cow-pen...

### Regarding the Characteristics of the Hand Gestures

### The Procedure for the Water Offering

Now, the **Procedure for the Water Offering** <term>Arghya-vidhi<gloss>The formal method of preparing and presenting a water offering to a deity</gloss></term>. First, one should place the ritual vessel upon its designated seat or pedestal.

Having consecrated the vessel with the **Root Mantra** <term>Mula-mantra<gloss>The primary mantra of the deity being worshipped, in this case, "Om Namah Shivaya"</gloss></term>, one should worship it with sandalwood paste, flowers, and consecrated grains. After chanting the mantra eight times, one should gaze upon the vessel while performing the Conch gesture. Thus is the ritual offering empowered. Then, one performs the placement of the six limbs upon one's own body:
- Obeisance to the heart.
- *Swaha* to the head.

*Vashat* to the crest. *Hum* to the armor. *Vaushat* to the three eyes. *Phat* to the weapon. Having performed this placement, one should then meditate upon the **Supreme Lord** <note>original: *Parameshwara*, referring to Shiva</note> and begin the worship. This completes the Consecration of the Six Limbs.

<meta>The previous page concluded a section on Shaiva ritual hand gestures (Mudras). This page shifts focus toward the daily obligatory rites known as Sandhya, drawing from legal and ethical texts (Dharmashastras). It emphasizes that these rituals are not just for students but are mandatory for householders to maintain their ritual purity and spiritual standing.</meta>

<margin>Dha 6</margin>

# Now, regarding the practices of celibate studentship in the first stage of life and the twilight rituals; a further distinction among them is mentioned in the Law-books.

### The performance of twilight rituals for the householder.

The householder <term>Grihastha<gloss>The second stage of life in the Hindu ashrama system, characterized by marriage, family, and social duties</gloss></term>, in particular, should worship at the twilights. One should always worship at evening and morning with a focused mind.

A Brahmin <note>original: *Brahmana*</note>, specifically, who does not worship at the twilight, is like a laborer <note>original: *Shudra*. In this ritual context, it implies one who lacks the sacred thread initiation and is therefore ineligible for Vedic rites</note>; he remains impure in all his actions day and night. There is no reward for him in this world, nor in the world beyond. One who lacks the twilight ritual is perpetually impure and unfit for any sacred acts.

Whatever he may perform, he shall not share in its fruits. However, those Brahmins who are devoted to their duty and worship at the twilight go to the supreme place where the Sun-god <note>original: *Divakara*, literally "The Day-maker"</note> dwells. One should recite the **Gayatri** <term>Gayatri<gloss>A highly revered Vedic mantra dedicated to Savitar, the solar deity, considered the "Mother of the Vedas"</gloss></term> properly according to the rule, along with the "Head" mantra <note>original: *shirasa sarddham*. This refers to a specific auxiliary portion of the mantra used in advanced practice</note>.

Such a wise man is released from sins, just as a lotus leaf is untouched by water. Regarding the three Vedas, the practices of celibacy, and the twilight rituals, there is another distinction. One should worship the morning twilight while the stars are still visible, according to the rule. One should worship the evening twilight while the sun is still present in the solar disk.

At the time of the midday sun, being focused on both twilights, one should recite the **Savitri** <note>Another name for the Gayatri mantra</note> while concentrated during the twilight. Having sipped water <term>Achamana<gloss>A ritual purification involving sipping small amounts of water from the palm</gloss></term> and becoming pure, one should daily perform the "Sacrifice to the Absolute" <term>Brahma-yajna<gloss>The daily study or recitation of the Vedas, considered one of the five great sacrifices of a householder</gloss></term>. One should recite the Gayatri, the Mother of the Vedas, to the best of one's ability.

Having performed three rounds of **breath control** <term>Pranayama<gloss>The practice of regulating the breath to steady the mind and purify the subtle body before prayer</gloss></term>, we then worship at the twilight.

This completes the **Rules for Twilight Rituals** <term>Sandhya-vidhi<gloss>The prescribed procedure for the junction-point prayers performed at dawn, noon, and dusk</gloss></term>.

<summary>This page outlines the mandatory nature of Sandhya (twilight rituals) for Brahmins, particularly householders, warning that neglect of these rites leads to ritual impurity and the loss of spiritual merit. It provides specific timing for the morning and evening prayers and emphasizes the importance of the Gayatri mantra and breath control.</summary>

<keywords>Sandhya, Gayatri mantra, Householder duties, Dharmashastra, ritual purity, Pranayama, Brahma-yajna, morning and evening rites</keywords>

# The Dialogue of the Great Sage Samvatsara

Om! Salutations to the Perfectly Awakened One <note>original: *Sambuddha*. This is a title for the Buddha, indicating the text likely originates from a Newar Buddhist context in Nepal, even while discussing Puranic themes.</note>.

Having heard this, the best of sages <note>original: *munipungavah*</note>, purified of their sins, looked at them again with wonder in their hearts. Then, what did the sage say?

"O lords of sages, what is that reality literally called 'The Year' <term>Samvatsara<gloss>Literally "The Year," here personified as a great sage or a divine principle</gloss></term>? Tell me all of this according to the Dharma."

*I perpetually bow to the two lotus feet of that Leader of the World, which are illuminated by the web of moonbeams from the crowns of the gods and demons.* [1]

*That scripture which I did not know, that sacred lore I did not know—all that has been understood by me through your grace, O observers of virtuous vows.*

*Whatever was given, whatever was offered in the sacred fire, whatever was recited, and whatever was accumulated—all that has become fruitful through your grace, O observers of virtuous vows.*

Nevertheless, I ask you, dear father, and though I have heard it before, I speak to you: Tell me truly, what happened during the crushing conflict between the gods and demons? 

Then, having been questioned, the high-souled sage, skilled in all matters, the greatly radiant **Samvatsara**, the lord of sages, replied:

"Listen, all you sages, to what you have asked, O observers of virtuous vows. Long ago, in the war between the gods and demons, when the power of the demons <note>original: *Daitya*</note> was increasing, the gods <note>original: *Vibudha*, literally "the wise ones"</note>, oppressed by the attacking demons, went for refuge."

"They went to **Narayana** <note>A name for Vishnu</note>, the high-souled one, who is saluted by all the gods. Then the Lord **Vishnu**, the soul of all, whose banner bears the eagle-man **Garuda**, spoke sweet words for the destruction of the demons:"

<unclear>“Do not fear, all you gods! I have granted you great fearlessness <note>original: *mahabhayam*; literally "great fear," but in this context of protection, it is almost certainly a scribal error for *abhayam* (fearlessness)</note>. Today I shall slay those demons, who are like thorns in the side of the gods.”</unclear>

## Now Begins the First Chapter

**The Blessed Lord spoke:**
What is that which I have previously said, about which you ask me, O son of Pandu <note>original: *Pandava*, referring to Arjuna</note>? None of those things I remembered in the past are known to me now. Tell me, O greatly wise one, what I have never heard and never seen, if such a thing is possible for you here.

**Arjuna spoke:**
O Blessed Lord, Knower of all duties, Expert in all scriptures: What is merit and what is demerit? What is knowledge, and what is the supreme knowledge? What is a place of pilgrimage <term>Tirtha<gloss>Literally a "ford" or crossing place; a holy site that allows a devotee to cross over from the mundane to the divine</gloss></term>? What is the "Unknown"? What is austerity, and what is self-restraint? What is charity, and what is this fire-offering? What is it that must be sacrificed at this time?

**Shri Krishna spoke:**
Listen, O son of Pandu, I shall tell you that which is auspicious and more secret than the most secret. By the mere hearing of this, one becomes liberated in a single moment. Not by charity, nor by the practice of austerities, nor by sacrifices, nor by self-restraints; <image-desc>circular hole for binding cord</image-desc> nor even by bathing in holy waters is liberation achieved for one's lineage.

That alone is "Knowledge and Realization" <term>Jnana-Vijnana<gloss>Jnana refers to theoretical or scriptural knowledge, while Vijnana refers to personal, experiential realization of that truth</gloss></term> through which the supreme state is attained.

As fire is seen by the wise to be latent within all pieces of wood, so does the one **Janardana** <note>original: *Janārdana*, a name for Krishna meaning "Agitator of Men" or "He who is sought by the people"</note> reside within all bodies. Just as clarified butter <term>Ghee<gloss>Purified butter used in rituals and cooking, representing the essence extracted from milk</gloss></term> is hidden within milk, so is Janardana hidden in the body. When one has churned [the body through practice], the Soul shines forth.

As there is oil within sesame seeds and honey within flowers, and as sight is perceived when the eye and the object meet, so is the Universal Soul of all embodied beings perceived. As the fragrance within a flower is not seen by the eye but is grasped through the sense of smell, so is the Soul understood.

As the fire within wood is seen only through the friction of churning, so the God dwelling within the body is seen only through constant practice. By realizing Him, the supreme state is obtained. That which knows the body—He is the embodied soul, He is Janardana. He who sees the Soul as both beyond qualities <term>Nirguna<gloss>The Absolute without attributes or form</gloss></term> and yet possessing qualities <term>Gunatmaka<gloss>The Divine manifested through material qualities like light, passion, or stability</gloss></term>, he alone truly sees God; He is God, the Supreme Lord.

That [vision] is called supreme purity; that is proclaimed as wisdom. That is compassion, that is supreme peace, and that is considered the highest liberation. That is forgiveness and that is contentment; He is God, the Supreme Lord. This is the supreme secret that I have spoken to you before—that which was told by the gods of old but never revealed to anyone else.

<margin>46</margin>

<summary>This page opens the 'Jñāna Vijñāna' dialogue where Krishna explains to Arjuna that supreme liberation is found not through external rituals or pilgrimages, but through the realization of the Divine residing within the body.</summary>
<keywords>Jnana Vijnana, Krishna, Arjuna, Janardana, Moksha, Atman, Yoga, Internal Fire, Nirguna, Meditation</keywords>

->**Salutations to the All-Knowing One!**<-
<note>original: oṃ namaḥ sarvajñāya; a standard opening for Buddhist texts, where "Sarvajna" refers to the Buddha as the omniscient one.</note>

He who, desiring the welfare of the entire world, has cast off the illusions of delusion, and stands as the leader among those who understand the **ultimate truth** <gloss>original: paramārtha; the highest reality that lies beyond mere appearances</gloss> and are drawing near to **awakening** <gloss>original: bodhi; the state of enlightenment or spiritual completion</gloss>—

He extends welfare to those great beings who are free from the defects arising from the side of **afflictive emotions** <gloss>original: saṃkleśa; the mental impurities such as greed, hatred, and delusion that bind one to suffering</gloss>, in order that they may attain complete and perfect awakening. || 1 ||

<meta>This page indicates a major shift in the manuscript's content. While the previous page was part of a Buddhist Prajnaparamita commentary, this page belongs to a Vaishnava Vedantic text. The vocabulary and themes focus on the deity Vishnu as the Supreme Person and the attainment of liberation through spiritual knowledge. <margin>48</margin>
...goes upward with me; he approaches that **Divine Person**, the **Supreme Person**. || 70 || He who, knowing thus, knows the imperishable Divine Person, knows the imperishable Truth; having attained Him, **he does not return** <gloss>original: na nivarttate; a technical term meaning the soul is no longer reborn into the cycle of suffering</gloss>, he does not return. || 71 || That by which one knows the imperishable Person, [the teacher] spoke that **Knowledge of Brahman** <gloss>original: brahmavidyā; the ultimate spiritual science of reality</gloss> in its true essence. || 72 ||

...this individual, having reached his allotted lifespan, then becoming detached, **desireless** <gloss>original: niṣkāma; acting without attachment to results</gloss>, and a **seeker of liberation** <gloss>original: mumukṣu</gloss>, contemplated the Self alone; he also became Brahman <note>The ultimate, impersonal reality</note>, just as it was for the ancient sages and for men. || 73 ||

...the heavens, the earth, the moon, the wind, and the sky; intention, reflection, and the imperishable; the suns—all this is the **manifestation of Vishnu** <gloss>original: viṣṇorvibhūti; the divine glory or immanent power of God throughout the universe</gloss>. || 74 ||

...[the ancestors]; Manu, Brahma, and Saraswati; the sacred cow, Gauri, the **Sadhyas** <note>A class of celestial beings or minor deities appearing in Vedic hymns</note>, Kadru, the Shvasrayas, Agni, Vishnu, and the suns—all these are the manifestation of Vishnu. || 75 ||

...may they [behold]. **The wise always see that highest step of Vishnu, like an eye stretched across the sky.** <note>original: tad viṣṇoḥ paramaṃ padaṃ sadā paśyanti sūrayaḥ; a famous quotation from the Rig Veda (1.22.20) describing the highest state of consciousness or the highest abode of God.</note> The inspired singers, ever-watchful and praising, kindle that which is the highest step of Vishnu. || 76 || He is this Vishnu: infinite, undecaying, and eternal.

...the sacrificers. Those who perform worship of the **Supreme Self** <gloss>original: paramātman</gloss> in whatever manner, attain **union** <gloss>original: sayuja</gloss>, the **same divine world** <gloss>original: sālokya</gloss>, **proximity** <gloss>original: sāmīpya</gloss>, or **equal power** <gloss>original: sāṣṭrya</gloss> with Him; they do not return. || 77 || And those others who reach the goal through the path of the learned, they too do not return. || 78 ||

...with a focused mind. Or rather, even by **chanting the Name** <gloss>original: nāma-saṃkīrtana</gloss>, one immediately goes to the state of no-return. || 6 || 

->**Thus ends the Fourth Instruction in the Knowledge of Brahman according to Vishnu.**<-

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on Vaishnava theology, explaining that all elements of the universe are manifestations of Vishnu and that through his knowledge or the chanting of his name, a seeker achieves liberation from rebirth. It cites the Rig Veda to validate the "highest step" of Vishnu as the ultimate goal of the wise.</summary>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>Following the previous page which contained a faded invocation and opening of a new section, this page begins a clear and detailed technical discussion on the classification of sounds (ruta and āhārya) and their direct correspondence to the seven musical notes (svaras) of the Indian musical scale.</meta>

<gloss>Fire 5</gloss> <gloss>Sim 6</gloss> <note>original: vahni 5, sima 6; these likely refer to specific numbering systems or mnemonic tags used by the scribe</note>
He speaks of the specific types of natural sounds. **Sutra 8.** I am stating the names of these six types of sounds categorized by their breadth:
1. Broad <note>original: pṛthu</note>
2. Broader <note>original: pṛthutara</note>
3. Broadest <note>original: pṛthutama</note>
1. Small <note>original: alpa</note>
2. Smaller <note>original: alpatara</note>
3. Smallest <note>original: alpatama</note>

In this context, "Broad" refers first to a large sound. "Broader" indicates a greater intensity than that, and "Broadest" indicates the highest intensity. In the same way, the terms "Small," "Smaller," and "Smallest" are to be understood. Now, he assigns the names of these six qualities in their proper order. **Sutra 9.** From these six qualities, the seven <term>Svaras<gloss>musical notes of the scale</gloss></term> arise in sequence. They are as follows:

1. From the **Broad** quality comes the note <term>Shadja<gloss>the first note, equivalent to "Do"</gloss></term>.
2. From the **Broader** quality comes the note <term>Rishabha<gloss>the second note, equivalent to "Re"</gloss></term>.
3. From the **Broadest** quality comes the note <term>Gandhara<gloss>the third note, equivalent to "Mi"</gloss></term>.
<margin>Vr 34</margin> 4. From the **Medium** quality comes the note <term>Madhyama<gloss>the fourth note, equivalent to "Fa"</gloss></term>.
5. From the **Smallest** quality comes the note <term>Panchama<gloss>the fifth note, equivalent to "Sol"</gloss></term>.
6. From the **Smaller** quality comes the note <term>Dhaivata<gloss>the sixth note, equivalent to "La"</gloss></term>.
7. From the **Small** quality comes the note <term>Nishada<gloss>the seventh note, equivalent to "Ti"</gloss></term>.

These specific types of natural sounds and the seven musical notes should be understood according to their respective numerical order. **Sutra 10.** This sutra is easy to understand. The definition of sound is stated to be of two kinds: the "natural cries" mentioned previously and "constructed sounds" <note>original: āhārya; literally "brought forth" or "artificial" sounds produced by symbols or instruments</note>. This is the remaining classification. **Sutra 11.** The sound that arises from symbols like the *Svasti* and others is called "constructed sound." It is of seven types. They are as follows:

1. <term>Svasti<gloss>the auspicious cross or well-being symbol</gloss></term>
2. <term>Pravardhamana<gloss>the symbol of prosperity or "the increasing one"</gloss></term>
3. <term>Shrivatsa<gloss>the "beloved of Shri," an endless knot symbol</gloss></term>
4. <term>Nandyavarta<gloss>a complex auspicious diagram or "turning of joy"</gloss></term>
5. <term>Kalasha<gloss>the sacred water pot or urn</gloss></term>
6. <term>Matsya<gloss>the fish symbol</gloss></term>
7. <term>Darpana<gloss>the mirror</gloss></term>

Sound is produced through these seven modes. **Sutra 12.** The definition of sound is completed in seven ways. **Sutra 13.** This sutra is easy to understand. From these types of sounds like the *Svasti*, the seven musical notes arise in sequence. They are as follows:

1. The note for the **Svasti** symbol is **Shadja**.
2. For the **Pravardhamana** symbol, it is **Rishabha**.
3. For the **Shrivatsa** symbol, it is **Gandhara**.
4. For the **Nandyavarta** symbol, it is **Madhyama**.
5. For the **Kalasha** symbol, it is **Panchama**.
6. For the **Matsya** symbol, it is **Dhaivata**.
7. For the **Darpana** symbol, it is **Nishada**.

In this way, the specific varieties of musical notes are to be considered. **Sutra 14.** He defines the nature of sound itself. **Sutra 15.** Sound is accomplished through eight notes, beginning with the "distinct" and others. These sounds should be understood as having soft and sweet tones. They are of three types...
<gloss>Ga 9</gloss> <gloss>Ma 10</gloss> <gloss>Pa 11</gloss> <gloss>Dha 12</gloss> <gloss>Ni 13</gloss> <note>these glosses correspond to the shorthand for the notes Gandhara, Madhyama, Panchama, Dhaivata, and Nishada</note>

<summary>This page outlines a system of musical theory where the seven notes of the scale (Svaras) are mapped onto two sets of categories: six physical sound qualities (ranging from Broad to Small) and seven auspicious symbolic forms (Aharya).</summary>
<keywords>Svara, musical notes, musicology, Shadja, Rishabha, Gandhara, Madhyama, Panchama, Dhaivata, Nishada, Ruta, Aharya, auspicious symbols</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed musical theory and the classification of sounds into notes like Shadja and Rishabha. However, this page marks a significant shift in subject matter, transitioning into a philosophical commentary on the Bhagavad Gita, specifically concluding Chapter 4 and beginning Chapter 5. This suggests the manuscript may be a compendium of various instructional texts.</meta>

तस्मात्प्रागुक्तं युक्तं एवा ॥ तथा च स्मृतिः ॥ **संशयात्मविनष्टस्य न लोक नापर न सुखं ॥** न हि संशयात्मनः सुखं संभवति ॥ अत्र च वक्ष्यमाणत्वात् ॥ इहलोके परलोके

Therefore, what was said previously is indeed correct. And so says the <term>Smriti<gloss>sacred tradition/scripture</gloss></term>: **"For the doubting soul, there is destruction; neither this world, nor the next, nor happiness exists for him."** <note>original: saṃśayātmavinaṣṭasya...; Bhagavad Gita 4.40</note> Indeed, happiness is not possible for one possessed by doubt. As will be explained here, this applies to both this world and the world beyond.

आह ॥ **संन्यासयोगेन कर्माणि ज्ञानसंछिन्नसंशयं ॥ आत्मानं न कर्माणि निबध्नंति धनंजय ॥** संन्यासेति ॥ योगेन परमार्थदर्शनलक्षणेन कर्माणि संन्यस्तव्यः ॥

He says: **"O Winner of Wealth <note>original: Dhananjaya; an epithet for Arjuna</note>, actions do not bind one who has renounced actions through Yoga, and whose doubts are rent asunder by knowledge."** <note>original: sannyāsayogena karmāṇi...; Bhagavad Gita 4.41</note> Regarding "renunciation": actions are to be renounced through Yoga, which is characterized by the vision of the Supreme Truth.

संशयो न भवति ॥ तथा चोक्तं ॥ **मयि सर्वाणि कर्माणि संन्यस्याध्यात्मचेतसा ॥ निराशीर्निर्ममो भूत्वा युध्यस्व विगतज्वरः ॥** तथा च ज्ञानसंछिन्नसंशयं ॥ ज्ञानेन आत्म-

Doubt does not arise for such a person. Thus it has been said: **"Renouncing all actions in Me with a spiritual mind, free from desire and selfishness, fight, delivered from your feverishness."** <note>original: mayi sarvāṇi karmāṇi...; Bhagavad Gita 3.30</note> Likewise, one "whose doubts are rent asunder by knowledge" means one whose doubt is cut away by the vision of the Self through knowledge.

**तस्मादज्ञानसंभूतं हृत्स्थं ज्ञानअसिनात्मनः ॥ छित्वैनं संशयं योगमातिष्ठोतिष्ठ भारत ॥** तस्मादिति ॥ अज्ञानादविवेकतः संभूतं जातमधुना हृत्स्थं हृदि स्थितं संशयं ॥

**"Therefore, with the sword of knowledge of the Self, cut away this doubt born of ignorance residing in your heart; take refuge in Yoga and stand up, O Descendant of Bharata <note>original: Bhārata; Arjuna</note>!"** <note>original: tasmādajñānasambhūtaṃ...; Bhagavad Gita 4.42</note> "Therefore" means: this doubt which has arisen from ignorance or a lack of discrimination and is now situated in the heart...

**इति श्रीमद्भगवद्गीतासूपनिषत्सु ब्रह्मविद्यायां योगशास्त्रे श्रीकृष्णार्जुनसंवादे ज्ञानकर्मसंन्यासयोगो नाम चतुर्थोऽध्यायः ॥ ४ ॥ ॥ अथ पञ्चमोऽध्यायः ॥ अर्जुन उवाच ॥**

**Thus ends the fourth chapter, titled "The Yoga of Knowledge, Action, and Renunciation," in the Upanishads of the glorious Bhagavad Gita, in the science of Brahman, in the scripture of Yoga, in the dialogue between Lord Krishna and Arjuna. (4). Now begins the Fifth Chapter. Arjuna spoke:**

कर्मणां पुनश्च योगं अनुष्ठानं शंससि प्रशंससि ॥ अतः व्यामिश्रवाक्यत्वात् ममाविज्ञातो विवेकः ॥ यदेतयोः संन्यासकर्मयोगयोः प्रशस्यतरं श्रेष्ठं यदप्येकं तत् ममोपदेष्टुं-

...and again You praise the performance of <term>Yoga<gloss>Karma Yoga, or the path of action</gloss></term> regarding these actions. Therefore, because of these seemingly conflicting statements, my discrimination is confused. Of these two—the renunciation of action and the yoga of action—tell me for certain which one is better and superior.

योगयोः ॥ यद्यपि संन्यासः कर्मयोगश्च एकफलौ तथापि अर्जुनस्य मंदबुद्धित्वात् पुनर्पृच्छति ॥ किंचात्र संन्यासशब्दे न स्वरूपतः कर्मत्याग उच्यते ॥ किंतु ब्रह्मार्पण-

...of the two yogas. Even though renunciation and the yoga of action lead to the same result, Arjuna asks again because of his dull intellect. Furthermore, the word "renunciation" <note>original: sannyāsa</note> here does not refer to the literal abandonment of the form of action, but rather to the offering of action to <term>Brahman<gloss>the Ultimate Reality or Divine</gloss></term>...

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A circular string hole is positioned in the gutter between the two text columns, interrupting the flow of the third line.</image-desc>

सुखं नास्ति ॥ किंतु अद्यैव कुत इति ॥ संशयात्मनः सर्वेप्यर्थेषु संशयोपपत्तेः ॥ तस्मात्संशयो न कर्तव्य इत्यभिप्रायः ॥ किमयं संशयो दुस्त्यजः न ॥ कथमित्यत

...there is no happiness. But why even today? Because for a doubting soul, doubt arises in all matters. Therefore, the intention is that doubt should not be harbored. Is this doubt impossible to abandon? No. "How so?" To answer this:

यो हि विहितान्येव कर्माणि करोति न तु ज्ञानेनात्मन्यारोपयति ॥ तस्य न संशयः ॥ यस्य च भगवद्भक्त्या सर्वकर्माणि भगवति समर्पितानि तस्य च

He who performs only the prescribed actions but does not superimpose them upon the Self through knowledge—he has no doubt. And for him whose every action is surrendered to the Lord through devotion to the Lord, for him also...

दर्शनलक्षणेन संछिन्नः संशयो यस्य स तं ॥ आत्मवंतं अप्रमत्तं ॥ न कर्माणि निबध्नंति बंधहेतवो न भवंति ॥ फलदानेन धनंजय ॥ यत एतदेवं

...he whose doubt has been cut by that which is characterized by the vision [of truth]. One who possesses the Self is one who is vigilant. Actions do not bind such a one; they do not become the cause of bondage by producing results, O Winner of Wealth. Since this is so:

केन ज्ञानअसिना ॥ आत्मनः स्वस्य आत्मविषयत्वात्स एव असिः तेन छित्वा एनं मोहकरं संशयं ॥ योगं कर्मातिष्ठ कुरु ॥ इदानीं युद्धाय उत्तिष्ठ भारत ॥

By what [is it cut]? By the "sword of knowledge." Because it concerns the Self of one's own being, that [knowledge] itself is the sword. Having cut this deluding doubt with it, take up Yoga—that is, perform action. Now, stand up for battle, O Descendant of Bharata!

**संन्यासं कर्मणां कृष्ण पुनर्योगं च शंससि ॥ यच्छ्रेय एतयोरेकं तन्मे ब्रूहि सुनिश्चितं ॥ १ ॥** संन्यासमिति ॥ संन्यासं परित्यागं कर्मणां शास्त्राविहितानां युद्धादीनां

**"O Krishna, You praise the renunciation of actions and then again You praise Yoga. Tell me decisively which one of these two is the better path." (1)** <note>original: sannyāsaṃ karmaṇāṃ kṛṣṇa...; Bhagavad Gita 5.1</note> Regarding "Renunciation": Renunciation means the abandonment of actions, even those like battle which are prescribed by scripture.

योग्यं तन्मे ब्रूहि सुनिश्चितं ॥ ननु व्यामिश्रमिवाक्येति पूर्वमेवोक्तं पुनश्च किमर्थं पृच्छति ॥ सत्यं ॥ तत्र पूर्वत्र ज्ञानकर्मणोः विरोध उक्तः ॥ अत्र तु संन्यासकर्म

Tell me which is most suitable. Now, it was already said previously that "your speech is confusing," so why does he ask again? True; but previously the opposition was stated between knowledge and action. Here, however, the comparison is between renunciation and the performance of action...

बुद्ध्या क्रियमाणं कर्मैव संन्यासशब्देनोच्यते ॥ तत्र च कस्य श्रेयस्त्वमिति पृच्छति ॥ एवं पृष्टो भगवानुवाच ॥ **श्रीभगवानुवाच ॥ संन्यासः कर्मयोगश्च निःश्रेयसकरावुभौ ॥**

...action performed with a [spiritual] intellect is what is referred to by the word "renunciation." He asks which of these possesses superior excellence. Being asked thus, the Blessed Lord spoke: **"The Blessed Lord said: Both renunciation and the Yoga of action lead to the supreme good."** <note>original: śrībhagavānuvāca...; Bhagavad Gita 5.2</note>

<summary>This page concludes the commentary on Chapter 4 of the Bhagavad Gita, emphasizing the destruction of doubt through the "sword of knowledge," and opens Chapter 5 where Arjuna asks Krishna to clarify the superiority between the renunciation of action and the yoga of selfless action.</summary>

# Page 52

**Om, salutations to Shiva.** <note>original: "oṃ namaḥ śivāya"; a traditional invocation to the deity Shiva, marking the auspicious beginning of the transcription.</note> That which is not expressed by speech, but that by which speech is expressed— ॥ 3 ॥

**Know that alone to be the <term>Brahman</term>** <note>original: "brahma"; the ultimate, infinite reality or the divine ground of all existence in Hindu philosophy.</note>, and not this which people worship here. <note>Refers to the distinction between the formless, absolute reality and the localized deities or objects of ritual worship.</note> ॥ 4 ॥

That which one does not think with the mind, but they say the mind is thought by That— ॥ 5 ॥

**Know that alone to be the Brahman**, and not this which people worship here. ॥ 6 ॥

That which one does not see with the eye, but by which one sees the eyes— ॥ 7 ॥

**Know that alone to be the Brahman**, and not this which people worship here. ॥ 8 ॥

That which one does not hear with the ear, but by which this hearing is heard— ॥ 9 ॥

**Know that alone to be the Brahman**, and not this which people worship here. ॥ 10 ॥

**Know that alone to be the Brahman**, and not this which people worship here. ॥ 12 ॥

**Thus ends the First Section.** ॥ 1 ॥

If you think, "I know it well," then you surely know only a little of the form of Brahman. Whether it is what you are, or what is among the gods, it is still to be sought by you. I think [it is] now known to you. ॥ 1 ॥

I do not think, "I know it well," nor do I know that I do not know it. He among us who knows That, knows It; and he does not know that he does not know It. <note>This verse expresses the paradoxical nature of spiritual realization: it is not an object of intellectual knowledge that can be "possessed."</note> ॥ 2 ॥

To whom It is unknown, to him It is known; to whom It is known, he does not know It. It is unknown to those who [claim to] know It; It is known to those who do not [claim to] know It. ॥ 3 ॥

When It is known through every state of consciousness, It is truly known, for then one attains immortality. Through the <term>Self</term> <note>original: "ātmanā"; the individual soul or essence which is identical with the universal Brahman.</note> one attains strength; through <term>Wisdom</term> <note>original: "vidyayā"; spiritual knowledge or higher learning.</note> one attains immortality. ॥ 4 ॥

If one knows It here [in this life], then there is truth; if one does not know It here, there is great destruction. Having perceived It in every being, the wise, departing from this world, [become immortal].

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>The current page is numbered 43, while the previous page provided for continuity was numbered 45. This may indicate a non-linear arrangement in the manuscript or a numbering error by the original scribe. This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of consciousness, shifting focus from the specific Buddhist deities mentioned previously to the mechanics of the intellect (buddhi) and the three states of consciousness (waking, dreaming, and deep sleep).</meta>

<margin>8</margin> <margin>43</margin>

**In the nature of consciousness indeed,** attributes and other qualities are certainly grasped by the <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "dhiyā"; refers to the faculty of understanding or cognitive thought.</note>. Even for one who is asleep, that state of sleep is grasped only by that same intellect. Just as in a dream, so it is in the intellect: the state of "deep sleep" is merely imagined within <term>Brahman</term> <note>original: "brahmaṇi"; the ultimate, infinite reality or absolute consciousness.</note>. From the perspective of <term>pure awareness</term> <note>original: "saṃvit"; the foundational consciousness that precedes thought.</note>, attributes and such are never found to be distinct or separate from it.

**Attributes and such by the intellect** should always be understood through the "intellect of attributes." To whom do these attributes belong? Or for whom is this "knowledge" spoken? For one in the state of deep sleep, attributes certainly do not exist; this is seen to be the case in truth. Just as it is in the intellect, so is the Absolute; the state of sleep is perceived within the body. By the intellect indeed

**everything is grasped;** attributes and such are considered to be products of the intellect. All that is called "sleep" is through the intellect, and the "waking state" is likewise perceived through the intellect. Just as without the intellect no one exists during the time of a dream, so without the intellect, the state of deep sleep would never be perceived. That without which the 

**Absolute does not** appear as anything at all—how does that intellect, which is itself nothing, perceive the Absolute? Therefore, without the intellect, the state of deep sleep would never be seen. The triad of <term>waking</term> <note>original: "jāgrat"</note>, <term>dreaming</term> <note>original: "svapna"</note>, and <term>deep sleep</term> <note>original: "suṣupti"</note> is perceived entirely by the intellect. That sleep is therefore said to **arise from meditation and such** <note>original: "dhyānādi saṃbhavā"</note> through the intellect. 

Attributes and such would not exist without it; the intellect is what perceives them. For one who is asleep, attributes certainly do not exist; this is seen in truth. Everything from meditation to attributes is grasped by the intellect. Just as without the intellect the waking and dream states are not seen, so

**without the intellect,** the state of deep sleep would never be perceived. Deep sleep is the cessation of meditation and other mental activities, transcending the waking and dream states. For one who is asleep, attributes certainly do not exist; this is seen in truth. As it is in the intellect, so it is in the Absolute; this is always seen in pure awareness. For the one whose <term>nature is consciousness</term> <note>original: "cid-rūpa"</note>, attributes and such are not 

**seen as meditation and such** by the intellect. Therefore, everything is known by the intellect—the state of sleep as well as the waking state. Indeed, without the intellect, nothing—neither sleep nor waking—is perceived. For one who is asleep, attributes certainly do not exist; this is seen in truth. Just as without the intellect, the waking state would never be seen.

<summary>This page argues that the three states of consciousness (waking, dreaming, and deep sleep) and all philosophical attributes are constructions of the intellect (buddhi). It concludes that even the Absolute (Brahman) is only perceived or imagined through the medium of the intellect, and without this cognitive faculty, no states of being could be recognized.</summary>
<keywords>intellect, buddhi, consciousness, cid-rūpa, Brahman, deep sleep, suṣupti, waking state, jāgrat, dreaming, svapna, pure awareness, samvit, attributes, dharma</keywords>

<meta>This page marks a significant transition from the ritualistic Shaiva and Shakta mantras of the previous folio. It begins with a salutation to Narayana (a name for the Supreme Being associated with Vishnu) and proceeds to quote one of the most famous passages of the Upanishads: the Sandilya-vidya (The Wisdom of Sandilya). Interestingly, while the text quoted is from the Chandogya Upanishad, the closing signature attributes it to the Kaushitaki Brahmana Upanishad, suggesting this manuscript may be a compilation or reflects a specific regional scribal tradition.</meta>

**Salutations to Narayana.** <note>original: "Namo Nārāyaṇāya"; Narayana is the supreme universal spirit in Vaishnava tradition, often identified with the preservation of the universe.</note> ॥ **All this is indeed Brahman.** <note>original: "Sarvaṃ khalvidaṃ brahma"; one of the "Great Sayings" (Mahavakyas) of Indian philosophy, asserting that the ultimate reality and the universe are one.</note> **From That it is born, into That it is dissolved, and in That it breathes. One should meditate on It with a tranquil mind.** ॥ **Now, truly, a person is made of will.** <note>original: "kratuḥ"; translated here as 'will' or 'purpose,' it refers to the focused intention that shapes a person's character and destiny.</note> **According to the will a person has in this world, so does he become after departing from here. Therefore, let him form a will.** ॥

**He is formed of mind, whose body is the life-breath, whose form is light, whose resolve is true, whose nature is like space, from whom all works, all desires, all fragrances, and all tastes proceed; who encompasses all this, who is without speech and without concern.** ॥ **This is my Self within the heart, smaller than a grain of rice, smaller than a grain of barley, smaller than a mustard seed, smaller than a grain of millet, or even the kernel within a grain of millet.** <note>This famous passage uses diminishing physical objects to emphasize the subtle, infinitesimal nature of the soul (Atman).</note> ॥

**This is my Self within the heart, greater than the earth, greater than the atmosphere, greater than the sky, greater than all these worlds.** <note>In contrast to the previous line, this emphasizes the soul's infinite and all-encompassing nature.</note> ॥ **From whom all works, all desires, all fragrances, and all tastes proceed; who encompasses all this, who is without speech and without concern—this is my Self within the heart; this is Brahman.** <note>original: "Etad Brahma"; identifying the individual soul with the universal absolute.</note> **"When I depart from here, I shall attain Him"—he who has this firm faith and has no doubt [reaches that state]. Thus spoke Sandilya, yes, Sandilya.** <note>Sandilya is an ancient sage to whom this specific meditation on the nature of God and the soul is attributed.</note> ॥ **Thus ends the fourteenth section of the third chapter.** ॥ 3 ॥ 14 ॥

**Now, truly, for one who knows this and meditates on the Great Chant** <note>original: "Udgītha"; the liturgical chanting of the 'Om' syllable in Vedic sacrifice.</note> **as "I am indeed all this," whatever has been said [previously] becomes true for him. He who knows this [attains this state].** ॥ **Thus the Kaushitaki Brahmana Upanishad is completed.** <note>original: "Kauṣītaki-brāhmaṇopaniṣat"; despite the quote being from the Chandogya Upanishad, the scribe identifies this as the end of the Kaushitaki Upanishad.</note> ॥

<meta>The manuscript transitions from the previous page's rhythmic, semi-Prakrit devotional chanting into a formal Sanskrit Tantric text. The text shifts from general meditation to a specific ritual structure involving a deity, a seer, and the "Nyasa" ritual (the mental placement of divine energies on the body).</meta>

### Page 91

-> **Om. Salutations to Shiva.** <-

**Now, the Lord speaks:**

Listen, O Goddess <note>original: "devi"; referring to Mahadevi/Parvati, the interlocutor in most Tantric dialogues.</note>, I shall declare the unsurpassed path of accomplishment, by whose grace even a mortal becomes equal to Shiva in this world. I will reveal the supreme, auspicious **Armor of the Great Bhairava** <note>original: "Mahabhairava-kavacham"; a "kavacha" or "armor" is a protective hymn intended to be "worn" by the practitioner through recitation or as an amulet.</note>, which removes all sins, is meritorious, and bestows all spiritual powers.

Listen with a focused mind, O Goddess, as I explain its worship. I will tell you how one may obtain the fruits of worship even without a mantra, without formal offerings, without repetitive chanting, without meditation, without fire sacrifices, and without the ritual of placement. 

Even he who does not know the meaning of the mantras, or the secret hand gestures <note>term: Mudras <gloss>symbolic or ritual hand gestures used in Hindu and Buddhist ceremonies to channel energy</gloss></note>, or the methods of meditation, shall obtain the fruits of worship. Listen carefully, O Goddess, for I tell you this with great effort. It is more secret than the most secret, meritorious, subduing all beings, divine, containing all deities, and the supreme essence of all mantras. It contains the power of all sacred pilgrimages, all sacrifices, and all religious laws; it is the most auspicious of all auspicious things. 

Therefore, a wise person should wear this armor with all their effort. Even if one is not an expert in the systems of mantra and tantra, they will achieve success through this armor alone—of this there is no doubt. Whatever desire one contemplates, one surely attains it. Through this armor, O Queen of the Gods, the perfection of the mantra is born. He who recites this supreme, auspicious armor with devotion shall achieve the fulfillment of all aims—this is the truth, truly, without a doubt.

### The Liturgical Framework

**For this Armor of the Great Bhairava:**
*   **The Seer:** Brahma <note>The "Rishi" or Seer is the one who first "heard" or realized the hymn.</note>
*   **The Meter:** Anushtubh <note>A standard poetic meter consisting of four quarters of eight syllables each.</note>
*   **The Deity:** The Great Bhairava
*   **The Seed-Syllable:** *Hrim* <note>term: Bija <gloss>a "seed" syllable; a single-syllable sound that represents the essence of a deity's power</gloss></note>
*   **The Power:** *Hum*
*   **The Pin:** *Krim* <note>term: Kilaka <gloss>literally a "pin" or "bolt"; the syllable that unlocks the power of the mantra</gloss></note>
*   **The Purpose:** I undertake this recitation for the accomplishment of all my goals.

### The Ritual of Placement 
<note>term: Nyasa <gloss>a ritual where the practitioner touches specific parts of their body while reciting syllables to "place" the deity's power within themselves</gloss></note>

*   Salutations to **Brahma the Seer** — on the head.
*   Salutations to the **Anushtubh meter** — on the mouth.
*   Salutations to the **Great Bhairava deity** — in the heart.
*   Salutations to the seed-syllable ***Hrim*** — on the navel.
*   Salutations to the power ***Hum*** — on the private parts.
*   Salutations to the pin ***Krim*** — on the feet.

**Thus ends the Ritual of Placement.**

It provides the essential liturgical structure (seer, meter, deity) and the instructions for Nyasa, the ritual sanctification of the body.</summary>

The text moves from ritual protection to the logical and scriptural investigation into the nature of the Absolute.</meta>

Bra.

</margin>

### The Four Aphorisms

**Now begins the Four Sutras.** <note>original: "catuḥ-sūtrī"; these are the first four essential statements of the Brahma Sutras which summarize the essence of Hindu philosophy.</note>

**Sutra: Now, therefore, the inquiry into Brahman.** <note>original: "athāto brahmajijñāsā"; the opening of the Brahma Sutras, inviting the seeker to investigate the Ultimate Reality.</note>
In this context, the word **"Now"** signifies immediate succession <note>original: "ānantaryārthaḥ"; implying that after achieving certain preliminary spiritual qualities, one is ready for this inquiry.</note>.

**Sutra: [Brahman is that] from which the birth, etc., of this [universe proceed].** <note>original: "janmādyasya yataḥ"</note>
Of this world—which is manifested through name and form, is comprised of many actors and enjoyers, is the localized support of specific places, times, causes, actions, and results, and possesses a design that is inconceivable even to the mind—that all-knowing, all-powerful Cause from which its birth, sustenance, and dissolution <note>original: "janma sthiti-bhaṅgaṃ"</note> proceed, is **Brahman**. This is the completion of the sentence.

**Sutra: Because [Brahman] is the source of the scriptures.** <note>original: "śāstrayonitvāt"</note>
The source of this great scripture, the Rigveda and others, which is augmented by many branches of knowledge, which is like an omniscient being, and is endowed with the quality of omniscience <note>original: "sarvajña-guṇānvitasya"</note>—the cause of that scripture is the All-knowing One.

**Sutra: But that [Brahman is the cause] because of the harmony [of all Vedantic texts].** <note>original: "tat tu samanvayāt"</note>
That Brahman—all-knowing, all-powerful, and the cause of the world's origin, sustenance, and dissolution—is understood from the **Vedanta** <term>Vedanta <gloss>the 'end of the Vedas,' referring to the Upanishads and the philosophy of the Ultimate Soul</gloss></term> scriptures. How? Because of the **Harmony**. In all the Upanishads, the sentences are harmoniously directed toward this specific meaning as their primary purpose.

**Sutra: Because of "seeing" [willing], the "Un-worded" [matter] is not the cause.** <note>original: "īkṣater nāśabdam"</note>
Based on the scriptural hearing that "He is without sound, without touch, without form, and undecaying," it might be thought that Brahman is beyond words. However, the "Un-worded" <note>original: "aśabdaṃ"; here referring to 'Pradhana' or unconscious matter in the Sankhya school of philosophy</note> is not the cause. Why? Because of **"seeing"** <note>original: "īkṣateḥ"</note>. The scripture says: "He saw: Let me create the worlds." This act of "seeing" or "willing" is a characteristic only of a conscious being.

**Sutra: If it be said that [this "seeing"] is figurative, [we say] no, because of the word "Self".** <note>original: "gauṇaścen nātmaśabdāt"</note>
If it is argued that this "seeing" is merely metaphorical, that is incorrect. Why? Because of the use of the word **Self** <note>original: "ātma-śabdāt"</note>. The instruction given is: "He is the Self; That Thou Art."

**Sutra: Because liberation is taught for one who is devoted to That.** <note>original: "tanniṣṭhasya mokṣopadeśāt"</note>
Because liberation is promised to the one who is firmly established in That—the Self.

**Sutra: And because there is no statement that it should be discarded.** <note>original: "heyatvavacanācca"</note>
If this desired cause were the non-self <note>original: "anātmā"; referring to unconscious matter</note>, then the scripture would have instructed the seeker of liberation to discard it. But such a statement does not exist.

**Sutra: Because of the merging into the Self.** <note>original: "svāpyayāt"</note>
Because of the phrase "he becomes merged into his own Self."

**Sutra: Because of the uniformity of the view.** <note>original: "gatisāmānyāt"</note>
In all the Upanishads, the understanding regarding the conscious Cause is uniform.

**Sutra: And because it is stated [directly] in the Veda.** <note>original: "śrutatvācca"</note>
And because the All-knowing Supreme Lord is directly declared by the Veda itself to be the Cause.

<summary>This page presents the core logical arguments of the Brahma Sutras, establishing Brahman (the Absolute) as the conscious, all-knowing cause of the universe through the "Four Aphorisms" and a refutation of the theory that unconscious matter is the primary cause.</summary>
<keywords>Brahma Sutra, Brahman, Vedanta, Shankara, Aphorism, Omniscience, Self, Liberation, Upanishads</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>The previous folio concluded a philosophical investigation into the Brahma Sutras. This page marks a distinct shift in content and tone, transitioning from abstract Vedantic logic to a narrative and instructional discourse involving Vishvarupa. In Hindu tradition, Vishvarupa was a three-headed priest chosen by the gods to serve as their preceptor after they had offended Brihaspati. This section focuses on the ethics of the priesthood, the sanctity of the Guru (teacher), and the necessity of Vedic study.</meta>

### Vishvarupa’s Discourse on Duty and the Teacher

**Vishvarupa said:** Although the duty <note>original: "dharma"</note> you have instructed is the supreme good for a human being, for a dull-witted person like myself, my own understanding is quite base. **(1)** Indeed, my **priestly lineage** <note>original: "viprakula"; the family of Brahmins</note> chooses that which is praiseworthy for ascetic Brahmins; however, this office of the priesthood, when performed for those intoxicated by the pride of wealth, leads to great unrighteousness <note>original: "adharma"</note>, even though Brahmins are otherwise worthy of worship. **(2)**

Nevertheless, I shall not disobey your command. For teachers <note>original: "ācāryas"</note> are indeed the gods, and the **Guru** <note>spiritual teacher</note> embodies all the deities. **(3)** Therefore, act in such a way that the priestly lineage does not perish. <note>central hole</note> Let everyone hear this, my effort. <note>central hole</note> Let my dull mind also accept this removal of censure. As for what they say regarding the means, listen to that. Here, too, I must perform something.

**Vishvarupa said:** For disciples, the Guru is the direct manifestation of all **sacred chants** <note>original: "sarva-mantramayo guruḥ"; the teacher is the personification of the power of the mantras</note>. Without the practice of these chants <note>central hole</note>, the successful completion of any work does not occur. **(1)** 

Let experts always contemplate success through the sacred texts and the counselor. Just as an elephant made of wood or a deer made of skin [is merely a physical form without the essence of the animal], so too is a **Brahmin** <note>a member of the priestly and scholarly class</note> who has [only] studied; there is nothing more to him than that [if he lacks practice]. **(2)** <note>original: "yathā kāṣṭhamayo hastī"; a famous analogy from the Laws of Manu suggesting that a scholar without practice or realization is a mere shell.</note>

<note>central hole</note> Through service to the Guru and the gods, one achieves the goals of righteousness, desire, and worldly success. Therefore, for one who is devoted to service, the successful completion of work shall always manifest. **(3)**

One who understands the meaning of the **scriptures on duty** <note>original: "dharmaśāstra"</note> and remains anchored in the teachings—such a steadfast person shall attain the supreme goal. **(4)** This is indeed the instruction of the sacred chants as spoken by the **foremost of sages** <note>original: "munipuṅgavaiḥ"</note>.

<vocab>Vishvarupa, Dharma, Viprakula, Brahmana, Guru, Mantra, Dharmashastra, Munipungava</vocab>

<summary>Vishvarupa accepts the role of preceptor despite his reservations about the spiritual risks of serving wealthy patrons, emphasizing that a Guru's authority is divine and that theoretical study without service is hollow.</summary>
<keywords>Vishvarupa, priestly duty, Guru-disciple relationship, sacred mantras, Brahmin ethics, dharma, spiritual success</keywords>

<meta>The previous folio concluded Vishvarupa’s discourse on the sanctity of the teacher and the moral obligations of the priesthood. # The Lore of the Conch Shell and Ritual Gifts

A conch born with variegated colors <note>original: "chitra-varna"</note> is considered very pleasing and brings favor. However, a conch shell that possesses twenty-one distinct colors is said to bring inauspiciousness. According to the standards of measurement <note>original: "mana-pramana"</note>, the wise declare a conch of twenty-six fingers <note>term: anguli; a traditional unit of measurement roughly equal to a finger's width</note> to be standard. Indeed, three specific varieties are proclaimed.

Some are described by their right-handed spiral <term>dakshinavarta; rare shells that coil clockwise, considered extremely sacred in Hindu ritual</term>, while others possess a brilliance like that of [green] grass. A medium-sized conch is said to be the width of [certain] fingers. A measure of eighteen units is called the "best" <note>original: "uttama"</note>, while the ancients cited a measure of ten units as the smallest. Right-turning conches

are auspicious for primary religious rites and other works. Even a left-turning conch <note>original: "vama-shankha"</note> is declared auspicious for men, bestowing fearlessness. These varieties of conches have been described according to the scriptures <note>original: "shastra"</note> and the sacred traditions <note>original: "agama"</note>. <note>binding hole</note> Whoever hears this becomes most fortunate and is liberated from all sins. For kings,

having attained a kingdom, the earth becomes stable. Sons <note>binding hole</note> are obtained, and there is long life and the increase of spiritual merit. There shall be prosperity in wealth and grain, as well as every kind of good fortune. A wise man should worship the conch itself, just as he should worship knowledge <note>original: "vidya"</note>.

Ministers at all times, and kings especially, [should perform this worship]. It should be honored with the care given to a precious gem <note>original: "ratna"</note> when it is received for the sake of a daughter <note>referring to a dowry or wedding gift</note>. <note>binding hole</note> It should be known that at the time of the priest, the gifting of a daughter is similarly significant. A priest is primary in the pursuit of knowledge, just as kings are primary in battle.

In the rainy season, a merchant <term>vaishya; the social class of traders and farmers</term> is honored by the gift of grain. Through the act of giving, a worker <term>shudra</term> attains all human happiness. At the time of giving a daughter in marriage <note>original: "kanya-pradana"</note>, one should likewise present a conch shell. By doing so, the bride remains beautiful and her sons achieve great fame.

The first of the grain crops should be offered while bowing at the feet of the king. A twenty-fold increase in grain is proclaimed through such an act. Three hundred measures of sugar <note>original: "guda"; jaggery or unrefined sugar</note> and all crops are to be honored. One should bestow gold and tawny cows <note>original: "kapila ga"</note> upon a priest.

<summary>This page details the ritual significance, auspicious colors, and physical measurements of conch shells, concluding with the spiritual merits of gifting them and other goods to priests and kings.</summary>
<keywords>Shankha, conch shell, dakshinavarta, ritual measurements, kanya-pradana, auspiciousness, caste duties, Dana (giving)</keywords>

## The Installation on the Six Limbs
<term>shadanga-nyasa; a ritual where specific sounds are "placed" on different parts of the practitioner's body to sanctify them and transform the physical self into a divine vessel</term>

**Om Hram**, salutations to the heart.
**Om Hrim**, *svaha* <note>a ritual exclamation for offerings</note> to the head.
**Om Hrum**, *vashat* to the crown of the head.
**Om Hraim**, *hum* to the armor <note>symbolizing spiritual protection surrounding the body</note>.
**Om Hraum**, *vaushat* to the three eyes.
**Om Hrah**, *phat* to the weapon.

Thus ends the installation.

Having performed the installation in this way:

### The Meditation
<term>dhyana; a guided visualization of the deity's physical form used to focus the mind during worship</term>

He resembles a mountain of black collyrium <note>original: "anjana"; a black mineral used as eyeliner, here signifying Mahakala's deep black complexion</note>, possessing sharp fangs and a terrifying appearance. He is adorned with various ornaments and is well-clothed in a tiger skin. He bears a trident, an axe, a sword, and the supreme gesture of granting boons. With four arms and a fearsome sight,

one should bow one's head to Mahakala, who eternally grants all happiness. Having meditated thus and worshipped him within the mind, one should chant:

**The Root Mantra**
**Om Hram Hrim Hrum Hraim Hraum Hrah, salutations to Mahakala.**

One should chant this either 1,008 times or 108 times. After the chanting, having bowed down,

one should recite the excellent and auspicious hymn. For this "King of Hymns to Mahakala," the Seer <note>original: "rishi"; the enlightened sage who first "heard" or revealed the hymn</note> is the venerable Bhairava. The poetic meter is Gayatri. The deity is Mahakala-Bhairava. *Hrim* is the seed syllable <note>original: "bija"</note>. *Hrum* is the power. *Krim* is the pin <note>original: "kilaka"; a metaphorical key that unlocks the mantra's power</note>. The ritual application <term>viniyoga; the formal statement of the purpose for reciting the text</term> is for the destruction of all my obstacles.

**Bhairava said:**

O Goddess, O Goddess, O Great Goddess! Listen to the secret that is very difficult to obtain: the King of Hymns to Mahakala, famous throughout the three worlds and auspicious. By reciting this, people are liberated from all fears, as well as poverty, fear itself, and those dangers arising from enemies. Disease, kings <note>representing legal or state-sanctioned oppression</note>, thieves, female spirits <note>original: "dakini"</note>,

ghosts, and hidden spirits <note>original: "preta" and "guhyaka"</note>—all these are pacified by the singing of this King of Hymns. This is more secret than the most secret, O Goddess, and very rare in the three worlds. Chanting is in vain, O Great Goddess, without reciting this King of Hymns. Therefore, with all effort, one should recite the hymn, O great-minded one.

I shall proclaim the King of Hymns; listen with a focused mind, O Goddess:

**Om, salutations to the Blessed Lord Mahakala, the Cause of Destruction.**
Salutations to the one with a terrifying face, who wears a garland of skulls.
Salutations to the one who rides a terrifying ghost.
Salutations to the one holding a skull-cup and a staff topped with a skull <note>original: "khatvanga"</note> in his hands.
Salutations to the one residing in the center of the navel.

<summary>This page outlines the preliminary rituals for the worship of Mahakala, including the "Six-Limb Installation" (Nyasa) and a meditation (Dhyana) on his fearsome form. It introduces the Mahakala-Stavaraja (King of Hymns), a protective text said to dispel poverty, disease, and supernatural threats.</summary>

### Page 6

**Svadhā** <note>original: "svadhā"; a ritual exclamation used during offerings, here serving as a sacred opening to the section</note>. The wise know the **half-measure** <note>original: "ardha mātrā"; the subtle, trailing vibration of a vowel, particularly the nasal sound at the end of the syllable "OM," which is considered the point of transition into the divine</note> of the vowel to be composed of the void of space. Residing in its center is the Goddess, who is remembered as the **Supreme Power** <term>shakti; the active, creative energy of the divine</term>.

O Lord, she takes the form of a serpent, the **Sleeping Kundalini** <note>original: "supta-kuṇḍalinī"; the dormant spiritual energy coiled at the base of the spine</note>. Pervading the moving and unmoving universe, she is always awake and radiantly shining. 

The wise understand the **half-measure** vibration. That divinity resides within all worlds as the soul of every physical form. Just as

a person might light a lamp using another lamp, so too does the master of Yoga always meditate on the **Mantra** <term>mantra; a sacred sound or phrase used as a tool for spiritual transformation</term> through the power of the Mantra itself. One should know that to be eternal; the Supreme Goddess is remembered as the Eternal One. Within her resides the Goddess celebrated as **Kundali** <note>original: "kuṇḍalī"; the 'coiled one'</note>. Among all mantras, she is the very form of the mantra, the essence of all sacred sound. 

She is **Wisdom** <note>original: "vidyā"</note>, the **Great Wisdom** <term>Mahavidya; the ten personifications of the highest feminine knowledge in Tantra</term>, and is known as **Supreme Nature** <term>Prakriti; the primal matter or creative potential of the universe</term>. That Goddess, by the name of **Kundali**, is the essence of all mantras. 

She is entered by Kundali and she is surrounded by Kundali. She is released through Kundali and liberated by Kundali. That Goddess named Kundali is remembered as the physical manifestation of all mantras. Just as

the Lord might light a lamp from a lamp, so too is the mantra [realized] through the mantra, for the Goddess of the Mantra is the **Lord of the Clan** <note>original: "kuleshvara"; a title for Shiva as the lord of the spiritual lineage or the bodily 'clan' of senses and energies</note>. 

The Supreme Goddess is **Kuleshvari** <note>the feminine counterpart to Kuleshvara</note>, celebrated as Kundali. Among mantras, she is the form of the mantra and the essence of the mantra. That Goddess named Kundali is the form of all physical manifestations. 

Just as the waking state exists for those who are awake, so does sleep exist for those who are sleeping. In the same way, she is the liberated state for the liberated; she is the physical embodiment of the mantras. She is Wisdom, she is Great Wisdom, and she is the soul of the mantra-body. So is she, the Goddess who is the essential form of all mantras.

As the waking state is fashioned for the waking, so is sleep fashioned. The physical form of the mantras is remembered; she is the soul of the embodiment of mantras.

### Thus ends the one hundred and fifth chapter of the "Greatness of the Region of the Supreme Being" <note>Purushottama Kshetra Mahatmya, referring to the sacred area of modern-day Puri</note> within the Original Brahma Great Purana, titled "The Increasing of the Lord's Penance and Meditative Absorption." || 105 ||

"Oh, what great merit and good fortune is mine that I see you, O Lord of Sages! Oh, I am blessed; my birth is successful because I have come to you. Oh, Hari <note>Vishnu</note> has shown grace to me, who has surrendered to him! Therefore, I shall anoint you; for the yearning <note>original: "kanduti," literally an itch or a longing</note> for Vishnu is within you."

**Markandeya said:**
"Do not indulge in useless delusion; I am not the one for you to anoint. I am known in the world as Markandeya, O best of the twice-born <note>term: dvija; a person of the upper three social classes who has undergone spiritual initiation</note>."

**Markandeya said:**
"If you are the imperishable Vishnu, the eternal and primeval one, then show your own divine self—that supreme body of yours from which the entire universe is born and in which it always resides. Show that to me, O All-pervading Lord; then I shall worship you."

**The Blessed Lord said:**
"Behold my incomparable form, divine and born of lordly Yoga <note>the divine power of creation and meditation</note>."

<meta>This page marks a transition from the narrative of Sage Markandeya in the previous chapter to a specific devotional hymn (stotra) known as a "Kavacha" or "Protective Armor." ->**Salutations to the blessed Ganesha. Om, salutations to the Great Time <note>original: "Mahakala," a form of Shiva representing the power of time and transformation</note>. Om, Great Time, Time, Time <meta>string hole</meta> Great Time (twice).**<-

Now I shall fully explain the secret that bestows spiritual perfection <term>siddhi; supernatural powers or spiritual attainment</term>.

**The One with Matted Hair <note>original: "Dhurjati," an epithet for Shiva</note> said:**

Listen, O Goddess! I shall proclaim the supreme secret, more hidden than all other secrets <meta>string hole</meta>. This is the Protective Armor <term>kavacha; literally "armor," a prayer meant to invoke a protective field around the practitioner</term> of the Great Time, which grants all spiritual perfections. || 1 ||

Just as it was spoken by the Lord of Nandi <note>Nandikesha, Shiva's primary attendant</note> in his form as Great Time, so I shall tell it to you. Listen with a focused mind, my dear. || 2 ||

For this Protective Armor of the Blessed Great <meta>string hole</meta> Time, the Sage is Mahakala. The poetic meter is Anushtubh. The deity is the Blessed Great Time. *Hrim* is the seed-syllable <note>bija; a foundational sound-syllable used in tantric practice</note>. *Shrim* is the power. The ritual application <term>viniyoga; the intended purpose of the prayer</term> is for the pacification of all my misfortunes through the recitation of this text.

**Now, the Meditation Image <note>original: "Dhyanam"; a visualization used to focus the mind before chanting</note>:**

I meditate on the All-Pervading Lord, who resembles dark blue eye-salve <note>original: "nilanjana"</note> and shines with the radiance of the world-destroying poison <meta>string hole</meta> <note>original: "kalakuta," the poison consumed by Shiva to save the world</note>. He has three eyes and two arms; he is peaceful, yet adorned with a garland of skulls. || 3 ||

Carrying a divine sword and shield, wearing a tiger skin as his upper garment, I joyfully worship the God Great Time <note>Mahakala</note>, who dwells in the cremation grounds. || 4 ||

Om, may Great <meta>string hole</meta> Time protect my head. May the Great Lord protect my face. May the Creator of the Universe protect my throat. May the Terrible One of Time <note>original: "Kalabhairava"</note> protect my heart. || 5 ||

May Great Time protect my navel. May the Great Lord protect my private parts. May the Lord of the World protect my thighs. May the Terrible One of Time protect my knees. || 6 ||

May Great <meta>string hole</meta> Time protect my calves. May the Great Lord protect my feet. May the Lord of Gods protect my entire body. Salutations to you, O Great Time! || 7 ||

**Thus ends the Protective Armor of the Blessed Great Time.**

<margin>Chapter 10</margin>
...together with their own respective forces, such as the Kalingas <note>The Kalingas were a people from the east coast of ancient India</note> and others. Through the power of the perfected ones <term>Siddhas; semi-divine beings who have attained spiritual perfection</term>, that region in the south was then most beautiful. [The inhabitants were] adorned with divine garments, fragrant divine flowers, and celestial perfumes; they were beautified by various wonderful golden ornaments. At the side of that place, the Ganges flows, pervaded by fragrance. Having bathed there in the stainless waters of the Mandakini <note>original: "Mandakini"; the celestial name for the river Ganges as it flows through the heavens or high mountains</note>—on the auspicious northern bank of the Ganges, a sacred and beautiful place was established, where deer wander freely. 

In the center of that region, a site was fashioned by Brahma <term>The creator god in the Hindu trinity</term>. In the eastern part of that place resides the god Shambhu <term>A name for Shiva, meaning "Source of Happiness"</term>. Having bathed there, O Sage among Brahmins <term>Viprarshe; a title for a priestly sage, indicating this text is a recorded dialogue</term>, the Brahmin remains established in purity. 

Having performed the bath according to the proper rites at the most holy "Ganga-Dvara" <note>original: "Ganga-dvara"; literally "The Gateway of the Ganges," the ancient name for modern-day Haridwar</note>, he goes to the supreme abode where the god Maheshvara <term>The Great Lord Shiva</term> dwells. Having bathed there, O Sage among Brahmins, one is no longer affected by the dust of worldly existence. Having bathed in this preeminent pilgrimage site <term>Tirtha; literally a "crossing place," a sacred site where the boundary between the human and divine is thin</term>, which was well-praised by Brahma himself—where the Creator, the Lord Rudra <term>The fierce, ancient form of Shiva</term>, Vishnu, and Maheshvara all reside—having bathed there, O Sage among Brahmins, one is liberated from all sins. 

In that place, charity should always be performed by the wise, O Sage among Brahmins. Whatever is given as a gift, offered into the sacred fire, chanted in prayer, or performed as penance by the great seers in that place—it all becomes inexhaustible <note>original: "akshaya"; a spiritual concept where the merit earned from an action never diminishes over time</note>, as ordained by Brahma. Having bathed there, O Sage among Brahmins...

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>Note on continuity: The previous leaf appeared to be an introduction to a grammatical text (Shabdarnava). However, this leaf returns to the Shivagita, specifically the conclusion of the sixteenth chapter. This suggests the manuscript may be a collection of various texts (a miscellany) or that the previous leaf was misidentified or out of sequence. This page focuses on the Advaita (non-dualist) philosophy of identifying the individual soul with Lord Shiva.</meta>

Moreover, thinking "How might our welfare be attained?", and having praised objects like the wish-fulfilling jewel <note>original: "Cintamani," a mythological gem capable of granting any desire</note> and others, one who has sought refuge in the Lord who appears like the Rudra of the great dissolution <note>original: "Pralayarudra," the form of Shiva that destroys the universe at the end of an eon</note> and has attained union with Shiva <note>original: "Shiva-sayujya"</note>, he is indeed Shiva himself. || 1 ||

One who, by the power of the manifestation of the light of pure consciousness <note>original: "samvit-pratha"</note>, having burnt away worldly attachments, finds your two lotus feet within his own heart—how can he, possessed of pure intellect, not become Shiva himself? || 2 ||

Although I have approached all branches of knowledge, please grant this instruction for our sake. || 3 ||

But Lord <note>original: "Bhagavan"</note>, the Lord of Uma <note>original: "Umapati," a name for Shiva referring to his consort Parvati</note> alone is all-knowing, all-powerful, the creator of all, all-pervading, and worthy of worship; how can He be of the same form as me? I, however, am limited, of little knowledge, of little power, not a lord, and always agitated by suffering. Why then is it said, "I am Brahman" <note>original: "Brahmāhamasmi," a famous Upanishadic declaration of the identity between the individual and the Absolute</note>?

What you have said is true; in the state of worldly existence <note>original: "samsara"</note>, it appears exactly so, but in the ultimate truth <note>original: "paramarthatah"</note>, that limitation does not exist at all. For instance: ignorance <note>original: "Ajnana"</note> is a certain effect of latent mental impressions, beginningless and indescribable; by its power, all this world appears. When it is removed, nothing of the world remains. It is like a flower in the sky or a city of celestial musicians <note>original: "kha-pushpa" and "gandharva-nagara," standard Indian philosophical metaphors for illusions or things that seem real but lack physical substance</note>. And just as a rope is mistaken for a snake—where the snake is negated once the rope is known—similarly, by the knowledge of the Self <note>original: "Atman"</note>, ignorance and its effects are negated.

<column-break/>

"Truly, there is no object other than myself; all this that is perceived is that Self alone. For me, who am eternally pure and liberated, for what object could there be a desire? Therefore, I am indeed Shiva." || 4 ||

Thinking thus, one attains the perfection of non-action <note>original: "naishkarmya-siddhi," the state where one is no longer bound by the results of their deeds</note>. But how can this beginningless ignorance be destroyed? Does anything change when ignorance is burnt by the fire of knowledge <note>original: "vidya"</note>? True. Just as a burnt cloth cannot function as a cloth <note>though it keeps its shape, it has no strength</note>, so also "burnt" ignorance cannot produce the projection of a false world <note>original: "vikshepa"</note>. And so, when nescience <note>original: "avidya"</note> is removed by the power of knowledge, Brahman alone remains. Therefore, one should cultivate the realization, "I am indeed Brahman." 

### Thus ends the sixteenth chapter of the Shivagita. || 16 ||

->May there be prosperity! May there be well-being! May there be auspiciousness!<-
->Rama! Rama! Rama!<-
